How do field theories detect the torsion in topological modular forms?
Daniel Berwick-Evans

Abstract
. We construct deformation invariants of 2 | 1 2|1 -dimensional Euclidean field theories valued in a cohomology theory approximating topological modular forms. This implies several results anticipated by Stolz and Teichner and gives the first torsion invariants of field theories valued in π ∗ ​ TMF \pi_{*}{\rm TMF} . The framework leads to a version of the elliptic Euler class described in terms of field-theoretic data, as well as a partial construction of the supersymmetric sigma model with target a string manifold.



1.
Introduction and statement of results

Stolz and Teichner describe categories of
2|1
-dimensional Euclidean field theories over a smooth manifold
M
indexed by a degree
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. They conjecture that such field theories determine geometric cocycles for topological modular forms
[
ST11
, Conjecture 1.17]

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}{\rm fully}\hbox{-}{\rm extended}\ 2|1\hbox{-}{\rm Euclidean}\\
{\rm field\ theories\ of\ degree}\ n\ {\rm over}\ M\end{array}\right\}
{\rm TMF}^{n}(M).
cocycle

It is further expected that this cocycle model inherits an analytic pushforward from quantization of field theories. The ultimate goal is to prove a TMF-generalization of the Atiyah–Singer index theorem.
In this paper we formulate and prove a 1-extended approximation to (
1
) via categories
2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
that encode the values of degree
n
field theories over
M
on (Ramond) supercylinders and supertori. Our definition also imposes a Wilsonian-style ellipticity condition on objects of
2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
, see Remark
1.15
. On the homotopical side,
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
is a height
\leq 1
approximation of
{\rm TMF}
with a map of ring spectra
{\rm TMF}\to{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
, see (
18
).

Theorem 1.1
.

Partially-defined
2|1
-Euclidean field theories determine geometric cocycles

(4)

\displaystyle 2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)\xrightarrow{{\rm cocycle}}{\rm KO}^{n}_{\rm MF}(M).

The Ando–Hopkins–Rezk
\sigma
-orientation of TMF
[
AHR10
]
provides a map of ring spectra

(5)

\displaystyle{\rm MString}\xrightarrow{\sigma}{\rm TMF}\to{\rm KO}_{\rm MF},

implying that
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
has Euler classes for vector bundles with string structures. The following approximates Stolz and Teichner’s proposed elliptic Euler class
[
ST04
, Theorem 1.0.3]
.

Theorem 1.2
.

A real vector bundle
V\to M
with geometric string structure determines an object
{\rm Eu}(V)\in 2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{{\rm dim}(V)}(M)
whose image under (
4
) is the
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
-Euler class of
V
.

Hopkins–Mahowald proved that the string orientation detects all torsion in
\pi_{*}{\rm TMF}

[
Hop02
, Theorem 6.25]
, and
Bunke–Naumann
[
BN14
, §1.4]
show that the 3-torsion in degrees
4k-1
injects along
\pi_{*}{\rm TMF}\to\pi_{*}{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
and that
{\mathbb{Z}}/24\simeq\pi_{3}{\rm TMF}\hookrightarrow\pi_{3}{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
injects.
The following approximates the anticipated
\pi_{*}{\rm TMF}
-valued analytic index while also giving the first
{\rm TMF}
-torsion detected by structures in supersymmetric Euclidean field theories.

Theorem 1.3
.

For an
n
-dimensional string manifold
X
, there is an object
\sigma(X)\in 2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{-n}({\rm pt})
that is a cocycle refinement (using (
4
)) of the class in the image of

[X]\in\pi_{n}({\rm MString})\to\pi_{n}{\rm TMF}\to\pi_{n}{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}={\rm KO}^{-n}_{\rm MF}({\rm pt}).

In particular,
S^{3}
with string structure specified by
1\in{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq{\rm H}^{3}(S^{3};{\mathbb{Z}})
determines a cocycle
\sigma(S^{3})\in 2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{-3}({\rm pt})
representing the generator
\nu
of
{\mathbb{Z}}/24\simeq\pi_{3}({\rm TMF})\hookrightarrow\pi_{3}({\rm KO}_{\rm MF})
.

1.1.
Context

In the late 1980s, Witten proposed a surprising connection between elliptic cohomology and quantum field theory
[
Wit87
,
Wit88a
]
. One focal point of his work is a cobordism invariant of string manifolds valued in integral modular forms

\displaystyle\left\{\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}{\rm String\ manifolds}\\
{\rm up\ to\ cobordism}\end{array}\!\!\right\}\to{\rm MF}^{\mathbb{Z}}

now called the
Witten genus
. In physical language, Witten argued that partition functions of quantum field theories with
\mathcal{N}=(0,1)
supersymmetry determine integral modular forms. The invariant (
1.1
) then arises from the partition function of the
\mathcal{N}=(0,1)
supersymmetric sigma model with target a string manifold. Segal
[
Seg88
,
Seg04
]
and subsequently Stolz and Teichner
[
ST04
,
ST11
]
provided mathematical frameworks in which aspects of Witten’s physical argument could be made precise, leading to the conjecture (
1
).

In parallel to these developments, Hopkins and collaborators clarified the homotopy-theoretic origins of the Witten genus
[
Hop94
,
AHS01
,
Hop02
,
Lur09
,
AHR10
]
. This culminated in the universal elliptic cohomology theory of topological modular forms
({\rm TMF})
and its string orientation,

(9)

{\rm MString}
{\rm TMF},
\pi_{*}{\rm MString}
\pi_{*}{\rm TMF}
{\rm MF}^{\mathbb{Z}}
.
\sigma
\pi_{*}\sigma
\phi
(
1.1
)

The map
\sigma
refines the Witten genus as indicated on the right above, using
the canonical (edge) homomorphism
\phi\colon\pi_{*}{\rm TMF}\to{\rm MF}^{\mathbb{Z}}
. The map
\phi
is a rational isomorphism, with kernel and cokernel nontrivial 2- and 3-torsion groups. It is natural to ask if Witten’s bridge between quantum field theory and modular forms extends to a link with topological modular forms. This question has proven difficult: a description of
{\rm TMF}
-torsion in the language of quantum field theory has eluded understanding for decades.

Part of the difficultly comes from our meager physical understanding of
\mathcal{N}=(0,1)
-theories. With enough supersymmetry, a quantum field theory becomes so constrained that its path integral is effectively controlled by algebra rather than analysis. This often leads to exact computations grounded in rigorous mathematics. For example, 2-dimensional theories with
\mathcal{N}=(2,2)
supersymmetry admit the
A
- and
B
-twists from mirror symmetry; the correlation functions in these theories can be evaluated using symplectic geometry and algebraic geometry, respectively
[
Wit88b
]
. By comparison,
\mathcal{N}=(0,1)
is the minimal supersymmetry in dimension 2, and so it imposes relatively few constraints on the path integral. Correspondingly few quantities have been computed by physicists. Due to this more challenging landscape,
\mathcal{N}=(0,1)
-theories have received comparatively little attention. And yet, minimally supersymmetric theories are in some sense the most fundamental.

Recent work in physics has used (
1
) to predict when a pair of
\mathcal{N}=(0,1)
-theories lie in the same deformation class
[
GJF18
,
JF20
,
Tac21
,
TY21
,
GPPV21
,
LP22
,
Lin22
]
. One can then ask for physically meaningful invariants witnessing these predictions.
Gaiotto, Johnson-Freyd and Witten
[
GJFW21
]
consider the
\mathcal{N}=(0,1)
-supersymmetric sigma model with target
S^{3}
. This is a supersymmetric Wess–Zumino–Witten model for the group
S^{3}\simeq{\rm SU}(2)
depending on a level
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq{\rm H}^{4}(B{\rm SU}(2);{\mathbb{Z}})
. Gaiotto–Johnson-Freyd–Witten argue that
k
mod 24 is a deformation invariant of the theory. In light of the isomorphism
\pi_{3}({\rm TMF})\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}/24
, this matches with the prediction from conjecture (
1
) and the
\pi_{*}{\rm TMF}
-valued refinement (
9
) of the Witten genus. Subsequent work of Gaiotto and Johnson-Freyd generalizes this invariant to
\mathcal{N}=(0,1)
supersymmetric sigma models with target a
(4k-1)
-dimensional string manifold, where the level is replaced by more general anomaly cancelation data
[
GJF19
]
.
Theorems
1.1
and
1.3
make the Gaiotto–Johnson-Freyd–Witten invariant precise and identify its values with
{\rm TMF}
-torsion in the image of
\pi_{4k-1}{\rm TMF}\to\pi_{4k-1}{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
. This gives a partial answer to the title question of this paper.

1.2.
Relation to prior work

Theorem
1.1
implies several results anticipated by Stolz and Teichner. Specifically,
[
ST04
, Theorem 1.0.2]
describes a map (contingent on
[
ST04
, Hypothesis 3.3.13]
)

\displaystyle\left\{\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}2|1\hbox{-}{\rm Euclidean\ field\ theories}\\
{\rm of\ degree}\ n\ {\rm over}\ M\end{array}\!\!\!\right\}\longrightarrow{\rm KO}^{n}(M)(\!(q)\!)

as well as a modularity property for the image of (
1.2
) when
M={\rm pt}
. A result closely related to (
1.2
) was proved by Pokman Cheung
[
Che07
]
, and a special case of the modularity property is stated as
[
ST11
, Theorem 1.15]
where a proof is outlined. The map of ring spectra

{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}\to{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!),

allows one to deduce a version of (
1.2
) from Theorem
1.1
. Furthermore, the modularity encoded by
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
reduces to Stolz and Teichner’s claimed modularity for (
1.2
) when
M={\rm pt}
. Building on (
1.2
), Stolz and Teichner describe a field theory in
[
ST04
, Theorem 1.0.3]
that is expected to provide a cocycle representative of the
{\rm TMF}
-Euler class of a string vector bundle under (
1
). In some sense, this object is the heart of the Stolz–Teichner program, providing a description of the string orientation of
{\rm TMF}
in terms of 2-dimensional supersymmetric field theories. Theorem
1.2
provides a partial construction and verifies that
{\rm Eu}(V)\in 2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
is a cocycle representative of the
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
-Euler class, and hence approximates the
{\rm TMF}
-Euler class. This realizes a 1-categorical version of the Stolz–Teichner program; see also Remark
9.12
. We caution that the precise definition of field theory differs in all three of
[
ST04
]
,
[
Che07
]
and
[
ST11
]
, although the spirit of the definitions are quite similar. The definitions in this paper are closely related to the ones in
[
ST11
]
, see Proposition
1.22
.

Our results also shed some light on Bunke and Naumann’s torsion invariants of
(4k-1)
-dimensional string manifolds
[
BN14
]

(13)

\displaystyle\pi_{4k-1}{\rm MString}\to{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)/({\mathbb{Z}}(\!(q)\!)+{\rm MF}_{2k}).

In Theorem
1.6
and Corollary
1.7
, we show that the composition of maps of spectra (
5
) determines the invariant (
13
) by passing to coefficients.
This description implies the conjecture
[
BN14
, §1.5, Open problem 3]
, see Corollary
1.8
. Theorem
1.3
then identifies the Bunke–Naumann invariant with the Gaiotto–Johnson-Freyd–Witten invariant, completing the argument outlined in
[
GJF19
, §2.5]
; see Remark
1.23
.

Theorems
1.1
-
1.3
forge new connections between algebraic topology and Dirac–Ramond operators in the physics literature.
In particular, Alvarez and Windey
[
AW10
]
study families of Dirac–Ramond operators
[
Wit88a
]
associated to a bundle of string manifolds
X\to M
, extracting modular invariants at the level of cohomology of
M
with coefficients over
{\mathbb{C}}
. A natural extension of their construction involves the
Bismut–Ramond superconnection
, which gives both integral and torsion refinements of Alvarez and Windey’s invariants; see §
3.3
. The Bismut–Ramond superconnection is the analytical input to the construction of the objects
\sigma(X)\in 2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{-n}({\rm pt})
in Theorem
1.3
.

The geometry behind Theorems
1.1
-
1.3
expand upon a verification of the conjecture (
1
) with
{\mathbb{C}}
coefficients in
[
BE21
,
BE22
]
, i.e., a height 0 confirmation of the Stolz–Teichner program. These previous results show that the value of a
2|1
-Euclidean field theory on supertori in
M
produces a class in
{\rm H}(M;{\rm MF}^{\omega})
, ordinary cohomology valued in weak modular forms; see Proposition
1.18
. The present paper extends this by also including the data of a
2|1
-Euclidean field theory on supercylinders in
M
. The trace of the values on supercylinders recovers the values on supertori, so that Theorems
1.1
-
1.3
directly generalize the results from
[
BE21
,
BE22
]
.

A crucial new ingredient developed in this paper and the companion
[
BE23b
]
is an ellipticity-type condition for field theories, namely that energy cutoffs vary smoothly and admit a type of Chern–Simons form, see Definition
1.14
. This Wilsonian approach to mathematical quantum field theory is influenced by Costello’s work
[
Cos11a
,
Cos11b
,
Cos11c
]
. In our framework, the existence of a cutoff is a
property
of a Stolz–Teichner field theory, not additional data; see Remark
1.15
.

An enduring challenge in modern mathematical physics is the search for a precise and complete definition of quantum field theory. In the early days of the subject, there were no definitions at all; now mathematicians are spoiled for choice, making it unclear which is the “correct” definition. Each mathematical framework has its own strengths and insights, e.g.,
[
Seg04
,
ST04
,
GMS04
,
HK04
,
Che07
,
HST10
,
Cos11a
,
Cos11c
,
ST11
,
DH11
,
Kit14
,
GP20
]
.
Conjecture (
1
) is an excellent proving ground, as it requires enough sophistication to identify structures in quantum field theory that encode an intricate object like
{\rm TMF}
. With these shifting landscapes in mind, the cocycle map (
4
) is designed to be as robust as possible to changes in the mathematical definition of field theory: an object of
2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
is the data attached to certain explicit moduli spaces of
2|1
-Euclidean supermanifolds, and such data is unambiguously part of the geometry of a
\mathcal{N}=(0,1)
-theory.
Restricting the data of a fully-defined theory to its values on these specific moduli spaces gives a diagram

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}{\rm fully}\hbox{-}{\rm extended}\\
2|1\hbox{-}{\rm Euclidean\ field\ theories}\\
{\rm of\ degree}\ n\ {\rm over}\ M\end{array}\right\}
{\rm TMF}^{n}(M)
2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
{\rm KO}^{n}_{\rm MF}(M).
conjecture (
1
)
restriction
Theorem
1.1

Theorem
1.1
therefore helps tame the zoo of possible definitions of field theory in (
1
): extending the partially-defined field theories in
2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
to fully-extended ones must lift a class in
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
to one in
{\rm TMF}
. Physicists have recently compiled lists of the
\mathcal{N}=(0,1)
-theories expected to represent specific
{\rm TMF}
-classes, e.g., see
[
GPPV21
, Table 2]
. This provides explicit cases where one can study lifting problems for
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
-classes to
{\rm TMF}
-classes and compare with extending down in a given quantum field theory.

Remark 1.4
.

Stolz and Teichner’s
[
ST11
, Conjecture 1.17]
states that a cocycle map (
1
) induces a bijection between concordance classes of field theories and
{\rm TMF}
classes.
It is easy to see that the map (
4
) factors through concordance classes, but we do not expect a bijection for the definitions used in this paper. The basic problem is that the dimensions of the vector bundles
{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
(see (
28
) below) are concordance invariants in our framework, but that are not invariants of the underlying class in
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
. This issue is already present when considering models for
{\rm K}
-theory in terms of superconnections on vector bundles. We know of two plausible approaches that could lead to the desired bijection on concordance classes. The first fixes an infinite dimensional separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, and considers subspaces
{\mathcal{E}}\subset M\times\mathcal{H}
where
{\mathcal{E}}
is no longer required to be locally trivial, but the family of projection operators determined by
{\mathcal{E}}
is required to vary continuously with
M
. This generalizes the definition of a rigged Hilbert space. A second option considers families of field valued in sheaves of topological vector spaces with no local freeness condition; see
[
ST11
, Remark 3.16]
. A sheafy generalization of Theorem
1.1
would require one to enhance the tools from index theory to apply in this more general setting, e.g., adapt
[
BGV92
, Ch. 9-10]
to consider superconnections and index bundles supported on general sheaves of topological vector spaces. While this seems quite interesting, it is tangential to the goals of the present paper: our examples of interest (namely, the
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
-Euler class and the supersymmetric
\sigma
-model) are defined on vector bundles, and so for simplicity we work with this class of field theories.

1.3.
Detailed overview of results

In the remainder of this introduction we describe the objects in Theorems
1.1
-
1.3
and outline proofs, highlighting some of the key intermediary results about
2|1
-Euclidean field theories and
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
.

1.3.1.
A
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
-index theorem

Let
{\rm MF}
denote the graded ring of weakly holomorphic modular forms (see §
A.1
for our conventions) and
{\rm H}_{{\rm MF}}
the commutative ring spectrum representing ordinary cohomology with coefficients in
{\rm MF}
. Similarly, let
{\rm H}_{{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}]}
denote the spectrum representing ordinary cohomology with coefficients in
{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}]
where
|u^{2}|=-4
. Define
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
as the pullback in commutative ring spectra,

(18)

{\rm TMF}
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!)
{\rm H}_{\rm MF}
{\rm H}_{{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}]}

\lrcorner

q
-expand
Ch
Miller

where
{\rm Ch}
is the Pontryagin character, and the lower horizontal arrow is determined by
q
-expansion of coefficients. By the universal property of the pullback, there is a map
{\rm TMF}\to{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
determined by smashing the Miller character with
{\rm H}_{\mathbb{C}}

[
Mil89
]
. The Miller character comes from evaluation of
{\rm TMF}
at the Tate curve, which essentially extracts the height 1 part of
{\rm TMF}
. Similarly, the Chern–Dold character
{\rm TMF}\to{\rm TMF}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\simeq{\rm H}_{\rm MF}
extracts the height 0 part. In this way,
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
is a height
\leq 1
approximation to
{\rm TMF}
.

Bismut’s families index theorem
[
Bis86
]
together with the analytic description of the
{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!)
-valued Witten genus from
[
Wit88a
]
leads to the following, proved in §
3.5
.

Proposition 1.5
.

Given a family of Riemannian string manifolds
\pi\colon X\to M
, the analytic index of the Bismut–Ramond superconnection determines a class
\sigma(X)\in{\rm KO}^{-n}_{\rm MF}(M)
.

The string orientation (
5
) also determines a topological index: for a family
\pi\colon X\to M
of string manifolds with fiber dimension
n
, consider the composition

(19)

\displaystyle[X]\in{\rm MString}^{-n}(M)\xrightarrow{\sigma}{\rm TMF}^{-n}(M)\to{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}^{-n}(M).

A characterization of
[{\rm MString},{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}]
in terms of orientations of
{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!)
and
{\rm H}_{\rm MF}
leads to the following index theorem for
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
, proved in §
3.6
.

Theorem 1.6
.

The topological index (
19
) equals the analytic index in Proposition
1.5
.

This index theorem recovers a previously-studied invariant of Bunke and Naumann and proves a related conjecture, as we now explain. When
M={\rm pt}
, Proposition
1.5
determines analytically-constructed invariants of string manifolds,

\displaystyle\left\{\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}{\rm String\ manifolds}\\
{\rm up\ to\ cobordism}\end{array}\!\!\right\}\simeq\pi_{*}{\rm MString}\to\pi_{*}{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}.

The homotopy groups of
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
are computed in Proposition
3.3
, and in particular

\pi_{4k}{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}\simeq{\rm MF}_{2k}^{\mathbb{Z}},\qquad\pi_{4k-1}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)/({\mathbb{Z}}(\!(q)\!)+{\rm MF}_{2k})

recovering integral modular forms in degrees
4k
and the target of the Bunke–Naumann invariants (
13
) in degrees
4k-1
.

Corollary 1.7
.

In degree
*=4k
, (
1.3.1
) agrees with the Witten genus as an integral modular form.
In degrees
4k-1
, it agrees with the Bunke–Naumann torsion invariants of string manifolds
[
BN14
]
.

The following verifies
[
BN14
, §1.5, Open problem 3]
.

Corollary 1.8
.

The Bunke–Naumann torsion invariant of
(4k-1)
-dimensional string manifolds is determined by the topological pushforward (

).

Proof.

This follows directly from Theorem
1.6
and Corollary
1.7
.
∎

1.3.2.
Stolz and Teichner’s
2|1
-Euclidean field theories

The categories
2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
in Theorem
1.1
come from distilling the geometric information of a
2|1
-Euclidean field theory of degree
n
in the framework developed by Stolz and Teichner in
[
ST11
]
.
We briefly review their setup below.

The definition of degree
n
field theory in conjecture (
1
) starts with a category of
2|1
-Euclidean bordisms over a smooth manifold
M
. A 1-extended version of this bordism category, denoted
2|1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}(M)
, is constructed in
[
ST11
]
. Roughly, a morphism in
2|1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}(M)
is

(23)

Y_{\sf out}^{1|1}
Y_{\sf in}^{1|1}
\Sigma^{2|1}
M
\Phi

for a
2|1
-Euclidean manifold
\Sigma^{2|1}
, a map
\Phi\colon\Sigma^{2|1}\to M
, and
1|1
-dimensional sub supermanifolds
Y_{{\sf in}},Y_{{\sf out}}\hookrightarrow\Sigma^{2|1}
that determine the source and target of the bordism. The precise definition of
2|1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}(M)
requires one consider families of bordisms and their isometries (as a symmetric monoidal stack), but we ignore this for now; see §
C.4
for details.

A
degree
n
field theory over
M
is a natural transformation

(24)

2|1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}(M)
{\sf{TA}}
E\Downarrow
\mathbbold{1}
{\rm Fer}_{n}

where
{\sf{TA}}
is the Morita category of (topological) algebras, bimodules, and bimodule maps. The functor
\mathbbold{1}
is the constant functor to the monoidal unit of
{\sf{TA}}
, and

(25)

\displaystyle{\rm Fer}_{n}\colon 2|1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}(M)\to{\sf{TA}},\qquad{\rm Fer}_{n}\otimes{\rm Fer}_{m}\simeq{\rm Fer}_{n+m}

is a
\otimes
-invertible functor equipped with the indicated isomorphisms for
n,m\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. A complete construction of
{\rm Fer}_{n}
has yet to appear, but it is expected that (
25
) is the unique supersymmetric extension of the
n
th tensor power of the chiral free fermion as constructed in
[
ST11
, §5-6]
.
Hypothesis
7.10
gives a precise formulation of this extension, and we assume this hypothesis below.

Remark 1.9
.

We emphasize that Theorems
1.1
-
1.3
do not depend on Hypothesis
7.10
, but rather it allows us to compare our results with
[
ST11
]
. In §
7.3
we prove the existence of a unique supersymmetric extension of
{\rm Fer}_{n}
for the class of
2|1
-Euclidean bordisms relevant in this paper, partially verifying Hypothesis
7.10
.

It turns out to be important to demand an additional structure on degree
n
field theories (
24
) called
reflection positivity
; this structure is implicit in the “adjunction transformations” that were part of the definition of
2|1
-dimensional field theory in
[
ST04
]
. Below we follow the approach of Freed–Hopkins
[
FH22
, §3]
: a
reflection structure
is
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-equivariance data for a natural transformation (
24
) (Definition
C.31
), and the
positivity property
is that a certain hermitian pairing is positive (Definition
7.25
).

Definition 1.10

(Definition
7.27
)
.

Let
2|1\hbox{-{\sf EFT}}^{n}(M)
denote the groupoid whose objects are reflection positive natural transformations (
24
).

Remark 1.11
.

We note that reflection positivity is unnecessary in the
d=1
version of conjecture (
1
): unoriented, real
1|1
-dimensional field theories recover real K-theory
[
HST10
]
.
1
1

1

In
[
HST10
]
, a positivity condition is imposed that is distinct from reflection positivity; it is unclear whether this condition is necessary to recover
{\rm KO}
-theory.
However, unoriented and real structures do not generalize to the
2|1
-dimensional setting: chiral supersymmetry is not preserved under orientation reversal, nor is it preserved by complex conjugation. The Miller character
{\rm TMF}\to{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!)
necessitates a map from
2|1
-Euclidean field theories to real K-theory if conjecture (
1
) is to hold. One mechanism (and indeed, the only one we know) to obtain real structures on Hilbert spaces of states in dimension 2 assumes reflection positivity, e.g., see
[
GPPV21
, 3.2.2]
.

1.3.3.
The categories
2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
and
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
-cocycles

The cocycle map in Theorem
1.1
comes from restricting (
24
) to certain explicit subcategories of
2|1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}(M)
,

(26)

\mathscr{C}^{2|1}(M)
2|1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}(M)
{\sf{TA}}.
E\Downarrow
trace
supercylinders
\mathbbold{1}
{\rm Fer}_{n}

(27)

\mathcal{L}_{0}^{2|1}(M)
supertori

The restriction of
E
along (
26
) affords a twisted representation of the category
\mathscr{C}^{2|1}(M)
of nearly constant supercylinders in
M
, viewed as bordisms between supercircles in
M
.
The restriction of
E
along (
27
) determines a line bundle with section over the stack
\mathcal{L}^{2|1}_{0}(M)
of nearly constant supertori in
M
, viewed as bordisms from the empty set to itself. The images of
\mathscr{C}^{2|1}(M)
and
\mathcal{L}^{2|1}_{0}(M)
in
2|1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}(M)
are compatible via a (categorical) trace, indicated by the dashed arrow. The moduli spaces
\mathcal{L}^{2|1}_{0}(M)
and
\mathscr{C}^{2|1}(M)
are finite-dimensional, and so the restrictions (
26
) and (
27
) extract data from a field theory that can be analyzed using standard techniques in super geometry.

Remark 1.12
.

The Euclidean supercylinders and supertori in subcategories
\mathscr{C}^{2|1}(M)
and
\mathcal{L}^{2|1}_{0}(M)
have nonbounding (i.e., odd) spin structures. In physical language, the restrictions (
26
) and (
27
) therefore only probe the Ramond sector of a
2|1
-Euclidean field theory, ignoring the Neveu–Schwarz sector. The Ramond sector is the part of the
\mathcal{N}=(0,1)
-supersymmetric sigma model relevant to Witten’s construction of the Witten genus
[
Wit88a
]
.

Restriction along (
26
) extracts familiar objects from index theory.

Proposition 1.13

(Propositions
8.2
,
8.16
,
8.17
)
.

The restriction (
26
) determines:

i.

a Fréchet vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}\to M
with fiberwise
S^{1}\times{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{n}
-action, where the eigenvalues of the
S^{1}
-action are bounded below;

ii.

a smooth, 1-parameter family of superconnections
{\mathbb{A}}_{\ell}
,
\ell\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
on
{\mathcal{E}}
that are
S^{1}
-invariant and
{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{n}
-linear.

The restriction along (
26
) of a reflection positive degree
n
field theory further determines

iii.

a hermitian metric on
{\mathcal{E}}
for which the
S^{1}
-action is unitary, the
{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{n}
-action is self-adjoint and the superconnection
{\mathbb{A}}
is self-adjoint;

iv.

a real structure on
{\mathcal{E}}
compatible with the
S^{1}
-action and superconnection for which the Clifford action is real relative to the standard real structure on
{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{n}
.

The
S^{1}
-eigenspaces extract a sequence of vector bundles with superconnections (Lemma
9.1
)

(28)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}\simeq\bigoplus_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathcal{E}}_{k},\qquad{\mathbb{A}}_{\ell}=\bigoplus_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathbb{A}}_{\ell,k}

where
{\mathcal{E}}_{k}=\{0\}
for
k\ll 0
. We emphasize that the
{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
need not be finite rank. The geometric objects in Proposition
1.13
generalize standard data in
\mathcal{N}=(0,1)
supersymmetric quantum field theory (reviewed in §
2.4
) via the
M
-family of operators

(29)

\displaystyle L_{0}:=\bigoplus_{k}(k+\frac{\ell}{2\pi}{\mathbb{A}}_{\ell,k}^{2}),\qquad\bar{L}_{0}:=\bigoplus_{k}\frac{\ell}{2\pi}{\mathbb{A}}_{\ell,k}^{2},\qquad\bar{G}_{0}:=\bigoplus_{k}\frac{\sqrt{\ell}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}{\mathbb{A}}_{\ell,k}.

We observe that these operators satisfy the condition that
L_{0}-\bar{L}_{0}
has integer eigenvalues (and so generates an
S^{1}
-action),
[L_{0},\bar{L}_{0}]=0
, and
\bar{G}_{0}^{2}=\bar{L}_{0}
. Assuming existence and uniqueness of solutions to differential equations (see Remark
9.6
) the degree
n
representation of
\mathscr{C}^{2|1}(M)
is given by

(30)

\displaystyle q^{L_{0}}{\bar{q}}^{\bar{L}_{0}+\theta\sqrt{2\pi}\bar{G}_{0}},\qquad q=e^{2\pi iz/\ell},\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0},\ z/\ell\in{\mathfrak{H}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}

recovering the usual super semigroup formula when
M={\rm pt}
, e.g.,
[
Seg04
, §4]
,
[
ST04
, §3.3]
, and
[
Che07
, Eq. 2.1.17]
. Geometrically,
\ell
denotes the circumference of a supercircle, and
\tau=z/\ell
is the conformal modulus of a supercylinder; see §
5
.

When attempting to connect
2|1
-Euclidean field theories over
M
with the topology of
M
, Proposition
1.13
suggests that we extract a sequence of
{\rm KO}
-classes

(31)

\displaystyle{\rm Index}\colon 2|1\hbox{-{\sf EFT}}^{n}(M)\dashrightarrow{\rm KO}^{n}(M)(\!(q)\!),\qquad E\mapsto\sum q^{k}{\rm Ind}({\mathbb{A}}_{\ell,k})

by applying the families index construction (e.g., see
[
BGV92
, Ch. 10]
) to each
{\mathbb{A}}_{\ell,k}
in (
28
) extracted from a
2|1
-Euclidean field theory
E
. The validity of the families index construction is a
property
of a superconnection, and hence the index (
31
) is defined on the following subcategory of
2|1\hbox{-{\sf EFT}}^{n}(M)
.

Definition 1.14
.

A degree
n
representation of
\mathscr{C}^{2|1}(M)

admits cutoffs
if it is infinitesimally generated (see Definition
9.5
) and the superconnections (
28
) admit smooth index bundles in the sense of Definition
9.3
. A field theory
E\in 2|1\hbox{-{\sf EFT}}^{n}(M)

admits smooth cutoffs
if the the associated representation of
\mathscr{C}^{2|1}(M)
does. Let
2|1\hbox{-{\sf EFT}}^{n}_{{\rm co}}(M)\subset 2|1\hbox{-{\sf EFT}}^{n}(M)
denote the full subcategory whose objects admit cutoffs.

Remark 1.15
.

The above terminology comes from the fact that the existence of the families index is the same as the existence of a smooth energy cutoff theory in the sense of Wilsonian effective field theory, e.g., see
[
Cos11b
, §1.3]
for a mathematical exposition where the definition of field theory also involves an ellipticity condition
[
Cos11b
, Definition 9.3.1]
.
In our framework, the existence of cutoffs is an analytic condition on field theories analogous to the ellipticity property for differential operators. This analogy is tight for
1|1
-dimensional field theories where ellipticity for a family of Dirac operators implies the cutoff property for the associated object in
1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
, see
[
BE23b
]
. It bears noting that from the very beginning of this subject, Witten suggested mathematicians use cutoffs to analyze 2-dimensional theories with
\mathcal{N}=(0,1)
supersymmetry
[
Wit88a
, §1]
.

Remark 1.16
.

If the
S^{1}
-eigenspaces
{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
in (
28
) are finite-dimensional vector bundles, the cutoff property in Definition
1.14
is automatically satisfied using the existence and uniqueness of solutions to ordinary differential equations, along with the existence of the families index bundles in finite-dimensions, e.g.,
[
BGV92
, §9.1]
. In particular,
M
-families of vertex algebras with connection produce the operators (
29
) and satisfy the cutoff condition. There are many proposed constructions relating (families of) vertex algebras with elliptic cohomology classes, e.g., see
[
Liu96
,
GMS04
,
HK04
,
Cos11a
,
Che13
,
Kit14
,
Hen15
,
LP22
]
. The representation (
30
) of Euclidean supercylinders offers a point of contact between these vertex algebraic constructions and the Stolz–Teichner program.

Remark 1.17
.

When
M={\rm pt}
, the cutoff property simplifies considerably, essentially imposing a growth constraint on the eigenvalues of
{\mathbb{A}}_{\ell}^{2}
, see Remark
D.15
.

Now we shift attention to moduli spaces of supertori and the restriction of a degree
n
field theory along (
27
). From general considerations this restriction produces a line bundle with section over
\mathcal{L}_{0}^{2|1}(M)

[
ST11
, §5.3]
. The following generalizes
[
BE22
, Theorem 1.1]
from a statement in degree
n=0
to arbitrary degree. The vector spaces
{\rm mf}^{k,l}
are
C^{\infty}
-versions of weak modular forms, viewed as a function on based, oriented lattices in
{\mathbb{C}}\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
that transform with weight
(-k/2,-l/2)
, see (
459
).

Proposition 1.18
.

Sections of the
n^{\rm th}
tensor power of the Pfaffian line are given by data

\displaystyle\Gamma(\mathcal{L}^{2|1}_{0}(M);{\sf Pf}^{\otimes-n})\simeq\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle Z\in\bigoplus_{j+k=n}\Omega^{j}(M;{\rm mf}^{k,0}))\\
\displaystyle Z_{v}\in\bigoplus_{j+k=n-1}\Omega^{j}(M;{\rm mf}^{(k+2),2})\\
\displaystyle Z_{\bar{\tau}}\in\bigoplus_{j+k=n-1}\Omega^{k}(M;{\rm mf}^{k,-2})\end{array}\right\}

satisfying

(36)

\displaystyle{d}Z=0\quad\partial_{v}Z={d}Z_{v}\quad\partial_{\bar{\tau}}Z={d}Z_{\bar{\tau}},

where
d
is the exterior derivative,
\partial_{v}
is the derivative with respect to the volume of a lattice, and
\partial_{\bar{\tau}}
is the derivative with respect to the antiholomorphic part of the conformal modulus of the lattice. In particular, a degree
n
field theory determines a (de Rham) cohomology class
[Z]
with the properties

(37)

\displaystyle[\partial_{v}Z]=[{d}Z_{v}]=0,\quad[\partial_{\bar{\tau}}Z]=[{d}Z_{\bar{\tau}}]=0,\implies[Z]\in{\rm H}^{n}(M;{\rm MF}^{\omega})

implying that
[Z]
is a class valued in the ring
{\rm MF}^{\omega}
of weak modular forms.

Remark 1.19
.

The datum
Z_{\bar{\tau}}
with property (
37
) recovers the holomorphic anomaly equation
[
GJF19
, pages 9 and 14]
.

To summarize the discussion so far, restricting a degree
n
field theory to supertori and supercylinders in
M
gives the indicated arrows

(38)

2|1\hbox{-{\sf EFT}}_{{\rm co}}^{n}(M)
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}^{n}(M)
{\rm KO}^{n}(M)(\!(q)\!)
{\rm H}^{n}(M;{\rm MF})
{\rm H}^{n}(M;{\rm MF}^{\omega})
{\rm H}^{n}(M;{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}]).
q
-expand
Ch
Restrict to cylinders + Index
Restrict to tori

These restrictions satisfy a compatibility property: the value of a field theory on a supertorus is the trace of the value on a supercylinder
[
ST12
, §2]
,

(39)

\displaystyle Z={\sf sTr}_{n}(q^{L_{0}}{\bar{q}}^{\bar{L}_{0}})\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M;C^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}))

in the notation of (
30
) and (
1.18
). Using that the
S^{1}
-eigenspaces in Propositon
1.13
are bounded below, the equality (
39
) implies that
[Z]\in{\rm H}^{n}(M;{\rm MF})\subset{\rm H}^{n}(M;{\rm MF}^{\omega})
determines a de Rham cohomology class with values in weakly holomorphic modular forms i.e.,
[Z]
is meromorphic at
i\infty
(see Definition
A.1
). This produces the long-dashed arrow in (
38
).

Remark 1.20
.

The supertrace on the right in (
39
) depends on the degree of the theory; it is a normalized version of the Clifford supertrace, see Remark
2.7
.

The dotted arrow in (
38
) is more difficult: the compatibility property (
39
) is not enough information to determine a map to
{\rm KO}^{n}_{\rm MF}
. Instead, one requires homotopy compatibility data relating de Rham cocycles in
\Omega^{n}(M;{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}])
that are extracted from the two sides of (
39
). On the left side, choosing a
\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\tau}}
-preimage
T
of
Z_{\bar{\tau}}
in (
36
) gives

Z_{\bar{\tau}}=\partial_{\bar{\tau}}T\implies\partial_{\bar{\tau}}(Z-dT)=0.

A choice of
T
always exists because the half plane
{\mathfrak{H}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
is Stein. By restricting to lattices of volume 1 and
q
-expanding, we obtain a differential form

(40)

\displaystyle(Z-dT)|_{v=1}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M;{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}])\qquad{\rm(depends\ on\ choice\ of}\ T).

On the right hand side of (
39
), the Pontryagin character of a choice of index bundle constructs a cocycle in the Cech–de Rham complex with coefficients in formal power series,

(41)

\displaystyle{\rm Ch}({\rm Ind}(\bar{G}_{0}))\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{U};{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}])\qquad{\rm(Chern\ character\ of\ the\ index\ bundle)}.

Definition
1.14
guarantees that an index bundle exists for each
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, but its construction depends on choices of cutoffs, see §
D.3
.
Using (
39
), the cocycles (
40
) and (
41
) represent the same cohomology class, but need not be equal as de Rham cocycles. Bismut’s local index theorem
[
Bis86
]
specifies a coboundary measuring their difference. This coboundary is the homotopy compatibility required for the dotted arrow in (
38
).

In §
8
, we construct the groupoids
2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
that are purpose-built to capture the pieces of a
2|1
-Euclidean field theory that determine a
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
-cocycle via (
4
).

Definition 1.21
.

The groupoid
2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
has objects degree
n
representations of
\mathscr{C}^{2|1}(M)
(Definition
8.1
) that are reflection positive (Definition
8.15
), trace class (Definition
8.20
) and admit cutoffs (Definition
1.14
). Morphisms are isomorphisms between degree
n
representations of
\mathscr{C}^{2|1}(M)
that are compatible with the reflection structure and trace.

Proposition 1.22

(Lemma
8.11
and Propositions
8.18
and
8.22
)
.

There is a span

2|1\hbox{-{\sf EFT}}^{n}(M)\hookleftarrow 2|1\hbox{-{\sf EFT}}_{{\rm co}}^{n}(M)\xrightarrow{\rm restrict}2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M),

i.e., the groupoid
2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
admits a restriction map from the subcategory of Stolz and Teichner’s field theories that admit cutoffs.

By definition, the dashed arrow in (
38
) factors through
2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
. This completes the sketch of the cocycle map (
4
) in Theorem
1.1
; the complete proof is in §
9.4
.

Remark 1.23
.

When
M={\rm pt}
, the construction of the dotted arrow in (
38
) reduces to the torsion invariant in the physical argument from
[
GJF19
, pages 14-15]
. In short, there are two possible ways to extract a holomorphic quantity from a
\mathcal{N}=(0,1)
-theory, namely (
40
) and (
41
). Each way depends on a choice, and the difference between the choices defines an invariant. The choices in
[
GJF19
, pages 14-15]
are identical to (
40
) and (
41
).

An object of
2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
is determined by the geometric data in Propositions
1.13
and
1.18
satisfying various properties. Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
follow from explicit construction of such data and verification of the properties. Theorem
1.3
(proved in §
9.6
) uses the Bismut–Ramond superconnection from Proposition
1.5
to construct a degree
-n
representation of supercylinders; Bismut’s local index theorem plays a vital role for verifying the cutoff property for this representation. Theorem
1.2
(proved in §
9.5
) follows by first realizing the
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
-Euler class of a string vector bundle
V\to M
in terms of an infinite sequence of finite rank Clifford module bundles built out of
V
, see §
3.7
. A choice of connection on
V
then supplies the data (
28
) which (after a choice of string structure) determines an object
{\rm Eu}(V)\in 2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{{\rm dim}(V)}(M)
. In this case, the cutoff property follows from Remark
1.16
.

1.4.
Acknowledgements

This project has been in gestation for several years and has benefited from conversations with many colleagues, including Matt Ando, Mark Behrens, David Ben-Zvi, Emily Cliff, Nora Ganter, André Henriques, Tom Nevins, Stephan Stolz and Arnav Tripathy. I would like to express particular thanks to Theo Johnson-Freyd and Peter Teichner for many enlightening conversations about supersymmetric field theories, and to Lennart Meier and Charles Rezk for their help in analyzing the string orientation of
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
. This work was supported by the National Science Foundation under grant number DMS-2205835.

2.
Motivation from supersymmetric quantum field theory

In this section we review Witten’s physical construction of the Witten genus via 2-dimensional quantum field theory with
\mathcal{N}=(0,1)
supersymmetry
[
Wit88a
]
. His argument shows that the partition function of this type of quantum field theory is an integral modular by exploiting a compatibility property between values on cylinders and values on tori. For families of field theories this compatibility property becomes compatibility data, which is the key observation for extracting
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
-valued invariants; compare diagrams (
38
) and (
2.5
).

2.1.
Supersymmetric quantum mechanics and the index theorem

Let
X
be an
n
-dimensional Riemannian spin manifold with principal spin bundle
P\to X
. The spinor bundle
{\mathbb{S}}:=P\times_{{\rm Spin}(n)}{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{n}
supports the
{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{-n}
-linear Dirac operator
\not{D}
, reviewed in §
D.1
below. McKean and Singer’s argument
[
MS67
]
shows that the Clifford supertrace
{\sf sTr}_{{\rm Cl}_{-n}}(e^{-t\not{D}^{2}})
is independent of
t
, and the limit

(42)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}{\sf sTr}_{{\rm Cl}_{-n}}(e^{-t\not{D}^{2}})={\rm sdim}_{{\rm Cl}_{-n}}(\ker(\not{D})),

computes the Clifford super dimension of the kernel of
\not{D}^{2}
. Under the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro construction
[
ABS64
]
, the Clifford module
\ker(\not{D})
is the
{\rm KO}^{-n}({\rm pt})
-valued index of the Dirac operator. The Clifford superdimension (
42
) is the rationalization of this class, which equals the usual index of
\not{D}
when the dimension
n
is divisible by 4, and is zero otherwise.
On the other hand, the
t\to 0
limit can be identified with the integral of the
\hat{A}
-form, e.g., see
[
BGV92
, Theorem 4.21]
. In summary, the
t\to 0
and
t\to\infty
limits give the two sides of the Atiyah–Singer index theorem

\displaystyle{\sf sTr}_{{\rm Cl}_{-n}}(e^{-t\not{D}^{2}})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle{\rm sdim}_{{\rm Cl}_{-n}}(\ker(\not{D}))&t\to\infty\\
\displaystyle\int_{X}\hat{A}(X)&t\to 0.\end{array}\right.

Below we review a description of (
2.1
) in the language of supersymmetric quantum mechanics due to Witten
[
Wit82
]
and Alvarez-Gaumé
[
AG83
]
.

The basic data of a quantum mechanical system is a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded space of states
\mathcal{H}
with a hermitian inner product and a self-adjoint even operator
H
(the Hamiltonian) generating time evolution. In the Wick-rotated theory, time evolution is a trace class semigroup representation

(46)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\to{\rm End}(\mathcal{H}),\qquad t\mapsto e^{-tH}\qquad\hbox to55.32pt{\vbox to39.56pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 39.12988pt\lower-5.55522pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} ;

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-35.79688pt}{11.15099pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{1.37361pt}{11.55972pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

As indicated on the right of (
46
), one can reformulate this semigroup representation geometrically: the parameter
t
represents a length of an interval
I_{t}
. Gluing intervals adds the lengths,
I_{t}\circ I_{s}\simeq I_{t+s}
, corresponding to multiplication in the semigroup.

A superalgebra
A
acting on
\mathcal{H}
is a
symmetry algebra
if it commutes with
H
. A trace on
A
(see §
B.2
) defines the
partition function

(47)

\displaystyle Z:={\sf sTr}_{A}(e^{-tH})\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{>0};A/[A,A])

valued in the abelianization, i.e., the zeroth Hochschild homology of
A
. In the geometric picture, the trace (
47
) corresponds to gluing together the ends of the length
t
interval in (
46
) to obtain a circle of circumference
t
.

Supersymmetric
quantum mechanics adds the data of odd self-adjoint operators
Q_{1},\dots Q_{\mathcal{N}}
satisfying
[
Wit82
]

Q_{i}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}[Q_{i},Q_{i}]=H\ {\rm for}\ i=1,\dots,\mathcal{N}\quad{\rm and}\quad[Q_{i},Q_{j}]=0\ {\rm for}\ i\neq j,

where the bracket
[-,-]
is the supercommutator. By the graded Jacobi identity, the
Q_{i}
generate a symmetry algebra.
A Riemannian spin manifold
X
determines a quantum mechanical system with
\mathcal{N}=1
supersymmetry via
[
ST04
, Remark 3.2.21]

(48)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}=\Gamma(X;{\mathbb{S}}),\quad H=\not{D}^{2},\quad Q=\not{D},

and the Clifford algebra
{\rm Cl}_{-n}
is a symmetry algebra commuting with
Q
. Supersymmetry promotes the semigroup representation (
46
) to a supersemigroup representation,

(49)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{>0}\to{\rm End}(\mathcal{H}),\qquad(t,\theta)\mapsto e^{-tH+\theta D}.

The geometric description in terms of intervals of length
t
in (
46
) generalizes to the gluing of
superintervals
of length
(t,\theta)
. This can be made precise in Stolz and Teichner’s formalism for
1|1
-Euclidean field theories, and we refer to the companion paper
[
BE23b
]
for details.

A consequence of supersymmetry is that the partition function (
47
) is independent of
t
. For the system (
48
), the
t\to\infty
limit computes (e.g., see
[
GJF19
, pg. 5-6]
)

\displaystyle Z={\sf sTr}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{-n}}(e^{-tQ^{2}})={\sf sTr}(\Gamma_{-n}\circ e^{-tQ^{2}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t\to\infty}}{{=}}\begin{array}[]{c}{\rm 1\hbox{-}point\ function}\\
{\rm of\ \Gamma_{-n}}\end{array}

where
\Gamma_{-n}
is a particular element of the Clifford action determining the Clifford supertrace, see (
D.1
). When
n=0
(or after a Morita equivalence, when
n=0\ {\rm mod}\ 8
) (
2.1
) is a signed count of ground states, called the
Witten index
of the quantum mechanical system. On the other hand the
t\to 0
limit can be computed using path integral techniques,

(53)

\displaystyle Z={\sf sTr}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{-n}}(e^{-tQ^{2}})=\int_{LX}e^{-S(\gamma)}d\gamma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t\to 0}}{{=}}\int_{X}\hat{A}(X)

as an integral over the (super) loop space. In the
t\to 0
limit, this integral (formally) localizes onto the constant loops
[
Ati85
]
,
[
Wit82
, §4]
.
One can then
define
the path integral as a formal application of the fixed point formula in
S^{1}
-equivariant cohomology, yielding the integral of the
\hat{A}
-form over
X
, e.g., see
[
JP90
]
. To summarize, the limits of (
2.1
) have the interpretation in supersymmetric quantum mechanics

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}t\to\infty:\\
{\rm count\ of}\\
{\rm ground\ states}\end{array}\right)\rightsquigarrow\quad{\rm sdim}_{{\rm Cl}_{-n}}(\ker(\not{D}))=\int_{X}\hat{A}(X)\quad\rotatebox[origin]{-180.0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}t\to 0:\\
{\rm localized}\\
{\rm path\ integral}\end{array}\right).

Physically, the equality (
2.1
) is the agreement between the Hamiltonian and Lagrangian (i.e., path integral) approaches to quantum mechanics, giving a physics argument for the Atiyah–Singer index theorem. The Witten genus and its modularity properties are deduced from a generalization of (
2.1
) in 2-dimensional supersymmetric quantum field theory.

2.2.
2-dimensional Euclidean quantum field theory

The basic data in a 2-dimensional Euclidean quantum field theory is a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded state space
\mathcal{H}
with a hermitian inner product, a self-adjoint Hamiltonian
H\in{\rm End}(\mathcal{H})
generating infinitesimal time evolution, and a self-adjoint momentum operator
P\in{\rm End}(\mathcal{H})
generating infinitesimal spatial translation. For 2-dimensional quantum field theories compactified on a circle of radius
r
, the operator
P
has spectrum in
\frac{1}{r}\cdot{\mathbb{Z}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
. Define the operators
[
Wit86
, Eq. 106]

(61)

\displaystyle L_{0}=(H+P)/2,\qquad\bar{L}_{0}=(H-P)/2.

Consider the semigroup
{\mathfrak{H}}/2\pi r{\mathbb{Z}}
determined by addition in
{\mathfrak{H}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
, the upper half plane. Define a representation of this semigroup via

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle q^{L_{0}}\bar{q}^{\bar{L}_{0}}=e^{-tH+ixP}\colon{\mathfrak{H}}/2\pi r{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\rm End}(\mathcal{H}),\\
\hbox{}\\
\tau=\frac{x+it}{2\pi},\ q=e^{2\pi i\tau}.\end{array}\qquad\hbox to93.73pt{\vbox to60.49pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 39.12988pt\lower-6.80186pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} ;

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{34.09329pt}{12.24026pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

This generalizes the time-evolution semigroup (
46
) in quantum mechanics. As indicated on the right of (
2.2
), one can reformulate this semigroup representation geometrically: the parameters
(r,\tau)
determine a cylinder
{\rm C}_{r,\tau}
viewed as a Euclidean (i.e., flat Riemannian) bordism between circles of radius
r
, where the complex parameter
\tau
encodes the height
t
of the cylinder and the rotation
x\ {\rm mod}\ 2\pi r
that measures the difference in parameterizations of the boundary circles
[
Wit86
, Figure 2]
. Composition in the semigroup then corresponds to the gluing of cylinders,

(66)

\xrightarrow{\rm glue=semigroup}
{\rm C}_{r,\tau^{\prime}}\circ{\rm C}_{r,\tau}={\rm C}_{r,\tau+\tau^{\prime}}
{\rm C}_{r,\tau}
{\rm C}_{r,\tau^{\prime}}

\xrightarrow{\rm glue=trace}
{\rm C}_{r,\tau}
{\rm T}_{r,\tau}

As explained in
[
Wit86
, §3]
, gluing the ends of a cylinder together corresponds to the trace,

Z_{r}(\tau):={\sf Tr}(q^{L_{0}}\bar{q}^{\bar{L}_{0}})\in C^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}/2\pi r{\mathbb{Z}})

and the
partition function

Z_{r}(\tau)
is interpreted as the value of the field theory on a torus
{\rm T}_{r,\tau}={\mathbb{R}}^{2}/(2\pi r{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus 2\pi\tau{\mathbb{Z}})
associated with the based lattice
2\pi r{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus 2\pi\tau{\mathbb{Z}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
. Path integral arguments lead one to expect that the partition function only depends on the underlying torus, not the based lattice. Hence, given operators (
61
) for each
r\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
, we expect an
{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
-invariant function
Z
,

(67)

\displaystyle Z(r,\tau):={\sf Tr}(q^{L_{0}}\bar{q}^{\bar{L}_{0}})\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times{\mathfrak{H}})^{{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})},

\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right]\cdot(r,\tau)=\left(r|c\tau+d|,\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\right),\quad\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right]\in{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})

where in (
67
)
L_{0}
and
\bar{L}_{0}
are now a family of operators depending on
r\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
.

An enhancement of the picture (
66
) endows the surfaces with spin structures, resulting in a
spin Euclidean field theory
. In this case, there are two possible circles of radius
r
corresponding to periodic spin structure (called the
Ramond sector
) and the antiperiodic spin structure (called the
Neveu–Schwarz sector
). Hence, a spin Euclidean field theory has a pair of Hilbert spaces of states and a pair of semigroup representations (
2.2
), one for each spin structure on the circle. The partition function of a spin theory comes in 4 flavors (
72
) corresponding to the 4 spin structures on a Euclidean torus
{\mathbb{C}}/2\pi r{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus 2\pi\tau{\mathbb{Z}}
; see §
4.2
. The relationship with the semigroups of operators comes from taking the trace or the supertrace in the Ramond or Neveu-Schwarz sector. The
{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
-invariance property (
67
) is refined to an
{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
-invariance property for the metaplectic double cover, where the action permutes three of the spin structures; see §
A.3
. Our focus in this paper is on the Ramond sector, and the partition function associated with the supertrace, i.e., the value of the theory on nonbounding tori.

2.3.
Symmetries, free fermions, and degree
n
field theories

The generalization of a symmetry algebra
A
in quantum mechanics is a
current algebra
in 2-dimensional field theory. A degree
n
Euclidean field theory carries an action by the current algebra of the
n
(chiral) free fermions, which is essentially determined by the Clifford algebra of a loop space, see (
68
). The discussion below is informal, but there are many mathematically rigorous approaches; see
[
Seg04
, §8]
and
[
ST04
, §2.3]
, and also
[
DH11
,
Ten17
,
LR20
]
.

For a spin circle with radius
r
, consider the (infinite-dimensional) Clifford algebra

(68)

\displaystyle{\rm Fer}_{n}(S^{1}_{r}):={\rm Cl}\Big(\Gamma(S^{1}_{r};{\mathbb{S}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n}),\int_{S^{1}_{r}}\langle-,-\rangle\Big)

where
\Gamma(S^{1}_{r};{\mathbb{S}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
is the space of sections of the
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
-twisted spinor bundle,
\langle-,-\rangle
is the
{\mathbb{C}}
-bilinear extension of the standard inner product on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, and the integral is defined using the isomorphism
{\mathbb{S}}\otimes{\mathbb{S}}\simeq\Omega^{1}_{S^{1}_{r}}
. For the nonbounding spin structure,
{\mathbb{S}}
is the trivial bundle; this allows the identification
{\rm Fer}_{n}(S^{1}_{r})\simeq{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}(L_{r}{\mathbb{R}}^{n})
with the complex Clifford algebra of the loop space of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
for loops of radius
r
with inner product
\int_{S^{1}_{r}}\langle-,-\rangle dt
.

Below we will focus on two pieces of data coming from a degree
n
Euclidean field theory:

(i.)

a
{\rm Fer}_{n}(S^{1}_{r})
-module structure on
\mathcal{H}
compatible with a semigroup representation (
2.2
);

(ii.)

a trace valued in a line bundle over the moduli of spin tori.

Each of these aspects requires a bit of setup.

We begin with (i). It is useful to view the pair
(\Gamma(S^{1}_{r};{\mathbb{S}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n}),\int_{S^{1}_{r}}\langle-,-\rangle)
in (
68
) as an odd symplectic vector space, and
{\rm Fer}_{n}(S^{1}_{r})
the associated Weyl algebra. From this perspective, a spin cylinder
{\rm C}_{r,\tau}
determines a Lagrangian subspace

(69)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\rm harmonic}({\rm C}_{r,\tau};{\mathbb{S}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})\xhookrightarrow{{\rm restrict}}\Gamma(S^{1}_{r};{\mathbb{S}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})\oplus\Gamma(S^{1}_{r};{\mathbb{S}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})

by restriction of harmonic spinors (i.e., the kernel of the Dirac operator) to the boundaries of
C_{r,\tau}
. A general construction associates to the Lagrangian subspace (
69
) a Fock module
{\rm Fer}_{n}({\rm C}_{r,\tau})
that is an irreducible
{\rm Fer}_{n}(S^{1}_{r})
-bimodule
[
ST04
, Definition 2.2.4]
. This Fock module is quite explicit, simply being
{\rm Fer}_{n}(S^{1}_{r})
as a bimodule over itself with left action modified by an algebra isomorphism
[
ST11
, page 59]

(70)

\displaystyle\varphi_{\tau}\colon{\rm Fer}_{n}(S^{1}_{r})\to{\rm Fer}_{n}(S^{1}_{r})

for each
\tau\in{\mathfrak{H}}
.
For each sector in a spin Euclidean field theory, a degree
n
structure determines the data of a
{\rm Fer}_{n}(S^{1}_{r})
-action on
\mathcal{H}
that is compatible with the semigroup representation (
2.2
) via (
70
).

Remark 2.1
.

Decomposing into Fourier modes allows one to describe (
68
) and (
70
) in terms of an infinite tensor product of finite-dimensional Clifford algebras, see (
523
), (
528
) and (
529
).

Now we turn to the trace data (ii) above. In a degree
n
Euclidean field theory, the trace of a semigroup representation in
{\rm Fer}_{n}(S^{1}_{r})
-modules takes values in the family of vector spaces
[
ST12
, §2]

(71)

\displaystyle{\sf sTr}_{{\rm Fer}_{n}}(q^{L_{0}}\bar{q}^{\bar{L}_{0}})\in{\rm Fer}_{n}({\rm C}_{r,0})\otimes_{{\rm Fer}_{n}(S^{1}_{r})\otimes{\rm Fer}_{n}(S^{1}_{r})^{\sf{op}}}{\rm Fer}_{n}({\rm C}_{r,\tau})\quad({\rm for}\ (r,\tau)\ {\rm fixed}),

i.e., the zeroth Hochschild homology of
{\rm Fer}_{n}(S^{1}_{r}
valued in the bimodule
{\rm Fer}_{n}({\rm C}_{r,\tau})
, analogously to the trace (
47
). For each
(r,\tau)
, the vector space (
71
) is 1-dimensional. Letting
(r,\tau)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times{\mathfrak{H}}
vary, the resulting line bundle is canonically isomorphic to the restriction of the
n
th power of the Pfaffian line along the covering map
[
ST04
, Definition 2.3.12]

{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times{\mathfrak{H}}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times{\mathfrak{H}}/\!\!/{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}),\quad r\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0},\ \tau\in{\mathfrak{H}}.

The
{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
-invariance condition (
67
) generalizes to
{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
-equivariance data for the Pfaffian line and an
{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
-invariance property for a section. For the supetrace in the Ramond sector, this reads

(72)

\displaystyle Z:={\sf sTr}_{{\rm Fer}_{n}}(q^{L_{0}}\bar{q}^{\bar{L}_{0}})\in\Gamma({\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times{\mathfrak{H}};{\rm Pf}^{\otimes n})^{{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})}.

These
{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
-invariant sections are a
C^{\infty}
-version of modular forms; see §
A.3
.

Remark 2.2
.

There are versions of the partition function (
72
) for the other 3 spin structures on the torus. A choice of sector (either Ramond or Neveu–Schwarz sector) together with a choice of ordinary trace or the supertrace corresponds to the 4 spin structures, where (
72
) is the supertrace in the Ramond sector. For the remaining 3 choices, the
{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
-action permutes the spin structures, and so the invariance condition analogous to (
72
) involves a compatibility condition among 3 functions, e.g., see
[
ST11
, page 60]
.

Remark 2.3
.

Symmetries of a quantum system are inextricably linked with anomalies. In the case at hand, Fock modules for the algebra (
68
) are closely related positive energy representations of the loop group
L{\rm Spin}(n)
. This leads to a connection between degree
n
field theories and classical Chern–Simons theory
[
ST04
, §5.2]
; roughly a degree
n
theory determines a (non-topological) boundary condition for classical Chern–Simons theory of the group
{\rm Spin}(n)
at level 1, which is an invertible topological field theory. Boundary theories (also known as
relative
or
twisted
theories) can be interpreted as anomalous where the bulk theory encodes the anomaly, e.g., see
[
Fre14
, §2.3]
and
[
FT14
]
.

2.4.
2-dimensional supersymmetric quantum field theory

A further enhancement of a degree
n
spin Euclidean field theory is a
supersymmetric Euclidean field theory
, which requires the data of odd square roots of the operators (
61
). The data of
k
odd square roots of
L_{0}
and
l
odd square roots of
\bar{L}_{0}
is called
\mathcal{N}=(k,l)
supersymmetry
. We will be most interested in
\mathcal{N}=(0,1)
supersymmetry, i.e., a single odd operator
\bar{G}_{0}
with

\bar{G}_{0}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}[\bar{G}_{0},\bar{G}_{0}]=\bar{L}_{0}.

Generalizing (
49
) in supersymmetric quantum mechanics, the operator
\bar{G}_{0}
promotes the semigroup representation (
2.2
) to a
super semigroup
representation

(73)

\displaystyle q^{L_{0}}\bar{q}^{\bar{L}_{0}+\sqrt{2\pi i}\theta Q},\qquad{\mathfrak{H}}^{2|1}\to{\rm End}(\mathcal{H})

where
{\mathfrak{H}}^{2|1}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}
is the upper half-space of the super Lie group
{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}
with multiplication

(\tau,\bar{\tau},\theta)\cdot(\tau^{\prime},\bar{\tau}^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})=(\tau+\tau^{\prime},\bar{\tau}+\bar{\tau}+\theta^{\prime}\theta^{\prime},\theta+\theta^{\prime}),\qquad(\tau,\bar{\tau},\theta),(\tau^{\prime},\bar{\tau}^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}.

In this case, the McKean–Singer argument that results in
t
-independence in (
2.1
) results in
\bar{q}
-independence in (
72
)
[
ST04
, page 46]
,

\displaystyle Z(r,\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\sf sTr}_{A}(q^{L_{0}}\bar{q}^{\bar{L}_{0}})={\sf sTr}_{A}(q^{L_{0}}|_{{\rm ker}(\bar{L}_{0})})=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\sf sTr}_{A}(q^{L_{0}}|_{{\rm ker}(\bar{L}_{0})\bigcap\mathcal{H}_{k}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}q^{k}{\rm sdim}_{{\rm Fer}_{n}}({\rm ker}(\bar{L}_{0})\bigcap\mathcal{H}_{k})

where
\mathcal{H}_{k}
is the
k
th eigenspace of
L_{0}-\bar{L}_{0}
. The above computation has three consequences. First,
Z=Z(r,\tau)
is independent of
\bar{q}
, and hence depends holomorphically on
{\mathfrak{H}}
. Second, the
q
-expansion of
Z
has integral coefficients, implying that the
{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
dependence is trivial as there is no nontrivial
{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
-deformation of an integral power series. Third and finally, since a trace class operator is compact and the semigroup representation is valued in trace class operators, the powers of
q
must be bounded below. This brings us to the punchline.

Expectation 2.4

(
[
ST04
, Theorem 3.3.14]
)
.

The partition function (
72
) of a 2-dimensional field theory with
\mathcal{N}=(0,1)
supersymmetry and degree
n
is an integral modular form of weight
-n/2
.

Remark 2.5
.

Another source of modular forms in 2-dimensional field theory are
chiral conformal
field theories.
A
conformal
field theory includes the data of operators
L_{k}
and
\bar{L}_{k}
on
\mathcal{H}
for
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
generating a representation of the Virasoro algebra on
\mathcal{H}
that extends (
72
). A
chiral
field theory has
\bar{L}_{k}=0
the zero operator for all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Hence, a chiral theory is canonically endowed with a generator of
\mathcal{N}=(0,1)
supersymmetry by taking
\bar{G}_{0}=0
. It is expected that chiral conformal theories have unique extensions to Euclidean field theories with
\mathcal{N}=(0,1)
supersymmetry; see Hypothesis
7.10
and Remark
7.11
below.

The geometric interpretation of the super semigroup (
73
) involves the gluing of Euclidean
supercylinders
. These can be visualized using the same pictures as (
66
), but where the moduli parameters are specified by
(r,\tau,\bar{\tau},\theta)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times{\mathfrak{H}}^{2|1}
, and gluing of Euclidean supercylinders gives

{\rm C}_{r,\tau,\bar{\tau},\theta}\circ{\rm C}_{r,\tau^{\prime},\bar{\tau}^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}}\simeq{\rm C}_{r,\tau+\tau^{\prime},\bar{\tau}+\bar{\tau}^{\prime}+\theta\theta^{\prime},\theta+\theta^{\prime}}.

The picture in (
73
) corresponding to the trace similarly involves a moduli space of supertori, determined by a lattice
(\ell_{1},\bar{\ell}_{1},\lambda_{1}){\mathbb{Z}}\oplus(\ell_{2},\bar{\ell}_{2},\lambda_{2}){\mathbb{Z}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}
. These ideas can be made precise in Stolz and Teichner’s framework.
The moduli spaces of these objects are remarkably rich, and subtle features of their geometry are crucial in the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

2.5.
The Dirac–Ramond operator and the Witten genus

Next we sketch Witten’s partial construction of the
\mathcal{N}=(0,1)
-supersymmetric sigma model with target a string manifold. To start, for a real vector bundle
V\to X
, define the formal power series of vector bundles

(74)

\displaystyle W(V)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{k\geq 0}{\rm Sym}_{q^{k}}(V\otimes{\mathbb{C}})=\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}q^{k}W_{k}(V)

where
{\rm Sym}^{l}(V)
denotes the
l^{\rm th}
symmetric power of
V
and

(75)

\displaystyle{\rm Sym}_{q^{k}}(V\otimes{\mathbb{C}})=\bigoplus_{l\geq 0}q^{kl}{\rm Sym}^{l}(V\otimes{\mathbb{C}})=\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}+q^{k}(V\otimes{\mathbb{C}})+q^{2k}{\rm Sym}^{2}(V\otimes{\mathbb{C}})+\dots

If
V
is equipped with a connection
\nabla^{V}
, then the vector bundles
W_{k}(V)
inherit a connection
W_{k}(\nabla^{V})
, and we write
W(\nabla^{V})
for the formal power series of these connections.

Definition 2.6
.

The
Dirac–Ramond
operator of a spin manifold
X
is the formal power series of
{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{-n}
-linear twisted Dirac operators (see
[
Wit88a
, Eq. 17]
)

\bar{G}_{0}:=\not{D}\otimes W(\nabla^{TX})=\sum_{k\geq 0}q^{k}\not{D}\otimes W_{k}(\nabla^{TX}),\quad\bar{G}_{0}\colon\Gamma({\mathbb{S}}\otimes W(TX))\to\Gamma({\mathbb{S}}\otimes W(TX))

where
\not{D}
is the
{\rm Cl}_{-n}
-linear Dirac operator on
X
and
\nabla^{TX}
is the Levi-Civita connection on
TX
. Define the
index
of the Dirac–Ramond operator as the formal power series,

(76)

\displaystyle{\rm Ind}(\bar{G}_{0}):=\phi(q)^{n}\sum_{k\geq 0}q^{k}{\rm Ind}_{{\rm Cl}_{-n}}(\not{D}\otimes W_{k}(TX))\in{\rm KO}^{-n}({\rm pt})(\!(q)\!)

where
{\rm Ind}_{{\rm Cl}_{-n}}
is the (real) Clifford index and
\phi(q)
is defined as

(77)

\displaystyle\phi(q)=\prod_{m>0}(1-q^{m})\in{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!).

Remark 2.7
.

The normalization by
\phi(q)
in (
76
) comes the trace for
n
copies of the chiral free fermion. The algebra
{\rm Fer}_{n}(S^{1}_{r})
is Morita equivalent to the Clifford algebra
{\rm Cl}_{n}
. Consequently, the trace for the fermions is compatible with the Clifford supertrace after normalizing by the trace of the Morita bimodule (
536
), which is precisely
\phi(q)^{n}
. This normalization also agrees with
[
Wit88a
, Equations 17-18]
.

The Ramond sector of the supersymmetric
\sigma
-model with target
X
has as its space of states the sections
\mathcal{H}=\Gamma(X;{\mathbb{S}}\otimes W(TX))
. For
r\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
define the operators

\bar{G}_{0}=\not{D}\otimes W(\nabla^{TX}),\qquad P|_{W_{k}(TX))}=k/r,\quad\implies\bar{L}_{0}=\bar{G}_{0}^{2},\quad L_{0}=\frac{1}{2}(H+P)=\bar{G}_{0}^{2}+P.

The algebra
{\rm Cl}_{-n}
acts on
\mathcal{H}
commuting with the above operators. Using the normalized trace outlined in Remark
2.7
, we have the partition function

(78)

\displaystyle Z:=\phi(q)^{n}{\sf sTr}_{{\rm Cl}_{-n}}(q^{L_{0}}\bar{q}^{\bar{L}_{0}})={\rm Ind}(\bar{G}_{0})\in{\rm KO}^{-n}({\rm pt})(\!(q)\!).

Hence,
Z
encodes a signed count of ground states of
H
for each momentum number
k
.

One may also compute the partition function (
78
) in the path integral formalism. To describe the result of this computation we recall the following.

Definition 2.8
.

Let
X
be a Riemannian manifold with Levi-Civita connection
\nabla^{X}
. The
Witten form
of
X
is

\displaystyle{\rm Wit}(\nabla^{X})=\exp\left(\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{{\sf Tr}(F^{2k})E_{2k}(q)}{(2\pi i)^{2k}(2k)}\right)\in\Omega^{\bullet}(X;{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!))

where
F=(\nabla^{X})^{2}
is the curvature of the Levi-Civita connection. The
Witten genus
of
X
is

(79)

\displaystyle\langle{\rm Wit}(\nabla^{X}),[X]\rangle=\int_{X}{\rm Wit}(\nabla^{X})\in{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!).

If
X
is even dimensional with a string structure (so that
[{\sf Tr}(F^{2})]=0
) then the Witten genus is a modular form of weight
n/2
. Note that (
79
) is automatically zero if
n\neq 0\ {\rm mod}\ 4
.

Analogously to (
53
), the path integral expression for the partition function can be rigorously defined by localization to the
T^{2}
-fixed points
[
Wit99
, §2.6]
,
[
BE
]

(80)

\displaystyle Z(\tau)=\int_{{\sf Map}(T_{\tau},X)}e^{-S(\phi)}d\phi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm vol}\to 0}}{{=}}\int_{X}{\rm Wit}(\nabla^{X})

and a formal application of the fixed point theorem results in an integral of the Witten form. However, there is some ambiguity in this computation coming from the fact that the 2nd Eisenstein series is defined by a conditionally convergent series. This is an example of an
anomaly
: a priori, different choices result in different values of the path integral (
80
). However, when
[p_{1}(\nabla^{X})]=0
all choices give the same result, and the anomaly vanishes.

In summary, the Ramond sector partition function of the supersymmetric
\sigma
-model with target
X
can be computed two ways: the first is a signed count of ground states, whereas the second evaluates the path integral using localization:

\begin{array}[]{c}X\ {\rm a\ }4k\hbox{-}{\rm dimensional}\\
{\rm string\ manifold}\end{array}
{\rm MF}^{\mathbb{Z}}
{\mathbb{Z}}(\!(q)\!)
{\rm MF}
{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!).

\lrcorner

q
-expand
\begin{array}[]{c}{\rm signed\ count\ of\ ground}\\
{\rm states}\ {\rm(supercylinders)}\end{array}
\begin{array}[]{c}{\rm path\ integral}\\
{\rm(supertori)}\end{array}

The signed count of ground states is extracted from the value of the field theory on supercylinders, whereas the path integral computes the value of the field theory on supertori.
Using the Atiyah–Singer index theorem and the identity (proved in
[
Zag86
]
)

(88)

\displaystyle\sigma(\tau,z)=\frac{e^{z/2}-e^{-z/2}}{z/2}\prod_{k>0}\frac{(1-q^{k}z)(1-q^{k}z^{-1})}{(1-q^{k})^{2}}=\exp\left(-\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{E_{2k}(q)}{2k(2\pi i)^{2k}}z^{2k}\right).

these two computations of the partition function are compatible when
X
has a string structure. Hence we obtain an equality of elements of
{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)

(89)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Z}}(\!(q)\!)\ni{\rm Ind}(\bar{G}_{0})=\int{\rm Wit}(\nabla^{X})\in{\rm MF}

showing that the Witten genus is an integral modular form.

In analogy to the physics argument for the index theorem (
2.1
), the equality (
89
) is an instance where the Lagrangian (i.e., path integral) agrees with the Hamiltonian formalism. When passing to families of field theories there is a surprise: the analogous computations no longer agree on the nose. Instead, the results are homotopy compatible where the field theory itself specifies the data of the homotopy. This is the basic mechanism behind Theorem
1.1
that allows one to generalize Witten’s argument for invariants of field theories valued in integral modular forms as the (strict) pullback in rings (
2.5
) to invariants valued in
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
as the (homotopy) pullback in commutative ring spectra (
18
).

3.
Approximating
{\rm TMF}
by
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}

3.1.
The cohomology theory
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}

The cohomology theory
{\rm TMF}
is constructed as the derived global sections of the sheaf of elliptic cohomology theories (or rather, their representing
E_{\infty}
-rings) on the moduli stack of elliptic curves
\mathcal{M}_{\rm ell}
, e.g., see
[
Lur09
, §1.3]
for an overview. Restriction of sections along the map
{\rm Spec}({\mathbb{Z}}(\!(q)\!))\to\mathcal{M}_{\rm ell}
classifying the punctured formal neighborhood of the Tate curve constructs the
Miller character

(90)

\displaystyle{\rm TMF}\to{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!).

Indeed, the formal group law associated with the Tate curve is isomorphic to the multiplicative group, and so restriction initially yields a map of commutative ring spectra
{\rm TMF}\to{\rm K}(\!(q)\!)
. However, the Tate curve has a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-action by automorphisms, implying that restriction factors through the
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-homotopy fixed points
{\rm K}(\!(q)\!)^{h{\mathbb{Z}}/2}\simeq{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!)
.

Remark 3.1
.

Lurie’s approach to elliptic cohomology constructs (
90
) as a map of
E_{\infty}
-rings, see
[
Lur09
, §4.3]
for a sketch. A map (
90
) of commutative ring spectra is constructed explicitly in
[
HL16
, Appendix A]
, where
{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!)
is defined as

(91)

\displaystyle{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!)=q^{-1}\operatornamewithlimits{{hocolim}}_{k}{\rm KO}\wedge\{1,q,q^{2},\dots,q^{k-1}\}_{+},

and the map (
90
) is shown to be unique up to homotopy. Below, it will suffice to work with the Miller character as a map of commutative ring spectra.

Smashing the map (
90
) with the spectrum
{\rm H}_{\mathbb{C}}
gives the outer homotopy commutative square in (
18
) where
{\rm MF}
is the ring of weakly holomorphic modular forms, and we use

\pi_{*}({\rm TMF})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\simeq{\rm MF},\qquad\pi_{*}({\rm KO}(\!(q)\!))\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}],\qquad|u^{2}|=-4.

Definition 3.2
.

Define
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
in (
18
) as the homotopy pullback in commutative ring spectra. Let
{\rm K}_{\rm MF}
be the similarly defined commutative ring spectrum gotten by replacing
{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!)
by
{\rm K}(\!(q)\!)
in (
18
).

The spectrum
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
inherits a map from
{\rm TMF}
by the universal property (
18
), and in this way is an approximation to
{\rm TMF}
.

Proposition 3.3
.

The coefficients of
{\rm K}_{\rm MF}
and
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
are

\displaystyle\pi_{*}{\rm K}_{\rm MF}

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}{\rm MF}^{\mathbb{Z}}_{k}&*=2k\\
{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)/({\mathbb{Z}}(\!(q)\!)+{\rm MF}_{2k})&k=2k-1\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\pi_{*}{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}{\rm MF}_{4k}^{\mathbb{Z}}&*=8k\\
{\mathbb{Z}}/2(\!(q)\!)&*=8k+1\\
{\mathbb{Z}}/2(\!(q)\!)&*=8k+2\\
{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)/(2{\mathbb{Z}}(\!(q)\!)+{\rm MF}_{4k+2})&*=8k+3\\
{\rm MF}^{\mathbb{Z}}_{4k+2}&*=8k+4\\
0&*=8k+5\\
0&*=8k+6\\
{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)/({\mathbb{Z}}(\!(q)\!)+{\rm MF}_{4k})&*=8k-1\\
\end{array}\right.

where
{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)/({\mathbb{Z}}(\!(q)\!)+{\rm MF}_{2k})
is the quotient of Laurent series with
{\mathbb{C}}
coefficients by the subgroup generated by Laurent series with
{\mathbb{Z}}
coefficients and the image of
q
-expansion.

Proof.

We compute using the presentations

(104)

\displaystyle\pi_{*}{\rm K}\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}[u^{\pm 1}],\qquad\pi_{*}{\rm KO}={\mathbb{Z}}[\eta,\alpha,\beta^{\pm 1}]/\langle 2\eta,\eta^{3},\eta\alpha,\alpha^{2}-4\beta\rangle,

where generators have the degrees
|u|=-2
,
|\alpha|=-4
,
|\eta|=-1
and
|\beta|=-8
. The ring homomorphism
\pi_{*}{\rm KO}\to\pi_{*}{\rm K}
is determined by
\beta\mapsto u^{4}
,
\alpha\mapsto 2u^{2}
,
\eta\mapsto 0
. The vertical arrow on the right in (
18
) is determined by the Pontryagin character in real K-theory, defined as the composition of complexification and the usual Chern character,

{\rm KO}\to{\rm K}\xrightarrow{{\rm Ch}}{\rm H}_{{\mathbb{C}}[u^{\pm 1}]},\qquad\pi_{*}({\rm K})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}[u^{\pm 1}],\qquad|u|=-2.

Hence, this composition has image in the 4-periodic subring
{\mathbb{C}}[u^{\pm 2}]\subset{\mathbb{C}}[u^{\pm 1}]
. The lower horizontal arrow in (
18
) can be identified with
q
-expansion (
450
) of modular forms
[
Lau99
]
.

With the above facts in place, the result follows from the Mayer–Vietoris sequence for the homotopy pullback square (
18
). For
{\rm K}_{\rm MF}
, this sequence reads

0\to\pi_{2k}{\rm K}_{\rm MF}\to u^{2k}\cdot{\mathbb{Z}}(\!(q)\!)\oplus{\rm MF}_{2k}\to u^{2k}\cdot{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)\to\pi_{2k-1}{\rm K}_{\rm MF}\to 0

using that
\pi_{*}{\rm K}
is concentrated in even degrees, and so (
3.3
) follows immediately. The real case is argued similarly, using exactness of the sequence

\displaystyle\pi_{*+1}{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!)\oplus\pi_{*+1}{\rm H}_{{\rm MF}}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\pi_{*+1}{\rm H}_{{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}]}\to\pi_{*}{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\pi_{*}{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!)\oplus\pi_{*}{\rm H}_{\rm MF}\to\pi_{*}({\rm H}_{{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}]})

and the 8-fold periodicity of
{\rm KO}
to establish the 8 cases above. For example, with
*=8k
we have

\pi_{8k+1}{\rm H}_{{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}]}\simeq\{0\},\quad\pi_{8k}{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!)\oplus\pi_{8k}{\rm H}_{\rm MF}\simeq\beta^{k}\cdot{\mathbb{Z}}(\!(q)\!)\oplus{\rm MF}_{4k},

\pi_{8k}({\rm H}_{{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}]})\simeq u^{4k}\cdot{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)

confirming
\pi_{8k}{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}\simeq{\rm MF}_{4k}^{\mathbb{Z}}
.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

The ring structure on
\pi_{*}{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
takes a bit more work to spell out. However, the ring homomorphisms
\pi_{*}{\rm MString}\to\pi_{*}{\rm TMF}\to\pi_{*}{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
allow us to compute most of the products of interest using the known formulas in string cobordism or
\pi_{*}{\rm TMF}
. For example, the generator
\nu\in\pi_{3}({\rm TMF})\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}/24
is sent to a (nontrivial) class
\nu\in\pi_{3}{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
with order 24
[
BN14
, §3]
, and this class necessarily satisfies
\eta^{3}=12\nu
for
\eta\in\pi_{1}({\rm KO}_{\rm MF})
.

3.2.
Families of string manifolds and the families Witten form

The following enhances a family of Riemannian spin manifolds (e.g., see
[
Fre87
, Geometric Data 1.1]
) to include a geometric string structure in the sense of
[
Wal13
]
.

Definition 3.5
.

A
family of Riemannian string manifolds
is

(1)

a smooth fibration of manifolds
\pi\colon X\to M
where the vertical tangent bundle
T(X/M)\to X
has a chosen metric
g^{(X/M)}
and spin structure;

(2)

a projection
P:TX\to T(X/M)
; and

(3)

a trivialization of the Chern–Simons 2-gerbe with connection associated with the spin structure on
T(X/M)
(see
[
Wal13
, Definition 1.1.5.]
).

Remark 3.6
.

A
family of Riemannian spin manifolds
is the data (1) and (2); these uniquely specify a (Levi-Civita) connection
\nabla^{X/M}
on
T(X/M)

[
Fre87
, Lemma 1.3]
which determines the Chern–Simons 2-gerbe with connection in (3) as we now describe. For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the
Chern–Simons 2-gerbe
is a 2-gerbe with connection over the stack classifying
n
-dimensional spin bundles with connection, denoted
*/\!\!/{\rm Spin}(n)^{\nabla}
. Given a map of stacks
X\to*/\!\!/{\rm Spin}(n)^{\nabla}
classifying a metrized vector bundle
V
with compatible connection
\nabla^{V}
and choice of spin structure, the Chern–Simons 2-gerbe with connection can be pulled back to
X
. The curvature of the resulting 2-gerbe on
X
is precisely
\frac{p_{1}}{2}(\nabla^{V})
, the fractional first Pontryagin form of
V
. A trivialization of the Chern–Simons 2-gerbe associated with
V
is in particular a choice of coboundary for
\frac{p_{1}}{2}(\nabla^{V})
. In the constructions below, the only information we will use from part (3) of Definition
3.5
is the 3-form
H\in\Omega^{3}(X)
with
dH=p_{1}(\nabla^{X/M})\in\Omega^{4}(M;{\mathbb{C}})
determined by the image of the integral trivialization of the degree 4 cocycle
\frac{p_{1}}{2}(\nabla^{X/M})\in Z^{4}(X;{\mathbb{Z}})
.

Definition 3.7
.

The
Witten form
of a family of Riemannian spin manifolds is

(105)

\displaystyle{\rm Wit}(\nabla^{X/M})=\exp\left(\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{u^{2k}{\sf Tr}(F^{2k})E_{2k}(q)}{2k(2\pi i)^{2k}}\right)\in\Omega^{\bullet}(X;{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}])

where
F
is the curvature of
\nabla^{X/M}
.

When
M={\rm pt}
, the above recovers Definition
2.8
. We emphasize that
E_{2}(q)
is not a modular form, see §
A.4
.
Hence, for a family
\pi\colon X\to M
that admits a string structure, the integral
[\int_{X/M}{\rm Wit}(\nabla^{X/M})]\in{\rm H}(M;{\rm MF})
determines a cohomology class with coefficients in modular forms. However, the differential form
\int_{X/M}{\rm Wit}(\nabla^{X/M})
need not be valued in modular forms. Instead we have the following.

Proposition 3.8
.

A choice of Riemannian string structure on the family
\pi\colon X\to M
uniquely specifies an odd differential form
\eta_{H}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(X;{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}])
with the property that

(106)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{W}}{\rm it}(\nabla^{X/M}):={\rm Wit}(\nabla^{X/M})+d\eta_{H}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(X;{\rm MF})\subset\Omega^{\bullet}(X;{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}])

is valued in modular forms. Integrating along the fibers
\pi\colon X\to M
,

\int_{X/M}{\mathbb{W}}{\rm it}(\nabla^{X/M})=\int_{X/M}{\rm Wit}(\nabla^{X/M})+d\int_{X/M}\eta_{H}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M;{\rm MF}),

therefore also yields a differential form valued in modular forms.

Proof.

We recall that
p_{1}(\nabla^{X/M})=-{\sf Tr}(F^{2})/8\pi^{2}
, and a geometric string structure specifies a 3-form
H
with
dH=p_{1}(\nabla^{X/M})\in\Omega^{4}(M;{\mathbb{C}})
, see Remark
3.6
. Then define

(107)

\displaystyle\eta_{H}=\left(H\cdot{\rm Wit}(\nabla^{X/M})\cdot\frac{e^{E_{2}p_{1}}-1}{p_{1}}\right)

where above
p_{1}=p_{1}(\nabla^{X/M})
and we use divisibility of
e^{E_{2}p_{1}}-1
by
p_{1}
. Then
