\eta_{H}
satisfies

d\eta_{H}=\left(e^{E_{2}p_{1}}{\rm Wit}(\nabla^{X/M})-{\rm Wit}(\nabla^{X/M})\right).

Since

(108)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{W}}{\rm it}(\nabla^{X/M}):=e^{E_{2}p_{1}}{\rm Wit}(\nabla^{X/M})=\exp\left(\sum_{k\geq 2}\frac{u^{2k}{\sf Tr}(F^{2k})E_{2k}(q)}{2k(2\pi i)^{2k}}\right)\in\Omega^{\bullet}(X;{\rm MF})

is a differential form valued in modular forms, the result follows. ∎

For future use, we also record a version of the families Witten form using the non-holomorphic (but modular) 2nd Eisenstein series
E_{2}^{*}(\tau,\bar{\tau})
, see (
477
). Define

(109)

\displaystyle{\rm Wit}^{*}(\nabla^{X/M})=\exp\left(\frac{u^{2}{\sf Tr}(F^{2})E^{*}_{2}(\tau,\bar{\tau})}{2(2\pi i)^{2k}}+\sum_{k\geq 2}\frac{u^{2k}{\sf Tr}(F^{2k})E_{2k}(\tau)}{2k(2\pi i)^{2k}}\right).

We have
{\rm Wit}^{*}(\nabla^{X/M})\in\Omega^{\bullet}(X;C^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})[u^{\pm 2}])^{{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})}
, where
{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
acts on
C^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})[u^{\pm 1}]
as

f(\tau,\bar{\tau})\mapsto f\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d},\frac{a\bar{\tau}+b}{c\bar{\tau}+d}\right),\qquad u\mapsto\frac{u}{c\tau+d}.

Hence,
{\rm Wit}^{*}(\nabla^{X/M})
is a differential form valued in a
C^{\infty}
-version of modular forms, see (
449
). It compares with the families Witten form (
105
) as

(110)

\displaystyle{\rm Wit}(\nabla^{X/M})=\exp\left(\frac{2\pi i}{\tau-\bar{\tau}}p_{1}(\nabla^{X/M})\right){\rm Wit}^{*}(\nabla^{X/M}),\quad p_{1}(\nabla^{X/M})=-\frac{u^{2}{\sf Tr}(F^{2})}{8\pi^{2}},

and
{\rm Wit}^{*}(\nabla^{X/M})
satisfies a formula analogous to (
106
) where

(111)

\displaystyle\eta_{H}=\left(H\cdot{\rm Wit}^{*}(\nabla^{X/M})\cdot\frac{e^{E_{2}^{*}p_{1}}-1}{p_{1}}\right),

where above
p_{1}=p_{1}(\nabla^{X/M})
.

Remark 3.9
.

An explicit formula for
\partial_{\bar{\tau}}{\rm Wit}^{*}(\nabla^{X/M})
is determined by (
478
). One can use this to extend
{\rm Wit}^{*}(\nabla^{X/M})
to an
{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
-invariant differential form valued in the differential graded algebra
(\Omega^{0,*}({\mathfrak{H}})[u^{\pm 1}],\bar{\partial})
, i.e.,
{\rm Wit}^{*}(\nabla^{X/M})
is part of the data of a derived global section over the stack
{\mathfrak{H}}/\!\!/{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
.

3.3.
The Bismut–Ramond superconnection

We recall some of the ingredients in Bismut’s families index theorem
[
Bis86
]
, see also §
D
below. For
\pi\colon X\to M
a family of Riemannian spin manifolds with principal spin bundle
P\to X
, define the spinor bundle over
X
as the associated bundle,

{\mathbb{S}}=P\times_{{\rm Spin}(n)}{\rm Cl}_{n}

which carries a fiberwise left action by the algebra bundle
{\rm Cl}(T(X/M))
and a (commuting) fiberwise right action by
{\rm Cl}_{n}
. This leads to an
M
-family of Dirac operators
\not{D}
acting on sections of
{\mathbb{S}}
. Since
\not{D}
graded commutes with the right action of
{\rm Cl}_{n}
, it commutes with the associated left action of
{\rm Cl}_{-n}={\rm Cl}_{n}^{\sf{op}}
. For
V\to X
a metrized real vector bundle with compatible connection
\nabla^{V}
, define the Fréchet vector bundle
\pi_{*}({\mathbb{S}}\otimes V)\to X
whose fiber at
x\in M
is the Fréchet space
\Gamma(({\mathbb{S}}\otimes V)|_{X_{x}})
where
X_{x}=\pi^{-1}(\{x\})
. The Dirac operator
\not{D}\otimes\nabla^{V}
defines an odd, Clifford-linear, self-adjoint endomorphism of
\pi_{*}({\mathbb{S}}\otimes V)\to M
. Let
{\mathbb{B}}^{V}
denote the
{\rm Cl}_{-n}
-linear Bismut superconnection adapted to
\not{D}\otimes\nabla^{V}
,

(112)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{B}}^{V}=\not{D}+\nabla^{\pi_{*}({\mathbb{S}}\otimes V)}-\frac{c(T)}{4}

where
\nabla^{\pi_{*}({\mathbb{S}}\otimes V)}
is a Clifford linear unitary connection on
\pi_{*}({\mathbb{S}}\otimes V)
, and
c(T)
is Clifford multiplication by the curvature 2-form
T
of the fiber bundle
\pi\colon X\to M
. Clifford multiplication by
T
uses the left action of
{\rm Cl}(T(X/M))
on the spinor bundle, which commutes with the right
{\rm Cl}_{n}
-action. We conclude that (
112
) commutes with the left
{\rm Cl}_{-n}
-action. A standard reference for the Bismut superconnection is
[
BGV92
, Ch. 10]
; the Clifford linear generalization is in
[
BE23a
, §7]
. Finally, we recall the
{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
-rescaling action

(113)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{B}}^{V}(t)=t^{1/2}\not{D}+\nabla^{\pi_{*}({\mathbb{S}}\otimes V)}-t^{-1/2}\frac{c(T)}{4},\qquad t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0},

that yields a 1-parameter family of superconnections whose behavior as
t\to 0
and
t\to\infty
controls Bismut’s families index theorem
[
Bis86
]
,
[
BGV92
, Ch. 10]
generalizing (
2.1
).

Definition 3.10
.

Given a family
\pi\colon M\to X
of Riemannian spin manifolds, define the
Bismut–Ramond superconnection
as the direct sum of
{\rm Cl}_{-n}
-linear superconnections

(114)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{A}}:={\mathbb{B}}^{W(T(X/M))}=\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}q^{k}{\mathbb{B}}^{W_{k}(T(X/M))}

defined on the Fréchet vector bundle over
X

(115)

\displaystyle\pi_{*}({\mathbb{S}}\otimes W(T(X/M)))=\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}q^{k}\pi_{*}({\mathbb{S}}\otimes W_{k}(T(X/M))).

We use the notation

{\mathcal{E}}:=\pi_{*}({\mathbb{S}}\otimes W(T(X/M)))\qquad{\mathcal{E}}_{k}:=\pi_{*}({\mathbb{S}}\otimes W_{k}(T(X/M)))

so that
{\mathcal{E}}=\bigoplus q^{k}{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
. Similarly, let
{\mathbb{A}}_{k}:={\mathbb{A}}|_{{\mathcal{E}}_{k}}={\mathbb{B}}^{W_{k}(T(X/M))}
denote the summand of the Bismut–Ramond superconnection on
{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
so
{\mathbb{A}}=\bigoplus_{k}{\mathbb{A}}_{k}
. Using the
{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
-rescaling action on Bismut superconnections (
113
)
we obtain the rescaled Bismut–Ramond superconnection,

(116)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{A}}(t)=\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}q^{k}{\mathbb{A}}_{k}(t),\qquad{\mathbb{A}}_{k}(t)=t^{1/2}\not{D}\otimes W_{k}(\nabla^{X/M})+\nabla^{{\mathcal{E}}_{k}}-t^{-1/2}\frac{c(T_{k})}{4}.

Definition 3.11
.

The
Chern character
of the Bismut–Ramond superconnection is

{\rm Ch}({\mathbb{A}})=\phi(q)^{n}\sum_{k\geq 0}q^{k}{\sf sTr}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{-n}}(e^{-u{\mathbb{A}}_{k}(u)^{2}})\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M;{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}])

where
\phi(q)
is defined in (
77
). We note
{\rm Ch}({\mathbb{A}})
has total degree
-n
(see Definition
D.12
).

Proposition 3.12
.

There is a uniquely specified differential form
\eta_{W}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(X;{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}])
,

(117)

\displaystyle\int_{X/M}{\rm Wit}(\nabla^{X/M})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Ch}({\mathbb{A}})+d\eta_{W}

that mediates between the Chern character of the Bismut–Ramond superconnection and the integral of the Witten form of the family
\pi\colon X\to M
.

Proof.

The identity (
88
) gives the equality of differential forms,

(118)

\displaystyle\phi(q)^{n}\sum_{k\geq 0}q^{k}\int_{X/M}\hat{A}(\nabla^{X/M}){\rm Ch}(\nabla^{W_{k}(T(X/M))})=\int_{X/M}\exp\left(\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{{\sf Tr}(F^{2k})E_{2k}(q)}{2k(2\pi i)^{2k}}\right).

We further have the transgression formula (see
[
BGV92
, Theorem 10.32]
when
n
is even and
[
BF86
, Theorem 2.10]
when
n
is odd)

\phi(q)^{n}{\sf sTr}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{-n}}(e^{-{\mathbb{A}}_{k}(s)^{2}})=\phi(q)^{n}\int_{X/M}\hat{A}(\nabla^{X/M}){\rm Ch}(\nabla^{W_{k}(T(X/M))})-d\int_{0}^{s}\alpha_{k}(t)dt,

that measures the change in the Chern character of the Bismut–Ramond superconnection under the rescaling (
116
), where

\alpha_{k}(s):=\phi(q)^{n}{\sf sTr}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{-n}}\left(\frac{d{\mathbb{A}}_{k}(s)}{ds}e^{-({\mathbb{A}}_{k}(s))^{2}}\right).

Hence if we define

(119)

\displaystyle\eta_{W}=\sum_{k\geq 0}q^{k}\int_{0}^{1}\alpha_{k}(t)dt

the result follows.
∎

3.4.
The families index of Dirac–Ramond operators with vector bundle kernels

Proposition 3.13
.

For each
k\geq 0
, suppose that the fiberwise kernels

{\mathcal{E}}_{k}^{0}:=\ker(\not{D}\otimes W_{k}(\nabla^{X/M})|_{M_{x}})\subset({\mathcal{E}}_{k})_{x}

have locally constant rank for
x\in M
. Let
p_{k}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{k}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{k}^{0}
denote the fiberwise orthogonal projection to the kernel. Then there is a uniquely determined differential form
\eta_{0}
satisfying

(120)

\displaystyle{\rm Ch}({\mathbb{A}})-\phi(q)^{n}\sum_{k\geq 0}q^{k}{\rm Ch}(p_{k}\nabla^{{\mathcal{E}}_{k}}p_{k})=d\eta_{0}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(X;{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!))

mediating between the Chern character of the superconnection and the power series of Chern characters of the kernel bundles.

Proof.

The argument follows from standard facts about Bismut superconnections; we review the key points. The spectral projection operators for a family of Dirac operators are smoothing
[
BGV92
, Proposition 9.10]
, and in particular the projections
p_{k}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{k}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{k}^{0}
are smoothing for each
k
. This implies that

(121)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{k}^{0}\to X,\qquad p_{k}\nabla^{{\mathcal{E}}_{k}}p_{k},

is a smooth vector bundle with smooth connection. Then using the 1-parameter family of rescaled superconnections (
116
), define

\alpha_{k}(s):=\phi(q)^{n}{\sf sTr}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{-n}}\left(\frac{d{\mathbb{A}}_{k}(s)}{ds}e^{-({\mathbb{A}}_{k}(s))^{2}}\right),\qquad\eta_{0}=\sum_{k\geq 0}q^{k}\int_{1}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}(t)dt.

In the limit
s\to\infty
, the Chern form of
{\mathbb{A}}_{k}(s)
approaches the Chern form of (
121
)
[
BGV92
, Theorem 9.19]
. Then (
120
) follows from general argument for Chern–Simons forms provided that the infinite integral converges; convergence follows form large
s
bounds on the
C^{l}
-norm of
\int_{1}^{s}\alpha(t)dt

[
BGV92
, Theorem 9.23]
.
∎

Proposition 3.14
.

Under the assumption of Proposition
3.13
, a family of Riemannian string manifolds
\pi\colon X\to M
uniquely determines a class
\sigma(X)\in{\rm KO}^{-n}_{\rm MF}(M)
.

Proof.

Since the kernels
{\mathcal{E}}_{k}^{0}
of the operators
\not{D}\otimes W_{k}(\nabla^{X/M})
are bundles of
{\rm Cl}_{-n}
-modules over
M
, they determine a class

(122)

\displaystyle\phi(q)^{n}\sum_{k\geq 0}q^{k}[{\mathcal{E}}_{k}^{0}]\in{\rm KO}^{-n}(M)(\!(q)\!)

by the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro construction
[
ABS64
]
. In fact, for each
k
the kernel bundle determines a map to the point-set model for the
{\rm KO}
-spectrum constructed in
[
HST10
, Theorem 7.1]
, see
[
BE23a
, §4]
. A differential form representative for the Chern character of (
122
) is given by
\phi(q)^{n}\sum_{k\geq 0}q^{k}{\rm Ch}(p_{k}\nabla^{{\mathcal{E}}_{k}}p_{k})
. On the other hand, the integral of the (modular) Witten form gives a cocycle

(123)

\displaystyle\int_{X/M}{\mathbb{W}}{\rm it}(\nabla^{X/M})\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M;{\rm MF}).

Finally, we obtain a homotopy between the Chern form of (
122
) and the
q
-expansion of (
123
),

(124)

\displaystyle\int_{X/M}{\mathbb{W}}{\rm it}(\nabla^{X/M})=\phi(q)^{n}\sum_{k\geq 0}q^{k}{\rm Ch}(p_{k}\nabla^{{\mathcal{E}}_{k}}p_{k})+d(\eta_{0}+\eta_{H}+\eta_{W})

using Propositions
3.8
,
3.12
and
3.13
. From the definition (
18
) of
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
as a homotopy pullback, the data (
122
), (
123
), and (
124
) determine a class
[\sigma(X)]\in{\rm KO}^{-n}_{\rm MF}(M)
.
∎

3.5.
The proof of Proposition
1.5

To remove the vector bundle kernel assumption in Proposition
3.13
, we apply the families Clifford index construction to the each of the superconnections
{\mathbb{A}}_{k}
; we refer to §
D.3
for a review. By standard properties of the Bimsut superconnection, the
{\mathbb{A}}_{k}
admit a smooth index bundle in the sense of Definition
D.14
(see Proposition
D.16
). Consequently, for each
k
we obtain an open cover

U_{k}^{\lambda}:=\{x\in X\mid\lambda\notin{\rm Spec}((\not{D}\otimes W_{k}(\nabla^{X/M}))^{2}_{x})\}\subset X

and for each
(\lambda,k)
there is a smooth, finite-rank vector bundle with connection over
U_{k}^{\lambda}

(125)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{k}^{<\lambda}:=\bigoplus_{\mu<\lambda}{\mathcal{E}}_{k}^{\mu},\qquad\nabla^{<\lambda}:=p^{\lambda}_{k}\circ{\mathbb{A}}^{[1]}|_{U_{\lambda}}\circ p^{\lambda}_{k},\quad p^{\lambda}_{k}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{k}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{k}^{<\lambda}

with fiberwise
{\rm Cl}_{-n}
-action. Above,
{\mathcal{E}}_{k}^{<\lambda}
is the (finite) direct sum (
566
) of
\mu
-eigenspaces of
(\not{D}\otimes W_{k}(\nabla^{X/M})^{2}
for
\mu<\lambda
, and
p^{\lambda}_{k}
is the projection (
567
).
Over each intersection
U_{k}^{\lambda}\bigcap U_{k}^{\lambda^{\prime}}
for
\lambda<\lambda^{\prime}
, there is a
{\rm Cl}_{-n}
-equivariant inclusion

{\mathcal{E}}_{k}^{<\lambda}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{E}}_{k}^{<\lambda^{\prime}}

and the restriction of
\not{D}\otimes W_{k}(\nabla^{X/M})
to the orthogonal complement
({\mathcal{E}}_{k}^{<\lambda})^{\perp}\subset{\mathcal{E}}_{k}^{<\lambda^{\prime}}
determines a
{\rm Cl}_{-1}
-action (
575
) generated by an odd operator
e_{k}^{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}
that commutes with the
{\rm Cl}_{-n}
-action. If we choose a locally finite subcover
\{U_{k}^{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{k}}
for a discrete subset
\Lambda_{k}\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
, the above determines a map from
X
to a model for the
-n
th space in the
{\rm KO}
-spectrum (
573
),

(126)

\displaystyle[\{U_{k}^{\lambda},{\mathcal{E}}_{k}^{<\lambda},e_{k}^{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{k}}]\colon M\to{\rm KO}^{-n}.

Different choices in this construction lead to a homotopic map (with specific homotopy). The class underlying the map (
126
) is the analytic index of the
M
-family of Dirac operators
\not{D}\otimes W_{k}(\nabla^{X/M})
.

Lemma 3.15
.

The data (
126
) determine a map
\sum_{k\geq 0}[{\rm Ind}_{\Lambda_{k}}({\mathbb{A}}_{k})]\colon M\to({\rm KO}(\!(q)\!))^{-n}
from
M
to a model for the
-n
th space in the spectrum
{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!)
. Different choices in this construction lead to a homotopic map with specific homotopy.

Proof.

For each
N
, define an open cover of
X
indexed by
\vec{\lambda}=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N})\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}^{N}

(127)

\displaystyle U^{\vec{\lambda}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{x\in X\mid\lambda_{k}\notin{\rm Spec}((\not{D}\otimes W_{k}(\nabla^{X/M})^{2}_{x})\}\subset X\ {\rm for}\ k\leq N\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle U_{k}^{\lambda_{1}}\bigcap\dots\bigcap U_{k}^{\lambda_{N}}

i.e.,
U^{\vec{\lambda}}
is a mutual refinement of the covers
\{U_{k}^{\lambda}\}
for
k\leq N
. For each
N
, the construction (
126
) gives a map

(128)

\displaystyle\left[\{U^{\vec{\lambda}},\bigoplus_{k=0}^{N}{\mathcal{E}}_{k}^{<\lambda_{k}},\bigoplus_{k=0}^{N}f_{k}^{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}\}_{\vec{\lambda}}\right]\colon X\to({\rm KO}\wedge\{1,q,\dots q^{N}\}_{+})^{-n}

i.e., a map to the
-n
th space in the
N
th spectrum in the homotopy colimit diagram (
91
). A refinement of an open cover determines a continuous functor between Čech categories (see Definition
D.6
); so by
[
Seg68
, Proposition 2.1]
the maps constructed above come equipped with canonical homotopy compatibilities for different values of
N
. Therefore, the maps (
128
) are part of the data of a map into the homotopy colimit diagram (
91
) and hence define a map into the spectrum
{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!)
. For different choices of locally finite cover
U^{\lambda}_{k}
, we obtain homotopies between maps (
573
) by Proposition
D.17
, which in turn give homotopies between maps to the homotopy colimit (
91
).
∎

Lemma 3.16
.

There is a uniquely determined coboundary between the Pontryagin character of the cocycle constructed in Lemma
3.15
and the differential form
\sum q^{k}{\rm Ch}({\mathbb{A}}_{k}(t))\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M;C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{>0})(\!(q)\!))
, viewing
t\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{>0})
as the standard coordinate function.

Proof.

By Lemma
D.18
, for each
(\lambda,k)
there are uniquely determined coboundaries,

(129)

\displaystyle d\eta_{k}^{\lambda}={\rm Ch}({\mathbb{A}}_{k}(t))|_{U_{\lambda}}-{\rm Ch}(\nabla^{<\lambda_{k}}).

The Pontryagin character of the cocycle constructed in Lemma
3.15
is a Čech–de Rham cocycle for each
N

(130)

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}^{N}q^{k}[U^{\vec{\lambda}},{\rm Ch}(\nabla^{<\lambda_{k}}),{\rm CS}(\nabla^{<\lambda_{k}},\nabla^{<\lambda^{\prime}_{k}})]\in C^{*}(\{U^{\vec{\lambda}}\},\Omega^{\bullet}(-;{{\mathbb{C}}[q]/q^{N+1}})).

with compatibilities between different
N
coming from refinements of the cover. For each
N
, restricting the coboundaries (
129
) along a refinement of covers gives a coboundary in the Čech–de Rham complex between (
130
) and
\sum_{k\geq 0}^{N}q^{k}{\rm Ch}({\mathbb{A}}_{k}(t))
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
1.5
.

Lemmas
3.15
and
3.16
give a cocycle representative of the families index
\sum_{k\geq 0}[{\rm Ind}(\not{D}\otimes W_{k}(\nabla^{X/M}))]\in{\rm KO}^{-n}(M)(\!(q)\!)
and a coboundary mediating between a cocycle representative of the Pontryagin character of the index and the Pontryagin character
{\rm Ch}({\mathbb{A}})\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M;{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}])
of the Bismut–Ramond superconnection. Then Proposition
3.8
and
3.12
give a homotopy between
{\rm Ch}({\mathbb{A}})
and
\int{\mathbb{W}}{\rm it}(\nabla^{X/M})
, where
\int{\mathbb{W}}{\rm it}(\nabla^{X/M})\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M;{\rm MF})
. Together this determines a continuous map
\sigma(X)\colon M\to{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}^{-n}
with underlying class
[\sigma(X)]\in{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}^{-n}(M)
. A priori this class depends on the choice of
\Lambda_{k}\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
for each
k
, but any two choices are homotopic with specified homotopy by Proposition
D.17
and standard properties of Chern–Simons forms.
∎

Proof of Corollary
1.7
.

In dimension
4k
, identifying the integral modular form
[\sigma(X)]\in{\rm KO}^{-4k}_{\rm MF}({\rm pt})\simeq{\rm MF}_{2k}^{\mathbb{Z}}
with the Witten genus follows from the classical argument in §
2.5
.

In dimensions
4k-1
, we compare the value of the analytic index in Proposition
1.5
with the invariant from
[
BN14
, Definition 3.1]
. Let
X
be a
(4k-1)
-dimensional string manifold. For degree reasons, we note that
\int_{X}{\mathbb{W}}{\rm it}(\nabla^{X})=0
and Chern character of the Dirac–Ramond operator and the index bundle is zero,

{\rm Ch}({\mathbb{A}})=\sum{\rm Ch}(\not{D}\otimes W_{j}(TX))=0\in\Omega^{-4k+1}({\rm pt};{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}])\simeq\{0\}.

Hence the class in
[\sigma(X)]\in{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}^{-(4k-1)}({\rm pt})
is determined by: (i) the index of the sequence of
{\rm Cl}_{-4k+1}
-linear Dirac operators
{\rm Ind}_{\Lambda_{j}}(\not{D}\otimes W_{j}(TX))\colon{\rm pt}\to{\rm KO}^{-4k+1}
, and (ii) a homotopy from the 0 differential form to itself determined by

\eta_{H}+\eta_{W}\in\Omega^{-4k}({\rm pt};{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}])\simeq{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)

in the notation of (
107
) and (
119
).

We perform a homotopy that trivializes the data (i) and modifies the data (ii). The index
{\rm Ker}(\not{D}\otimes W_{j}(TX))
is a
{\rm Cl}_{-4k+1}
-module, for which there necessarily exists an extension to
{\rm Cl}_{-4k+1}\otimes{\rm Cl}_{1}
-module structure
[
ABS64
]
. The generator of the
{\rm Cl}_{1}
-action provides a deformation
(\not{D}\otimes W_{j}(TX))(t):=\not{D}\otimes W_{j}(TX)+\delta_{j}(t)
for a family of smoothing operators
\delta_{j}(t)
where

\displaystyle\not{D}\otimes W_{j}(TX)+\delta_{j}(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\not{D}\otimes W_{j}(TX)&t<1/2\\
{\rm invertible}&t>1\end{array}\right.

This 1-parameter family of superconnections has an associated Chern–Simons form
\eta_{j}
satisfying
[
BGV92
, Theorem 9.26]

{\rm Ch}(\not{D}\otimes W_{j}(TX))=d\eta_{j}.

In Bunke’s framework
[
Bun09
]
, the deformation (
3.5
) is the data of a
taming
, and the form
\eta_{j}
has an explicit description as an integral
[
Bun09
, §1.2.7]
and
[
BN14
, 3.2]
.
The class
[\sigma(X)]\in{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}^{-4k+1}({\rm pt})
is then determined by the image under the quotient map

[\eta_{H}+\eta_{W}+\phi(q)^{n}\sum q^{j}\eta_{j}]\in{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}^{-4k+1}({\rm pt}),\quad{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)\to{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)/(\varepsilon{\mathbb{Z}}(\!(q)\!)+{\rm MF}_{2k})\simeq{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}^{-4k+1}({\rm pt}).

This recovers Bunke and Naumann’s formula
[
BN14
, Eq. 22]
for their analytic invariant of string manifolds.
∎

3.6.
The
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
index theorem

The index theorem looks to compare dashed arrows

(134)

{\rm MString}
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!)
{\rm H}_{\rm MF}
{\rm H}_{{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}]}.

\lrcorner

Such maps are characterized by string of
{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!)
and
{\rm H}_{\rm MF}
together with a homotopy witnessing their compatibility.
The following shows that the homotopy compatibility data is unique up to homotopy when it exists.

Lemma 3.17
.

There is an exact sequence

0\to[{\rm MString},{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}]\hookrightarrow[{\rm MString},{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!)]\times[{\rm MString},{\rm H}_{\rm MF}]\to[{\rm MString},{\rm H}_{{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)}]

of homotopy classes of maps of commutative ring spectra.

Proof.

General properties of a homotopy pullback provide the Mayer–Vietoris sequence,

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle[{\rm MString},\Sigma^{-1}{\rm H}_{{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}]}]\to[{\rm MString},{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}]

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow[{\rm MString},{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!)]\times[{\rm MString},{\rm H}_{\rm MF}]\twoheadrightarrow[{\rm MString},{\rm H}_{{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}]}]

But
{\rm MString}
is rationally even, so
[{\rm MString},\Sigma^{-1}{\rm H}_{{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}]}]=0
and the lemma follows.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.6
.

By Lemma
3.17
, maps
{\rm MString}\to{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
are homotopic if and only if their composite maps
{\rm MString}\to{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!)
and
{\rm MString}\to{\rm H}_{\rm MF}
are homotopic.
For the topological index (
5
), the composite map
{\rm MString}\to{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!)
sits in a homotopy commutative diagram of commutative ring spectra
[
AHR10
]

{\rm MString}
{\rm TMF}
{\rm MSpin}
{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!)
\sigma
forget
A-B-S
Miller

for the map
{\rm MSpin}\to{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!)
coming from the extension of the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro orientation whose map on coefficients is the Witten genus of a spin manifold. By the Atiyah–Singer index theorem, this is homotopic to the map corresponding to the analytic index in Proposition
1.5
. On the other hand, the map

(135)

\displaystyle{\rm MString}\to{\rm TMF}\to{\rm H}_{{\rm MF}}\to{\rm H}_{{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}]}.

is an orientation of a rational cohomology theory, and hence differs from the standard orientation
{\rm MString}\to{\rm MSO}\to{\rm H}_{{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}]}
by a Riemann–Roch factor gotten from the ratio of Thom classes, e.g., see
[
AHR10
, §3.3]
. This difference class is the invertible element

(136)

\displaystyle\exp\left(\sum_{k\geq 2}\frac{u^{2k}p_{k}E_{2k}(q)}{2k}\right)\in{\rm H}^{0}({\rm BString};{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}])

where
{\rm p}_{k}
are the pullbacks of the universal Pontryagin classes along the forgetful map
{\rm BString}\to{\rm BSO}
. This is the same class (
123
) associated with the analytic index, and so the composite maps
{\rm MString}\to{\rm H}_{\rm MF}
are homotopic. This completes the proof.
∎

3.7.
The
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
-Euler class

By general considerations, the string orientation (
5
) of
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
determines Euler classes for vector bundles
V\to M
with string structures,

(137)

\displaystyle{\rm Eu}(V)\in{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}^{{\rm dim}(V)}(M).

Below we construct a representative of
{\rm Eu}(V)
built from Clifford modules and differential forms, using known descriptions of Euler classes of string vector bundles in
{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!)
and in
{\rm H}_{\rm MF}
. The following is analogous to Definition
3.5
.

Definition 3.18
.

Given a real vector bundle
V\to M
with metric compatible connection
\nabla^{V}
a
geometric string structure on
(V,\nabla^{V})
is

(1)

a spin structure on
V
;

(2)

a trivialization of the Chern–Simons 2-gerbe with connection associated with the spin structure on
V
(see
[
Wal13
, Definition 1.1.5.]
).

Remark 3.19
.

Analogously to Remark
3.6
, the only geometric data we require below from the trivialization of the Chern–Simons 2-gerbe is the differential form
H\in\Omega^{3}(M)
that is the image of the integral trivialization of the cocycle
p_{1}(V)\in Z^{4}(M;{\mathbb{Z}})
.

A spin structure on an
n
-dimensional vector bundle
V\to M
determines a bundle of left
{\rm Cl}_{n}={\rm Cl}^{\sf{op}}_{-n}
-modules,

(138)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{S}}_{V}:=P\times_{{\rm Spin}_{n}}{\rm Cl}_{-n},\qquad[{\mathbb{S}}_{V}]\in{\rm KO}^{n}(M)

that represents the
{\rm KO}
-Euler class of
V
, e.g., see
[
ST04
, Remark 3.2.22]
,
[
FHT11
, §3.6]
or
[
Fre12
, 1.92]
.

Lemma 3.20
.

For a vector bundle
V\to M
with geometric string structure, the
{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!)
-Euler class associated with the string orientation is

(139)

\displaystyle\phi(q)^{-{\rm dim}(V)}[{\mathbb{S}}_{V}\otimes\bigotimes_{k\geq 0}\Lambda_{q^{k}}V]\in{\rm KO}^{n}(M)(\!(q)\!)

where

(140)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{q^{k}}V={\mathbb{C}}\oplus q^{k}\Lambda^{1}V\oplus q^{2k}\Lambda^{2}V\oplus\cdots

Proof.

Since the
{\rm MString}
-orientation of
{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!)
factors through
{\rm MSpin}
, the
{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!)
-Euler class for the string orientation differs from the standard Euler class (
138
) by a Riemann–Roch factor, i.e., a difference class in
{\rm KO}({\rm BSpin})(\!(q)\!)
. This difference class was computed by Miller
[
Mil89
]
, yielding (
139
).
∎

Remark 3.21
.

In contrast to (
75
), we note that the sum (
140
) is finite when
V
is finite-dimensional. It is related to Witten’s spinor bundle on loop space
[
Wit88a
, Equation 24]
in the same manner that the usual spinor bundle (
550
) is related to the Euler class in
{\rm KO}
-theory (
138
).

Similarly, the Euler class associated to the string orientation of
{\rm H}_{\rm MF}
differs from the standard Euler class by a Riemann–Roch factor.

Lemma 3.22
.

For a vector bundle
V\to M
with geometric string structure, the
{\rm H}_{\rm MF}
-Euler class associated with the string orientation is

(141)

\displaystyle[{\rm Pf}(F){\mathbb{W}}{\rm it}^{-1}(\nabla^{V})]\in{\rm H}^{{\rm dim}(V)}(M;{\rm MF})

where
{\rm Pf}(F)
the cocycle representative of the ordinary Euler class of
V
for the standard
{\rm MSO}
-orientation of
{\rm H}_{\rm MF}
, i.e.,
{\rm Pf}(F)
is the Pfaffian of the curvature of the connection on
V
.

Proof.

The proof is an easier version of the proof of Lemma
3.20
, e.g., see
[
BE21
, §3.3]
. The difference class is the modular Witten form,
{\mathbb{W}}{\rm it}^{-1}(\nabla^{V})
, see (
108
).
∎

Proposition 3.23
.

The
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
-Euler class associated with the string orientation (
5
) is determined by (
139
) and (
141
) together with a homotopy (constructed in the proof) that is unique up to higher homotopy.

Proof.

We extend the data (
139
) and (
141
) to a class in
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
by constructing a homotopy between differential forms in
\Omega^{\bullet}(M;{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}])
. The cocycle representing the class (
139
) has a Chern form determined by the geometric string structure on
V
: the vector bundle
{\mathbb{S}}_{V}\otimes\bigotimes_{k\geq 0}\Lambda_{q^{k}}V
inherits a connection from the connection on
V
. Using (
88
) and the splitting principle, this Chern form is

(142)

\displaystyle\phi(\tau)^{-{\rm dim}(V)}{\rm Ch}(\nabla^{{\mathbb{S}}_{V}\otimes\bigotimes_{k\geq 0}\Lambda_{q^{k}}V})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Pf}(F)\exp\left(-\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{{\sf Tr}(F^{2k})E_{2k}(q)}{(2\pi i)^{2k}(2k)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Pf}(F){\rm Wit}(\nabla^{V})^{-1}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M;{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)[u^{\pm 2}]).

The geometric string structure on
V
further specifies a coboundary mediating between the Chern form (
142
) and the differential form representative of the
q
-expansion of (
141
),

\displaystyle{\rm Pf}(F)({\mathbb{W}}{\rm it}^{-1}(\nabla^{V})-{\rm Wit}^{-1}(\nabla^{V}))={\rm Pf}(F)d\eta_{H},

(143)

\displaystyle\eta_{H}=\left(H\cdot{\rm Wit}(\nabla^{V})^{-1}\cdot\frac{-e^{E_{2}p_{1}}-1}{p_{1}}\right).

Hence, we find that the data (
139
), (
141
), and (
3.7
) determine a cocycle a class in
{\rm KO}^{{\rm dim}(V)}_{\rm MF}(M)
. By Lemma
3.17
, the homotopy (
3.7
) is unique up to higher homotopy, and so this class is necessarily
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
-Euler class (
137
) associated to the string orientation (
5
).
∎

4.
Super Euclidean geometry in dimension 2

4.1.
Super Euclidean geometries

Before specializing to the dimensions of interest, we collect some general facts about super Euclidean geometry in arbitrary super dimension. The following generalizes Thurston’s notion of
model geometry

[
Thu97
, Definition 3.8.1]
.

Definition 4.1

(
[
ST11
, §2.5]
)
.

A
rigid geometry
is the data of a triple
(G,{\mathbb{M}},{\mathbb{M}}^{c})
consisting of a supermanifold
{\mathbb{M}}
with a left action by a super Lie group
G
and a codimension 1 subsupermanifold
{\mathbb{M}}^{c}\subset{\mathbb{M}}
.

Definition 4.2

(
[
ST11
, Definitions 2.26, 2.28, 2.33, and 4.4]
)
.

An
S
-family of
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-supermanifolds
is a locally trivial fiber bundle
Y\to S
and an open cover
\{U_{i}\}
of
Y
with isomorphisms to open sub supermanifolds
f_{i}\colon U_{i}\xrightarrow{\sim}V_{i}\subset S\times{\mathbb{M}}
and transition data
g_{ij}\colon V_{i}\bigcap V_{j}\to G
compatible with the
f_{i}
via the
G
-action on
{\mathbb{M}}
and satisfying a cocycle condition.

An
isometry
between
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-supermanifolds is a map
\varphi\colon Y\to Y^{\prime}
over
S
that is locally given by the
G
-action on
{\mathbb{M}}
relative to the open covers
\{U_{i}\}
of
Y
and
\{U_{i}^{\prime}\}
of
Y^{\prime}
, and satisfying a compatibility condition with the transition data
g_{ij}
and
g_{ij}^{\prime}
.

A
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-pair
is a pair of bundles of supermanifolds
(Y^{c},Y)
over
S
with an inclusion
Y^{c}\subset Y
over
S
, where
Y
has a
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-structure with respect to a chart
\{U_{i}\}
, and
f_{i}({U_{i}\bigcap Y^{c}})\subset S\times{\mathbb{M}}^{c}\subset S\times{\mathbb{M}}
.
Isometries
between
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-pairs are isometries
\varphi\colon Y\to Y^{\prime}
such that
\varphi(Y^{c})=Y^{\prime c}\subset Y
.

Pulling back bundles along base changes
S^{\prime}\to S
, the category of
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-pairs determines a stack on the site of supermanifolds; see
[
ST11
, Definition 2.33]
and also
[
BBES22
, Remark A.23]
. Any
S
-family of
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-supermanifolds determines a
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-pair with
Y^{c}=\emptyset
, so that
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-supermanifolds form a full substack of the stack of
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-pairs.

The rigid geometries relevant to the Stolz–Teichner program are defined as follows; see also
[
Fre99
, Lecture 3]
for the (non-Wick rotated) super Poincaré analog.

Definition 4.3

(
[
ST11
, §4.2]
)
.

A
super Euclidean geometry
is

(G,{\mathbb{M}},{\mathbb{M}}^{c})=({\mathbb{E}}^{d|\delta}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(d),{\mathbb{R}}^{d|\delta},{\mathbb{R}}^{(d-1)|\delta}),\qquad d,\delta\in{\mathbb{N}}

where
{\mathbb{M}}^{c}={\mathbb{R}}^{(d-1)|\delta}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d|\delta}={\mathbb{M}}
is the standard inclusion and the super Lie group
{\mathbb{E}}^{d|\delta}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(d)
is constructed out of the data:

i.

\Delta
a complex spinor representation of
{\rm Spin}(d)
with dimension
{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Delta)=\delta
, and

ii.

\Gamma\colon\Delta\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\Delta\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
a
{\rm Spin}(d)
-equivariant, nodegenerate symmetric pairing for the action on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
through the double cover
{\rm Spin}(d)\to{\rm SO}(d)
.

From these data, define a super Lie group
{\mathbb{E}}^{d|\delta}
with underlying supermanifold
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times\Pi\Delta\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{d|\delta}
and multiplication

(144)

\displaystyle(v,\theta)\cdot(w,\eta)=(v+w+\Gamma(\theta,\eta),\theta+\eta),\qquad v,w\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}(S),\ \theta,\eta\in\Pi\Delta(S),

using the functor of points description of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d|\delta}
, see (
496
).
The semidirect product
{\mathbb{E}}^{d|\delta}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(d)
acts on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d|\delta}
(on the left) thereby determining a rigid geometry.

Remark 4.4
.

Throughout,
{\mathbb{E}}^{d|\delta}
will denote the super Euclidean translation group with multiplication (
144
) to distinguish from the supermanifold
{\mathbb{R}}^{d|\delta}
on which it acts. We caution that the notation
{\mathbb{E}}^{d|\delta}
conceals the (possibly obstructed) data
(\Delta,\Gamma)
. Indeed, given a super dimension
d|\delta
, there need not exist an associated super Euclidean geometry. Notably,
d|1
-Euclidean geometries exist precisely in dimensions
d=0,1,2
. Furthermore, if a super Euclidean geometry in a particular super dimension exists, it need not be unique, e.g., there are two possible
2|1
-Euclidean geometries, see Definition
4.14
below.

Example 4.5

(Euclidean geometry without supersymmetry)
.

Taking
\delta=0
in Definition
4.3
, the pairing
\Gamma
is no additional data. This leads to a Euclidean geometry
(G,{\mathbb{M}},{\mathbb{M}}^{c})\simeq({\mathbb{E}}^{d}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(d),{\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{R}}^{d-1})
for which Euclidean manifolds are
d
-manifolds with a flat metric and spin structure. This enhances Thurston’s definition of Euclidean manifold by adding a spin structure
[
Thu97
, Example 3.3.2 and §4.2]
. Just as in Thurston’s case, examples of Euclidean manifolds come from quotients of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
by a discrete subgroup of
{\mathbb{E}}^{d}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(d)
such that the associated quotient map is a covering space, or equivalently, the discrete subgroup of
{\mathbb{E}}^{d}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(d)
acts properly discontinuously. A Euclidean pair
Y^{c}\subset Y
is a flat
d
-manifold
Y
together with a totally geodesic codimension 1 submanifold
Y^{c}
. We provide explicit examples of Euclidean pairs for
d=2
in §
4.2
below.

Definition 4.6
.

For a fixed super Euclidean geometry and a smooth manifold
M
, define
d|\delta\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}(M)
as the stack on the site of supermanifolds whose objects are
S
-families of super Euclidean pairs
(Y^{c}\subset Y)
together with a map
\Phi\colon Y\to M
. Define morphisms in
d|\delta\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}(M)
as isometries
\varphi
of super Euclidean pairs with the property that the triangle commutes:

(145)

Y^{c}\subset Y
Y^{\prime}\supset Y^{\prime c}
M.
\varphi
\Phi
\Phi^{\prime}

We set
d|\delta\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}:=d|\delta\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}({\rm pt})
, and note that a smooth map
M\to N
determines a map of stacks
d|\delta\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}(M)\to d|\delta\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}(N)
over
d|\delta\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Suppose a super Euclidean geometry on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d|\delta}
has been specified. Then there is a faithful functor

(146)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\colon d|0\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}(M)\to d|\delta\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}(M).

In particular, a spin manifold with flat metric determines a super Euclidean manifold.

Proof.

This is an elaboration on
[
ST11
, Equation 4.14]
, where the functor (
146
) is called
superfication
. From Definition
4.3
, the inclusion of the reduced manifold
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d|\delta}
is equivariant with respect to the inclusion of the reduced Lie group

(147)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{d}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(d)\subset{\mathbb{E}}^{d|\delta}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(d).

Given a family
Y^{c}\subset Y\to S
of Euclidean pairs, let
{\rm Spin}(Y)\to S
be the principal
{\rm Spin}(d)
bundle of the vertical tangent bundle of
Y\to S
. Then define

\mathcal{S}(Y^{c}\subset Y):=(\Pi({\rm Spin}(Y)|_{Y^{c}}\times_{{\rm Spin}(d)}\Delta)\subset\Pi({\rm Spin}(Y)\times_{{\rm Spin}(d)}\Delta)),

where
\Pi
is the parity reversal functor (
495
) that sends a complex vector bundle
V\to N
over an ordinary manifold to the supermanifold
\Pi V
that regards the fibers of
V
as odd. The cover
\{U_{i}\}
defining the super Euclidean structure on
Y
gives a cover
\{\Pi({\rm Spin}(Y)|_{U_{i}}\times_{{\rm Spin}(d)}\Delta)\}
of
\mathcal{S}(Y)
with gluing data from
{\mathbb{E}}^{d}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(d)\subset{\mathbb{E}}^{d|\delta}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(d)
. Hence, one obtains a canonical super Euclidean structure on
\mathcal{S}(Y^{c}\subset Y)
. Furthermore, maps between Euclidean pairs
(Y^{c},Y)\to(Y^{\prime c},Y^{\prime})
determine maps of spin bundles, leading to maps between the resulting supermanifolds in the image of
\mathcal{S}
(using that
\Pi
is a functor). Working in a chart defining the Euclidean structure, one finds that these maps are indeed super Euclidean isometries. Finally, we take the map
\mathcal{S}(Y)\to M
given by the composition

\mathcal{S}(Y)=\Pi({\rm Spin}(Y)\times_{{\rm Spin}(d)}\Delta))\to Y\xrightarrow{\Phi}M

where the first arrow is induced by the projection and the second arrow is part of the data of an object in
d|0\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}(M)
. This map is compatible with super Euclidean isometries in the image of (
147
), and so
this completes the construction of
\mathcal{S}
. This functor is faithful precisely because (
147
) is an injection.
∎

Lemma 4.8
.

Given a
d|\delta
-Euclidean pair
Y^{c}\subset Y\to S
, the reduced family
Y^{c}_{\rm red}\subset Y_{\rm red}\to S_{\rm red}
has the structure of a
d
-dimensional Euclidean pair from Example
4.3
, i.e., the fibers of
Y_{\rm red}\to S_{\rm red}
are flat Riemannian spin manifolds and
Y^{c}_{\rm red}
is a (fiberwise) totally geodesic codimension 1 submanifold. This determines a map of stacks

d|\delta\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}(M)\to d|0\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}(M)

covering the reduction functor from supermanifolds to manifolds.

Proof.

The cover
\{U_{i}\}
defining the super Euclidean structure on
Y
gives a cover
\{(U_{i})_{\rm red}\}
of
Y_{\rm red}
with gluing data from
{\mathbb{E}}^{d}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(d)
. This defines an
S_{\rm red}
-family of Euclidean manifolds in the sense of Example
4.5
. Similarly, the reduced map of (
166
) is the standard inclusion
{\mathbb{R}}^{d-1}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. Hence, the reduced family of a super Euclidean pair is a family of Euclidean spin manifolds and a (fiberwise) totally geodesic codimension 1 submanifold.
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

The stack
d|\delta\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}(M)
has an involution that to a
d|\delta
-dimensional super Euclidean pair associates an isometry

(148)

\displaystyle{\rm fl}\colon(Y^{c},Y)\to(Y^{c},Y)

that restricts to the spin flip automorphism on the reduced Euclidean family.

Proof.

This is an elaboration on
[
ST11
, §2.6]
. Locally on
Y\to S
, the involution corresponds to the automorphism
-1\in{\rm Spin}(d)
acting on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d|\delta}
as

(149)

\displaystyle{\rm fl}(v,\theta)=(v,-\theta),\qquad(v,\theta)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d|\delta}(S).

The action of
-1
commutes with the action of
{\mathbb{E}}^{d|\delta}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(d)
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d|\delta}
. This implies that these local involutions glue to determine a global involution on
Y\to S
. Since the inclusion
{\mathbb{R}}^{(d-1)|\delta}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{d|\delta}
is preserved by the action of
-1\in{\rm Spin}(d)
, we get an involution (
148
) for every
d|\delta
-dimensional Euclidean pair. Finally, we observe that
-1\in{\rm Spin}(d)<{\mathbb{E}}^{d}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(d)
acts by the spin flip on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
(this is the trivial action on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, but flips the sheets of the spin double cover of the frame bundle), and hence acts by the spin flip on the reduced family.
∎

Next we observe that the super Euclidean isometry group carries an
{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
-action by homomorphisms determined by

(150)

\displaystyle\mu\cdot(v,\theta)=(\mu^{2}v,\mu\theta),\qquad\mu\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}(S),\ (v,\theta)\in{\mathbb{E}}^{d|\delta}(S).

From (
144
), this gives a homomorphism
{\sf rg}_{\mu}\colon{\mathbb{E}}^{d|\delta}\to{\mathbb{E}}^{d|\delta}
that commutes with the
{\rm Spin}(d)
-action. We further note that (
150
) determines a map
{\sf rg}_{\mu}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{d|\delta}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d|\delta}
that is equivariant relative to the homomorphism
{\mathbb{E}}^{d|\delta}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(\delta)\to{\mathbb{E}}^{d|\delta}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(\delta)
determined by (
150
).

Remark 4.10
.

The notation in (
150
) comes from the fact that the dilation action induces the renormalization group flow on field theories.

Lemma 4.11
.

The stack
d|\delta\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}(M)
has an
{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
-family of endofunctors

(151)

\displaystyle{\sf RG}_{\mu}\colon d|\delta\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}(M)\to d|\delta\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}(M),\qquad{\sf RG}_{\mu}\circ{\sf RG}_{\mu^{\prime}}\simeq{\sf RG}_{\mu\mu^{\prime}},\quad\mu,\mu^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}

determined by applying the dilation (
150
) to the charts
U_{i}\subset S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d|\delta}
of a super Euclidean pair and the dilation action (
150
) to a super Euclidean isometry.

Proof.

The proof is entirely analogous to that of Lemma
4.9
.
∎

Example 4.12
.

For the Euclidean geometry without supersymmetry from Example
4.5
, the functor
{\sf RG}_{\mu}
from Lemma
4.9
scales the metric on a flat Riemannian manifold by a global factor of
\mu^{2}
, where the square root
\mu
specifies a lift of this action to the spinor bundle.

4.2.
2-dimensional Euclidean geometry (no supersymmetry)

To gather intuition, we expand on the
d=2
case of Example
4.5
. This geometry is modeled on the flat Riemannian spin manifold
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
with isometry group
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2)
acting by translations and rotations. Euclidean pairs are therefore flat 2-manifolds with spin structure and an embedded geodesic.

Euclidean spin tori give examples of compact 2-dimensional Euclidean manifolds. Ignoring spin structures for the moment, every Euclidean torus has
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
as its universal cover and so is a quotient of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
by the action of a subgroup
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{E}}^{2}
of the Euclidean translation group. Let
{\sf Lat}\subset{\mathbb{E}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{E}}^{2}
denote the submanifold of positively-oriented generators of such
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
-subgroups, i.e., the open submanifold

(152)

\displaystyle{\sf Lat}:=\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in{\mathbb{E}}^{2}\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\mid\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\ {\rm are\ a\ positively\ oriented\ basis\ of\ }{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\}.

The vectors
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
generate a (based, oriented) lattice
\{n\ell_{1}+m\ell_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\mid(n,m)\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\}
, and we obtain a smooth fibration over
{\sf Lat}
whose fibers are the tori
T_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}={\mathbb{R}}^{2}/(\ell_{1}{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\ell_{2}{\mathbb{Z}})
. To equip these fibers with spin structures we require the data of a lift

{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
{\mathbb{E}}^{2}
{\mathbb{E}}^{2}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2)
\langle\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\rangle

to an action of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
that preserves the spin structure. A short computation (compare Lemma
4.26
below) reveals that there are 4 such possible lifts corresponding to choices of
\pm 1\in{\rm Spin}(2)
for each generator of the fundamental group

(153)

\displaystyle\langle(\ell_{1},\pm 1),(\ell_{2},\pm 1)\rangle\colon{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{E}}^{2}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2).

These 4 choices are commonly referred to as periodic-periodic for (+1,+1), periodic-antiperiodic for
(+1,-1)
, antiperiodic-periodic for
(-1,+1)
, and antiperiodic-antiperiodic for
(-1,-1)
. Consequently, the moduli stack of pointed Euclidean spin tori is

(154)

\displaystyle({\sf Lat}\coprod{\sf Lat}\coprod{\sf Lat}\coprod{\sf Lat})/\!\!/{\rm Spin}(2)\times{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}),

where
{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
changes the basis of the lattice, and
{\rm Spin}(2)
acts by rotation of lattices through the homomorphism
{\rm Spin}(2)\to{\rm SO}(2)
. The periodic-periodic spin structure is odd (i.e., nonbounding), corresponding to the trivial double cover of the frame bundle; this component is preserved by the
{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
-action. The remaining three choices are even (or bounding) spin structur; these are permuted by the
{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
-action. Hence, the stack (
154
) has two connected components. Removing the basepoint in (
154
) amounts to including the action of a torus on itself by translation, enlarging the automorphism groups in (
154
) by
{\mathbb{E}}^{2}/(\ell_{1}{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\ell_{2}{\mathbb{Z}})
. We refer to §
A.3
for an alternate description of the moduli stack of spin tori (
154
) involving the metaplectic double cover of
{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
.

Next we enhance the stack (
154
) to include maps from Euclidean tori to a smooth manifold
M
. First we observe that for any lattice there is a diffeomorphism,

(155)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}^{2}/{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathbb{R}}^{2}/(\ell_{1}{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\ell_{2}{\mathbb{Z}}),\qquad(x,y)\mapsto(\ell_{1}x,\ell_{2}y)

with the standard torus (i.e., for the unit square lattice
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\subset{\mathbb{E}}^{2}
). Hence, for the periodic-periodic spin structure we get a map of stacks

\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)/\!\!/({\mathbb{E}}^{2}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2)\times{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}))\to 2\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}(M),\quad(\phi,\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}^{2}/(\ell_{1}{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\ell_{2}{\mathbb{Z}})\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{2}/{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\xrightarrow{\phi}M,

where the mapping space

(156)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{2}(M):={\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}/{\mathbb{Z}}^{2},M)\times{\sf Lat}

is regarded as a sheaf (of sets). For the even spin structures, there are analogous maps where one either restricts to the subgroup of
{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
preserving a chosen spin structure, or considers the coproduct over the periodic-antiperiodic, antiperiodic-periodic, and antiperiodic-antiperiodic spin structures.

Remark 4.13
.

The isomorphism
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}
allows one to view a Euclidean 2-torus as a complex manifold. The orientation condition for the pair
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
in (
152
) translates to the ratio of generators having positive imaginary part

(157)

\displaystyle{\sf Lat}\simeq\{z_{1},z_{2}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}\mid{\rm im}(z_{2}/z_{1})>0\}.

The isomorphism
{\rm Spin}(2)\times{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\simeq U(1)\times{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
further identifies isometries of pointed Euclidean tori with isomorphisms between complex manifolds. In the complex setting, a spin structure is the same data as a square root of the canonical bundle
[
Ati71
]
. This canonical bundle is trivializable, and so describing a square root in terms of monodromy in
\{\pm 1\}
recovers the 4 choices of spin structure (
153
).

Next we describe
2
-dimensional Euclidean pairs given by an infinite cylinder with an embedded (closed) geodesic. These examples arise by taking quotients by
{\mathbb{Z}}
-actions on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
compatible with the standard inclusion
{\mathbb{R}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
. The stabilizer of the subspace
{\mathbb{R}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
is

(158)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{1}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/4\subset{\mathbb{E}}^{2}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2),

and the further subgroup
{\mathbb{E}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{Z}}/2\subset{\mathbb{E}}^{1}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/4
preserves the orientation on
{\mathbb{R}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
. For
(\ell,\pm 1)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times\{\pm 1\}\subset{\mathbb{E}}^{1}\times\{\pm 1\}
, let

(159)

\displaystyle{\rm S}_{\ell}^{\rm p}={\mathbb{R}}/(\ell,+1){\mathbb{Z}},\quad{\rm S}_{\ell}^{\rm a}={\mathbb{R}}/(\ell,-1){\mathbb{Z}},\quad{\rm C}_{\ell}^{\rm p}={\mathbb{R}}^{2}/(\ell,+1){\mathbb{Z}},\quad{\rm C}_{\ell}^{\rm a}={\mathbb{R}}^{2}/(\ell,-1){\mathbb{Z}},\quad

denote the circles of circumference
\ell
and the infinite cylinders of circumference
\ell
, with periodic or antiperiodic spin structure as indicated. For
\tau\in{\mathbb{E}}^{2}
, we obtain inclusions

(160)

\displaystyle i_{\tau}\colon{\rm S}_{\ell}^{{\rm p}/{\rm a}}={\mathbb{R}}/(\ell,\pm 1){\mathbb{Z}}\xhookrightarrow{i_{0}}{\mathbb{R}}^{2}/(\ell{\mathbb{Z}},\pm 1)\xrightarrow{{L}_{\tau}}{\mathbb{R}}^{2}/(\ell{\mathbb{Z}},\pm 1)={\rm C}_{\ell}^{{\rm p}/{\rm a}}

where
i_{0}
is determined by the standard inclusion
{\mathbb{R}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and the map
{L}_{\tau}
descends from the (left) translation action of
\tau\in{\mathbb{E}}^{2}
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
. We note that
i_{\tau}=i_{\tau^{\prime}}
if
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
have the same image in the quotient
{\mathbb{E}}^{2}/\ell{\mathbb{Z}}
. Hence, the data (
160
) determine a family of super Euclidean pairs parameterized by
(\pm 1,\ell,\tau)\in{\mathbb{Z}}/2\times({\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times{\mathbb{E}}^{2})/{\mathbb{Z}}
for the quotient by the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-action

(161)

\displaystyle n\cdot(\ell,\tau)=(\ell,n\ell+\tau),\qquad n\in{\mathbb{Z}},\ \ell\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0},\ \tau\in{\mathbb{E}}^{2}.

Analogously to (
155
) there are diffeomorphisms (that are not Euclidean isometries)

(162)

\displaystyle f_{\ell}\colon{\rm S}^{{\rm p}/{\rm a}}_{\ell}\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}},\qquad F_{\ell}\colon{\rm C}^{{\rm p}/{\rm a}}_{\ell}\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathbb{R}}^{2}/{\mathbb{Z}}

between the circle and cylinder of circumference
\ell
with the (standard) circle and cylinder of circumference 1. Hence for each spin structure on the circle we get a map of stacks

{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}/{\mathbb{Z}},M)\times({\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times{\mathbb{E}}^{2})/\ell{\mathbb{Z}}\to 2\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}(M),\qquad(\phi,\ell,\tau)\mapsto({\rm S}^{{\rm p}/{\rm a}}_{\ell}\xhookrightarrow{i_{\tau}}{\rm C}^{{\rm p}/{\rm a}}_{\ell}\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{2}/{\mathbb{Z}}\xrightarrow{\phi}M),

sending an
S
-point of the source to an
S
-family of Euclidean pairs over
M
. The subgroup (
158
) acts through isometries on the resulting family of Euclidean pairs.

4.3.
2|1
-Euclidean geometry

Next we turn attention to super Euclidean geometry in dimension
2|1
. Unpacking the data from Definition
4.3
in this case,
\Delta={\mathbb{C}}
and the pairing
\Gamma
is a
{\rm Spin}(2)\simeq U(1)
-equivariant map

(163)

\displaystyle\Gamma\colon\Delta\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\Delta\simeq{\mathbb{C}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}_{2}\oplus{\mathbb{C}}_{-2}.

In the target above, we have decomposed
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}
into irreducible representations of
U(1)
of weight
2
and
-2
. Therefore, we have two possible choices of
U(1)
-action on
\Delta
that makes (
163
) an equivariant map corresponding to
\Delta={\mathbb{C}}_{\pm 1}
, the weight
+1
and
-1
representations. With this choice fixed,
\Gamma
is the inclusion of
{\mathbb{C}}_{\pm 1}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\pm 1}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}_{\pm 2}
as a summand.

Definition 4.14

(
2|1
-dimensional Euclidean geometries)
.

Let
{\mathbb{E}}^{2|(0,1)}
denote the super Lie group associated to the pairing (
163
) with the choice
\Delta={\mathbb{C}}_{-1}
. Then
{\mathbb{E}}^{2|(0,1)}
has underlying supermanifold
{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}
and multiplication (using the functor of points description (
498
) of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}
)

(164)

\displaystyle(z,\bar{z},\theta)\cdot(z^{\prime},\bar{z}^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})=(z+z^{\prime},\bar{z}+\bar{z}^{\prime}+\theta\theta^{\prime},\theta+\theta^{\prime}),\quad(z,\bar{z},\theta),(z^{\prime},\bar{z}^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}(S).

Similarly, let
{\mathbb{E}}^{2|(1,0)}
denote the super Lie group associated to the pairing (
163
) where we choose
\Delta={\mathbb{C}}_{1}
. This results in the multiplication

(165)

\displaystyle(z,\bar{z},\theta)\cdot(z^{\prime},\bar{z}^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})=(z+z^{\prime}+\theta\theta^{\prime},\bar{z}+\bar{z}^{\prime},\theta+\theta^{\prime}),\quad(z,\bar{z},\theta),(z^{\prime},\bar{z}^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}(S).

Consider the semidirect products
{\mathbb{E}}^{2|(0,1)}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2)
and
{\mathbb{E}}^{2|(1,0)}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2)
defined by the action (using the functor of description (
499
) of
{\rm Spin}(2)
)

(u,\bar{u})\cdot(z,\bar{z},\theta)=(u^{2}z,\bar{u}^{2}\bar{z},\bar{u}\theta),\qquad(u,\bar{u})\in{\rm Spin}(2)(S).

The above defines a pair of
2|1
-dimensional super Euclidean geometries

(G,{\mathbb{M}},{\mathbb{M}}^{c})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}({\mathbb{E}}^{2|(0,1)}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2),{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1},{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1})\\
({\mathbb{E}}^{2|(1,0)}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2),{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1},{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1})\end{array}\right.

for the codimension 1 sub supermanifold given by the map

(166)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1},\quad(t,\theta)\mapsto(t,t,\theta)=(z,\bar{z},\theta),\quad(t,\theta)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}(S),\ (z,\bar{z},\theta)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}(S).

The two choices in
2|1
-Euclidean geometry correspond to the two possible chiralities for supersymmetry in dimension 2, often referred to as
\mathcal{N}=(1,0)
or
\mathcal{N}=(0,1)
supersymmetry. Our primary focus below is on the
2|1
-Euclidean geometry determined by (
164
), and (following
[
ST04
, §4.3]
) eventually we will adopt the notation
{\mathbb{E}}^{2|1}:={\mathbb{E}}^{2|(0,1)}
when this choice is clear. However, for the moment we explain some interconnections between the pair of super Euclidean geometries in Definition
4.14
related to complex conjugation and orientation reversal of spin manifolds; ultimately, this is the structure required to define reflection positivity for
2|1
-Euclidean field theories in the sense of §
C.4
.

Lemma 4.15
.

The map of supermanifolds

(167)

\displaystyle{\sf or}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1},\qquad{\sf or}(z,\bar{z},\theta)=(\bar{z},z,\theta)

is equivariant for the action of super Euclidean groups relative to the isomorphism

(168)

\displaystyle{\sf or}\times{\sf inv}\colon{\mathbb{E}}^{2|(0,1)}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2)\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathbb{E}}^{2|(1,0)}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2)

determined by the product of (
167
) and the inversion isomorphism (using
{\rm Spin}(2)
is abelian)

(169)

\displaystyle{\sf inv}\colon{\rm Spin}(2)\to{\rm Spin}(2),\qquad(u,\bar{u})\mapsto(\bar{u},u).

Furthermore, (
167
) reverses the orientation on the reduced manifold
({\mathbb{R}}^{2|1})_{\rm red}={\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, and restricts to the identity on the subspace
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}
.

Proof.

Directly from the definitions we see that
{\sf or}
determines an isomorphism of super Lie groups
{\mathbb{E}}^{2|(0,1)}\simeq{\mathbb{E}}^{2|(1,0)}
. Since precomposition with inversion exchanges the representations
{\mathbb{C}}_{+2}
and
{\mathbb{C}}_{-2}
of
{\rm Spin}(2)\simeq U(1)
, one obtains the claimed extension to a map of semidirect products (
168
). In terms of real coordinates (
497
) on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}
, the map (
167
) is
(x,y)\mapsto(x,-y)
, and so reverses the orientation on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
. Finally, the definition (
166
) of the inclusion
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}
demonstrates that
{\sf or}|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}}={{\rm id}}
.
∎

We refer to Definition
B.10
for conjugation and real structures on supermanifolds.

Lemma 4.16
.

There is an isomorphism of supermanifolds

(170)

\displaystyle{\sf c}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}\xrightarrow{\sim}\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2|1}

that is equivariant for actions relative to an isomorphism

(171)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{2|(0,1)}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2)\xrightarrow{\sim}\overline{{\mathbb{E}}^{2|(1,0)}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2)},

where both (
170
) and (
171
) are determined by the linear map
(\overline{\phantom{A}})\colon{\mathbb{C}}_{-2}\to\overline{{\mathbb{C}}}_{+2}
gotten from complex conjugation. Furthermore, (
170
) restricts to the identity on the reduced manifold
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}
, and restricts to a map
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\to\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}^{1|1}\subset\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2|1}
between subspaces.

Proof.

Complex conjugation determines an isomorphism between the
{\rm Spin}(2)
-equivariant vector bundles
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{C}}_{-2}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times\overline{{\mathbb{C}}}_{+2}
over
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
. Applying the functor
\Pi
from (
495
), one obtains the map (
170
) of supermanifolds whose induced map on reduced manifolds is the identity. From the definition of the super Euclidean group (using that the options in (
163
) are conjguate), we obtain a homomorphism
{\mathbb{E}}^{2|(0,1)}\xrightarrow{\sim}\overline{{\mathbb{E}}^{2|(1,0)}}
. Finally,
{\rm Spin}(2)
-equivariance of the map of vector bundles gives the extension (
171
) to semidirect products, where we use the canonical real structure on
{\rm Spin}(2)
.
∎

Remark 4.17
.

A
real structure
for a Euclidean geometry is an isomorphism
{\mathbb{R}}^{d|\delta}\to\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{d|\delta}
that is equivariant for an isomorphism of super Lie groups
{\mathbb{E}}^{d|\delta}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(\delta)\to\overline{{\mathbb{E}}^{d|\delta}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(\delta)}
. Such a real structure exists for the
1|1
-Euclidean geometry, see
[
HST10
, Example 6.20]
. Lemma
4.16
is not a real structure for the
2|1
-Euclidean geometry, as the isomorphism of super Lie groups (
171
) changes the chirality of the supersymmetry. Because of this chiral supersymmetry, the
2|1
-Euclidean geometry does not admit a real structure.

Proposition 4.18
.

The maps in Lemma
4.15
determine a morphism of stacks

(172)

\displaystyle{\sf Or}\colon 2|(0,1)\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}(M)\to 2|(1,0)\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}(M),

that on reduced families of 2-dimensional Euclidean manifolds reverses the fiberwise orientations.
The maps in Lemma
4.16
determine a morphism of stacks

(173)

\displaystyle{\sf C}\colon 2|(1,0)\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}(M)\to 2|(0,1)\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}(M),

covering the complex conjugation functor on supermanifolds, i.e., an
S
-family is sent to an
\overline{S}
-family.

Proof.

Applying
{\sf or}
to the charts of a family
Y\to S
with
({\mathbb{E}}^{2|(0,1)\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2)},{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1})
-structure, Lemma
4.15
constructs a
({\mathbb{E}}^{2|(0,1)\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2)},{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1})
-structure. Furthermore, since
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}
is preserved by
{\sf or}
, this construction sends a
({\mathbb{E}}^{2|(0,1)\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2)},{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1})
-pair
(Y^{c}\subset Y)
over
S
to a
({\mathbb{E}}^{2|(1,0)\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2)},{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1})
-pair over
S
. These constructions change the geometric structures but not the underlying family of supermanifolds; in particular, a map
\Phi\colon Y\to M
determines a map for either geometric structure, and hence we have constructed the functor (
172
) on objects. The values on morphisms are determined by the homomorphism (
168
), using that (
167
) is equviariant with respect to this homomorphism.

For the second statement, we start with a general observation: for a
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-family
Y\to S
, the conjugate family
\overline{Y}\to\overline{S}
has a canonical
(\overline{G},\overline{{\mathbb{M}}})
structure. Hence, for a family
Y\to S
with
({\mathbb{E}}^{2|(1,0)}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2),{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1})
-structure, the family
\overline{Y}\to\overline{S}
automatically has a
(\overline{{\mathbb{E}}^{2|(1,0)}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2)},\overline{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}})
-structure. Using Lemma
4.16
we endow
\overline{Y}\to\overline{S}
with a
({\mathbb{E}}^{2|(0,1)}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2),{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1})
-structure. As in the previous case, since
{\sf c}
sends the subspace
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
to the subspace
\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}^{1|1}
, this extends to a map on pairs, i.e.,
(\overline{Y}^{c}\subset\overline{Y})
has the canonical structure of a super Euclidean pair. Finally, we use the canonical real structure on
M
(as an ordinary manifold) to obtain a map
\overline{Y}\to M
as

\overline{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{\Phi}}}{{\to}}\overline{M}\simeq M
.
This defines the functor (
173
) on objects. The values on morphisms are determined by the homomorphism (
171
), using that (
170
) is equviariant.
∎

Definition 4.19
.

The
reflection
functor on
2|1
-Euclidean supermanifolds is the composition

(174)

\displaystyle 2|(0,1)\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}(M)\xrightarrow{{\sf Or}}2|(1,0)\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}(M)\xrightarrow{{\sf C}}2|(0,1)\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}(M)

which is a map of stacks covering complex conjugation on the category of supermanifolds.

Lemma 4.20
.

The reflection functor generates a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-action on the stack
2|(0,1)\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}(M)
.

Proof.

From (
167
) and (
170
), we observe that
\overline{({\sf c}\circ{\sf or})}\circ({\sf c}\circ{\sf or})={{\rm id}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}}
. Hence, the identity map on families of super Euclidean pairs supplies the necessary 2-morphism to define a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-action on the stack, and the claim follows.
∎

Finally, we observe some connections between
2|1
-dimensional Euclidean geometry and
1|1
-dimensional Euclidean geometry (e.g., as studied in
[
HST10
, §6]
). Define
{\mathbb{E}}^{1|1}
as the supermanifold
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
endowed with the multiplication

(t,\theta)\cdot(s,\eta)=(t+s+i\theta\eta,\theta+\eta),\qquad(t,\theta),(s,\eta)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}(S).

Lemma 4.21
.

There are exact sequences of super Lie groups

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ccccccccc}1&\to&{\mathbb{E}}&\to&{\mathbb{E}}^{2|(0,1)}&\to&{\mathbb{E}}^{1|1}&\to&1,\\
&&t&\mapsto&(t,t,0),\ (z,\bar{z},\theta)&\mapsto&(2{\rm im}(z),\theta)\end{array}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ccccccccc}1&\to&{\mathbb{E}}&\to&{\mathbb{E}}^{2|(1,0)}&\to&{\mathbb{E}}^{1|1}&\to&1,\\
&&t&\mapsto&(t,t,0),\ (z,\bar{z},\theta)&\mapsto&(-2{\rm im}(z),\theta)\end{array}

where the indicated formulas are understood using the functor of points, and we have used the (slightly abusive) notation

{\rm im}(z):=\frac{z-\bar{z}}{2i}\in C^{\infty}(S),\qquad(z,\bar{z})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}(S).

Proof.

First observe that the maps on
S
-points satisfy the required reality conditions to give the claimed maps between supermanifolds. To see that these are furthermore maps of super Lie groups comes down to the equality

\left(\frac{z-\bar{z}}{i}+\frac{w-\bar{w}}{i}+i\theta\eta,\theta+\eta\right)=\left(\frac{z+w-(\bar{z}+\bar{w}+\theta\eta)}{i},\theta+\eta\right)

for the sequence involving
{\mathbb{E}}^{2|(0,1)}
, with a similar equality for the case involving
{\mathbb{E}}^{2|(1,0)}
. Exactness of the sequences is clear:
{\mathbb{E}}
is a normal subgroup (in fact, a central subgroup) with quotient
{\mathbb{E}}^{1|1}
.
∎

Lemma 4.22
.

The inclusion (
166
) is
{\mathbb{Z}}/4
-equivariant for the action by the 4th roots of unity
{\mathbb{Z}}/4\subset U(1)\simeq{\rm Spin}(2)
. Under the reflection functor, the generator
i\in U(1)
of this
{\mathbb{Z}}/4
-action is sent to
-i\in\overline{U(1)}
.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that the map (
168
) is inversion on
{\rm Spin}(2)\simeq U(1)
.
∎

Remark 4.23
.

The
{\mathbb{Z}}/4
-action on
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
in the previous lemma recovers the “pin generator” from
[
HST10
, Example 6.16]
, which is an important structure in unoriented
1|1
-Euclidean field theories.

4.4.
Supertori as examples of
2|1
-Euclidean supermanifolds

Definition 4.24
.

Let
{\sf Hom}({\mathbb{Z}}^{2},{\mathbb{E}}^{2|1}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2))
denote the sheaf of sets on the site of supermanifolds whose
S
-points are families of homomorphisms

\Lambda\colon S\times{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\to({\mathbb{E}}^{2|1}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2)).

Homomorphisms pull back along base changes, so that
{\sf Hom}({\mathbb{Z}}^{2},{\mathbb{E}}^{2|1}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2))
is a presheaf, and families of maps satisfy the gluing property equivalent to the sheaf property. Define a subsheaf

(181)

\displaystyle{\sf Hom}({\mathbb{Z}}^{2},{\mathbb{E}}^{2|1}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2))_{{\sf Lat}}\subset{\sf Hom}({\mathbb{Z}}^{2},{\mathbb{E}}^{2|1}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2))

whose
S
-points are families of injective homomorphisms that on reduced manifolds determine an oriented basis for
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
pointwise in
S_{\rm red}
, see (
152
). The natural
{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
-action on
{\sf Hom}({\mathbb{Z}}^{2},{\mathbb{E}}^{2|1}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2))
by precomposition restricts to an action on the subsheaf (
181
).

Remark 4.25
.

The pointwise basis condition is equivalent to the complex-valued functions

z_{1}\colon S_{\rm red}\times(1,0)\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2)\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}\quad{\rm and}\quad z_{2}\colon S_{\rm red}\times(1,0)\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2)\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}

having the property (
157
).

The condition defining the subsheaf (
181
) guarantees that the
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
-action on
S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}
determined by an
S
-point
\Lambda
of (
181
) is free, and hence the quotient is a supermanifold,

(182)

\displaystyle T_{\Lambda}:=(S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1})/\Lambda.

Furthermore, the cover
S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}\to T_{\Lambda}
determines a
2|1
-Euclidean structure on
T_{\Lambda}
. Hence, (
182
) determines a family of
2|1
-Euclidean supermanifolds parameterized by the subsheaf (
181
).

Lemma 4.26
.

The sheaf (
181
) is a coproduct of four components

(183)

\displaystyle{\sf Hom}({\mathbb{Z}}^{2},{\mathbb{E}}^{2|1}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2))_{{\sf Lat}}\simeq s{\sf Lat}\coprod s{\sf Lat}_{{\rm p}{\rm a}}\coprod s{\sf Lat}_{{\rm a}{\rm p}}\coprod s{\sf Lat}_{{\rm a}{\rm a}}

where each component is characterized by

C^{\infty}(s{\sf Lat})\simeq C^{\infty}({\sf Lat})[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]/(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}),\quad C^{\infty}(s{\sf Lat}_{{\rm p}{\rm a}})\simeq C^{\infty}({\sf Lat})[\lambda_{2}],

C^{\infty}(s{\sf Lat}_{{\rm p}{\rm a}})\simeq C^{\infty}({\sf Lat})[\lambda_{1}],\quad C^{\infty}(s{\sf Lat})\simeq C^{\infty}({\sf Lat}_{{\rm a}{\rm a}})[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]/(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}),

for odd functions
\lambda_{i}
to be described explicitly in the proof.

Proof.

To start, we establish notation that repackages the information of
\Lambda
an
S
-point of the subsheaf (
181
). Let

\displaystyle(\ell_{1},\bar{\ell}_{1},\lambda_{1},u_{1},\bar{u}_{1})\colon S\times(1,0)\to{\mathbb{E}}^{2|1}\rtimes U(1),

\displaystyle(\ell_{2},\bar{\ell}_{2},\lambda_{2},u_{2},\bar{u}_{2})\colon S\times(0,1)\to{\mathbb{E}}^{2|1}\rtimes U(1)

denote the restrictions of
\Lambda
to the generators of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
. For these
S
-points to determine an
S
-family of homomorphisms, one has the commutativity condition

(184)

\displaystyle(\ell_{1},\bar{\ell}_{1},\lambda_{1},u_{1},\bar{u}_{1})\cdot(\ell_{2},\bar{\ell}_{2},\lambda_{2},u_{2},\bar{u}_{2})=(\ell_{2},\bar{\ell}_{2},\lambda_{2},u_{2},\bar{u}_{2})\cdot(\ell_{1},\bar{\ell}_{1},\lambda_{1},u_{1},\bar{u}_{1}).

Together with the condition (
157
), one finds that
u_{1}^{2}=u_{2}^{2}=1
. Hence, we find four components corresponding to the four possible solutions

\displaystyle(u_{1},u_{2})=(1,1):\ s{\sf Lat},

\displaystyle(u_{1},u_{2})=(1,-1):\ s{\sf Lat}_{{\rm p}{\rm a}},

\displaystyle(u_{1},u_{2})=(-1,1):\ s{\sf Lat}_{{\rm a}{\rm p}},

\displaystyle(u_{1},u_{2})=(-1,-1):\ s{\sf Lat}_{{\rm a}{\rm a}},

proving the first statement in the lemma. For the second statement, one views each component above as determining a map

(\ell_{1},\bar{\ell}_{1},\lambda_{1},\ell_{2},\bar{\ell}_{2},\lambda_{2})\colon S\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}.

The condition (
157
) requires that on reduced manifolds the above determines an
S_{\rm red}
-point of
{\sf Lat}\subset{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{C}}\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}
. The commutativity condition (
184
) with the specified values of
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
imposes the further conditions

\displaystyle(u_{1},u_{2})=(1,1):\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}=\lambda_{2}\lambda_{1}

\displaystyle(u_{1},u_{2})=(1,-1):\ \lambda_{1}=0,

\displaystyle(u_{1},u_{2})=(-1,1):\ \lambda_{2}=0,

\displaystyle(u_{1},u_{2})=(-1,-1):\ \lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}.

Together, this gives the claimed description of functions.
∎

Remark 4.27
.

Lemma
4.26
shows that
s{\sf Lat}_{{\rm p}{\rm a}},s{\sf Lat}_{{\rm a}{\rm p}},
and
s{\sf Lat}_{{\rm a}{\rm a}}
are representable subsheaves of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}
with reduced manifolds
{\sf Lat}
. On the other hand,
s{\sf Lat}
is not representable as its functions are not the functions on any supermanifold. This makes the geometry of the periodic-periodic supertori more subtle.

Next we enhance the above to produce super Euclidean manifolds over a smooth manifold
M
. We will focus on the periodic-periodic supertori over
M
, though the other cases are analogous (and ultimately a bit easier). First we observe that for any periodic-periodic lattice there is an isomorphism of supermanifolds over
S

[
BE22
, Lemma 3.9]
,

S\times({\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}/{\mathbb{Z}}^{2})\xrightarrow{\sim}T_{\Lambda},\quad(x,y,\theta)\mapsto(\ell_{1}x+\ell_{2}y,x(\bar{\ell}_{1}+\lambda_{1}\theta)+y(\bar{\ell}_{2}+\lambda_{2}\theta),\theta+x\lambda_{1}+y\lambda_{2})

with the constant
S
-family with fiber the standard supertorus
{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}/{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
, determined by the canonical inclusions
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}
. This isomorphism between supertori over
S
is determined by the above
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
-equivariant map
S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}\to S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}
, and is typically not an isomorphism of Euclidean supertori over
S
.

Definition 4.28
.

Define the
super Euclidean double loop space
of
M
as the sheaf on supermanifolds

(185)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{2|1}(M):={\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}/{\mathbb{Z}}^{2},M)\times s{\sf Lat}.

Identifying an
S
-point
(\Lambda,\phi)\in(\mathcal{L}^{2|1}(M))(S)
with

\Phi\colon T_{\Lambda}\simeq S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}/{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\xrightarrow{\phi}M

gives a functor from
\mathcal{L}^{2|1}(M)\to 2|1\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}(M)
to the stack of
2|1
-Euclidean supermanifolds over
M
.

There is an action of
{\mathbb{E}}^{2|1}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2)\times{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
on
\mathcal{L}^{2|1}(M)
where
{\mathbb{E}}^{2|1}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2)
acts by isometries on supertori, and
{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
acts on
s{\sf Lat}
by changing the basis of a lattice. This action is computed explicitly in
[
BE22
, §3.3]
. By construction, there is a map of stacks
[
BE22
, Lemma 3.15]

\mathcal{L}^{2|1}(M)/\!\!/({\mathbb{E}}^{2|1}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2)\times{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}))\to 2|1\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}(M),\quad(\phi,\Lambda)\mapsto T_{\Lambda}\simeq S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}/{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\xrightarrow{\phi}M.

4.5.
Examples of
2|1
-Euclidean pairs

The most basic example of a
2|1
-Euclidean pair over
S
comes from the standard inclusion (
166
). Further examples arise from
{\mathbb{Z}}
-quotients of this basic one. We start by showing that the moduli space of
{\mathbb{Z}}
-actions involved is the same as in the case (
160
) without supersymmetry.

Lemma 4.29
.

Given an
S
-point
(\ell,\bar{\ell},\lambda,u,\bar{u})\in({\mathbb{E}}^{2|1}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2))(S)
, consider the left
{\mathbb{Z}}
-action generated by

(186)

\displaystyle S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}\xrightarrow{{L}_{\ell,\bar{\ell},\lambda,u,\bar{u}}}S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1},\qquad(z,\bar{z},\theta)\mapsto(u^{2}(\ell+z),\bar{u}^{2}(\bar{\ell}+\bar{z}+\lambda\theta),\bar{u}(\lambda+\theta)).

This
2|1
-Euclidean isometry preserves the subspace
S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\subset S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}
and is free if and only if the generator lies in the subgroup

(187)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{\times}\times{\mathbb{Z}}/2\subset{\mathbb{E}}^{2|1}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2).

Finally, the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-action generated by
(\ell,\pm 1)\in({\mathbb{R}}^{\times}\times{\mathbb{Z}}/2)(S)
is the same as the action generated by
(-\ell,\pm 1)
.

Proof.

The statement first requires the map (
186
) to restrict along
S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\subset S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}
, i.e., a dashed arrow making the diagram commute

S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}
S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}.
{L}_{\ell,\bar{\ell},\lambda,u,\bar{u}}

Using the formula (
166
) the horizontal inclusions, we require
(\ell,\bar{\ell},\lambda,u,\bar{u})
to lie in the subgroup
{\mathbb{E}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{Z}}/4\subset{\mathbb{E}}^{2|1}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2)
where
{\mathbb{Z}}/4\subset U(1)\simeq{\rm Spin}(2)
includes as the 4th roots of unity. Since the action is through the ordinary Lie group
{\mathbb{E}}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/4
, freeness of the action can be checked on reduced manifolds where the statement is clear.
∎

Given
(\tau,\bar{\tau},\eta)\in{\mathbb{E}}^{2|1}(S)
, define the inclusion
i_{\tau,\bar{\tau},\eta}
as the composition

(188)

\displaystyle i_{\tau,\bar{\tau},\eta}\colon S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\xhookrightarrow{i_{0}}S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}\xrightarrow{{L}_{\tau,\bar{\tau},\eta}}S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1},

where
i_{0}\colon S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\hookrightarrow S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}
is the standard inclusion, and
{L}_{\tau,\bar{\tau},\eta}
is the left translation action by
(\tau,\bar{\tau},\eta)\in{\mathbb{E}}^{2|1}(S)
.

Lemma 4.30
.

The map (
188
) descends to the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-quotient generated by
(\ell,+1)\in({\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times{\mathbb{Z}}/2)(S)\subset({\mathbb{E}}^{2|1}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(2))(S)
for all values of
(\tau,\bar{\tau},\eta)
. The map (
188
) descends to the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-quotient generated by
(\ell,-1)\in({\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times{\mathbb{Z}}/2)(S)
if and only if
\eta=0
.

Proof.

The statement amounts to checking commutativity of the diagram

S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}
S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}.
i_{\tau,\bar{\tau},\eta}
(\ell,\pm 1)\cdot
i_{\tau,\bar{\tau},\eta}
(\ell,\pm 1)\cdot

For an
S
-point
(t,\theta)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}(S)
, using (
166
) this is equivalent to

(t+\tau+\ell,t+\bar{\tau}+\eta\theta+\ell,\pm\eta\pm\theta)=(t+\ell+\tau,t+\ell+\bar{\tau}\pm\eta\theta,\eta\pm\theta).

The above requires
\pm\eta\pm\theta=\eta\pm\theta
, and the result follows.
∎

Following (
159
), for
(\ell,\pm 1)\in({\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times{\mathbb{Z}}/2)(S)
, define families of
supercircles
and (infinite)
supercylinders
of circumference
\ell

(189)

\displaystyle{\rm S}_{\ell}^{\rm p}=(S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1})/(\ell,+1){\mathbb{Z}},\quad{\rm S}_{\ell}^{\rm a}=(S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1})/(\ell,-1){\mathbb{Z}},

(190)

\displaystyle{\rm C}_{\ell}^{\rm p}=(S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}/(\ell,+1){\mathbb{Z}},\quad{\rm C}_{\ell}^{\rm a}=(S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1})/(\ell,-1){\mathbb{Z}},

with periodic or antiperiodic spin structure corresponding to the
{\rm p}
and
{\rm a}
, respectively. For
(\tau,\bar{\tau},\eta)\in{\mathbb{E}}^{2|1}(S)
, Lemma
4.30
yields inclusions denoted
i_{\tau,\bar{\tau},\eta}
(compare (
160
))

(191)

i_{\tau,\bar{\tau},\eta}\colon{\rm S}^{{\rm p}}_{\ell}=(S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1})/(\ell,+1){\mathbb{Z}}\xhookrightarrow{i_{0}}(S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1})/(\ell{\mathbb{Z}},+1)\xrightarrow{{L}_{\tau,\bar{\tau},\eta}}(S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1})/(\ell{\mathbb{Z}},+)={\rm C}^{{\rm p}}_{\ell}

(192)

i_{\tau,\bar{\tau},0}\colon{\rm S}^{{\rm a}}_{\ell}=(S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1})/(\ell,-1){\mathbb{Z}}\xhookrightarrow{i_{0}}(S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1})/(\ell{\mathbb{Z}},-1)\xrightarrow{{L}_{\tau,\bar{\tau},0}}(S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1})/(\ell{\mathbb{Z}},-)={\rm C}^{{\rm a}}_{\ell}

where
i_{0}
is determined by the standard inclusion (
166
) and
{L}_{\tau,\bar{\tau},\eta}
comes from the translation action of
(\tau,\bar{\tau},\eta)\in{\mathbb{E}}^{2|1}(S)
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}
. We note that
i_{\tau,\bar{\tau},\eta}=i_{\tau^{\prime},\bar{\tau}^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}
if
(\tau,\bar{\tau},\eta)
and
(\tau^{\prime},\bar{\tau}^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
have the same image in the quotient
(S\times{\mathbb{E}}^{2|1})/\ell{\mathbb{Z}}
. Hence, the data (
191
) determine a family of super Euclidean pairs parameterized by
(\ell,\tau)\in({\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times{\mathbb{E}}^{2|1})/{\mathbb{Z}}
, and (
192
) determines a family of super Euclidean pairs parameterized by
(\ell,\tau,\bar{\tau})\in({\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times{\mathbb{E}}^{2})/{\mathbb{Z}}
where in both cases the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-action defining the quotient is determined by

(193)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times{\mathbb{E}}^{2|1}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times{\mathbb{E}}^{2|1},\qquad n\cdot(\ell,\tau,\bar{\tau},\eta)=(\ell,n\ell+\tau,n\ell+\bar{\tau},\eta).

We can enhance the above to produce Euclidean manifolds over a smooth manifold
M
; we will describe the case for the periodic spin structure, though the antiperiodic construction is entirely analogous. Define standard versions of supercircles and supercylinders

{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}/{\mathbb{Z}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}/{\mathbb{Z}}

for the quotient by the action of the standard subgroup
{\mathbb{Z}}\subset{\mathbb{E}}^{1}\subset{\mathbb{E}}^{1|1}\subset{\mathbb{E}}^{2|1}
.

Lemma 4.31
.

For
\ell\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}(S)
, there are isomorphisms of supermanifolds over
S

({\rm S}^{\rm p}_{\ell}\subset{\rm C}^{\rm p}_{\ell})\simeq(S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}/{\mathbb{Z}}\subset S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}/{\mathbb{Z}}),

with standard families. These isomorphisms are typically not isomorphisms of super Euclidean pairs.

Proof.

We begin by establishing a little notation. Viewing
\ell\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}(S)\subset C^{\infty}(S)^{\rm ev}
as the pullback of the standard coordinate on
{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
along a map
S\to{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
, there is a function
\sqrt{\ell}\in C^{\infty}(S)^{\rm ev}
gotten from pulling back the (positive) square root of this coordinate. With this notation in place, define isomorphisms from the families (
191
) to constant families with fiber a standard supercylinder,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}F_{\ell}\colon{\rm C}^{\rm p}_{\ell}\xrightarrow{\sim}S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}/{\mathbb{Z}}\qquad{\rm via}\qquad(z,\bar{z},\theta)\mapsto(\ell^{-1}z,\ell^{-1}\bar{z},\ell^{-1/2}\theta).\end{array}

It is straightforward to check that the displayed map
S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}\to S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}
descends to the claimed isomorphisms between
{\mathbb{Z}}
-quotients in (
4.5
), and furthermore sends subspaces
{\rm S}^{\rm p}_{\ell}\subset{\rm C}^{\rm p}_{\ell}
to the corresponding standard supercircle. For the maps (
4.5
) to be
2|1
-Euclidean isometries, one must have
\ell=1
and
F_{\ell}={{\rm id}}
.
∎

Using the above, define a functor

\displaystyle{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}/{\mathbb{Z}},M)\times({\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times{\mathbb{E}}^{2|1})/{\mathbb{Z}}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle 2|1\hbox{-{\sf Euc}}(M),

(196)

\displaystyle(\phi,\ell,\tau,\bar{\tau},\eta)

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle({\rm S}^{\rm p}_{\ell}\xhookrightarrow{i_{\tau,\bar{\tau},\eta}}{\rm C}_{\ell}^{\rm p}\xrightarrow{F_{\ell}}S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}/{\mathbb{Z}}\xrightarrow{\phi}M)

that sends an
S
-point of the source to the
2|1
-Euclidean pair in (
191
) with the map to
M
given by the above composition, using the isomorphism (
4.5
).

5.
Supercylinders in a smooth manifold

5.1.
Warm-up: Euclidean cylinders in a manifold (without supersymmetry)

To start we will construct moduli spaces of Euclidean cylinders in a manifold
M
using the 2-dimensional Euclidean geometry in §
4.2
. The basic building block is the Euclidean pair (
160
), given by a circle embedded in a Euclidean cylinder with spin structure. Our focus will be on the periodic spin structure, so we will drop the
{\rm p}/{\rm a}
decoration, setting
{\rm S}_{\ell}:={\rm S}^{\rm p}_{\ell}
and
{\rm C}_{\ell}:={\rm C}^{\rm p}_{\ell}
below. Taking the standard identification
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}
, define the closed upper and lower half-spaces

(197)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{H}}_{+}:=\{\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}\mid{\rm im}(\tau)\geq 0\},\qquad{\mathbb{H}}_{-}:=\{\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}\mid{\rm im}(\tau)\leq 0\}

and the open upper and lower half-spaces,

(198)

\displaystyle{\mathring{{\mathbb{H}}}}_{+}:=\{\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}\mid{\rm im}(\tau)>0\},\qquad{\mathring{{\mathbb{H}}}}_{-}:=\{\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}\mid{\rm im}(\tau)<0\}.

Note we have the redundant notation
{\mathfrak{H}}={\mathring{{\mathbb{H}}}}_{+}
for the open upper half plane; we use
{\mathring{{\mathbb{H}}}}_{+}
when emphasizing its role as a subspace of
{\mathbb{H}}_{+}
.

Definition 5.1
.

A
positively-oriented Euclidean cylinder in
M
is given by data
(\Phi,\ell,\tau_{\sf in},\tau_{\sf out})
for
\ell\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
,
