are the standard complex coordinates.

Next suppose that the data (i)-(v) satisfy the properties

i.

The endormorphism
\bigoplus q^{k}e^{-({\mathbb{A}}_{\ell,k}(2{\rm im}(\tau))^{2}}
of
\Omega^{\bullet}(M;{\mathcal{E}})=\Omega^{\bullet}(M;\bigoplus{\mathcal{E}}_{k})
has a trace (i.e., is nuclear) and there an equality of differential forms

(435)

\displaystyle Z=\phi(\ell,\tau)^{-n}\sum q^{k}{\sf sTr}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{n}}(e^{-({\mathbb{A}}_{\ell,k}(2{\rm im}(\tau))^{2}}),

and

ii.

the superconnections
{\mathbb{A}}_{k,\ell}
admit index bundles in the sense of Definition
D.14
.

Then the above determines an object
E\in 2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
.

Proof.

The sequence of superconnections determines an infinitesimally generated degree
n
representation of
\mathscr{C}^{2|1}(M)
using (
430
). The hermitian inner product, self-adjointness, and real structures guarantee that this representation is reflection positive. The differential forms
(Z,Z_{\bar{z}},Z_{v})
and property (
435
) imply that the representation is also trace class. Finally, the existence of index bundles imply the cutoff condition. Hence, by Definition
1.21
we obtain an object
E\in 2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
.
∎

Let
V\to M
be a real vector bundle with geometric string structure. In §
3.7
we constructed the
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
-Euler class of
V
. We promote this to a construction of an object
{\rm Eu}(V)\in 2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
whose image under the cocycle map (
4
) is the
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
-Euler class of
V
. Using (
430
), we construct an infinitesimally generated degree
n
representation of
\mathscr{C}^{2|1}(M)
from 1-parameter family of superconnections determined by

(436)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}={\mathbb{S}}_{V}\otimes\bigotimes_{k\geq 0}\Lambda_{q^{k}}V,\qquad{\mathbb{A}}_{k}=\nabla_{k}+2\pi\ell H,\qquad\nabla_{k}=\nabla^{{\mathbb{S}}_{V}\otimes\bigotimes_{k\geq 0}\Lambda_{q^{k}}V}|_{{\mathcal{E}}_{k}}

where
H\in\Omega^{3}(M)\hookrightarrow\Omega^{3}(M;{\rm End}({\mathcal{E}}_{k}))
is part of the data of the geometric string structure. The
{\rm Cl}_{n}
-action on
{\mathcal{E}}
is inherited from the action on
{\mathbb{S}}_{V}
, see (
549
). This determines data (i)-(iv) in Proposition
9.10
.

From (
436
) we compute

(437)

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi(z)^{-{\rm dim}(V)}\sum_{k\geq 0}q^{k}{\sf sTr}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{n}}(e^{-\nabla_{k}^{2}+\frac{2\pi\ell}{2{\rm im}(\tau)}dH})={\rm Ch}(\nabla^{{\mathbb{S}}_{V}\otimes\bigotimes_{k\geq 0}\Lambda_{q^{k}}V})e^{\frac{p_{1}}{{\rm im}(z)}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Pf}(F){\rm Wit}(\nabla^{V})^{-1}e^{\frac{\pi p_{1}}{{\rm im}(z)}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Pf}(F){\rm Wit}^{*}(\nabla^{V})^{-1}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M;C^{\infty}({\mathbb{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{>0})[u^{\pm 1}])^{{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})}

using (
109
), (
142
), (
435
), where
z=\tau/\ell
. From this we find

(438)

\partial_{v}Z=0,\quad\partial_{\bar{z}}Z=\frac{ip_{1}/2}{{\rm im}(z)^{2}}{\rm Pf}(F){\rm Wit}(\nabla^{V})^{-1}e^{\frac{p_{1}}{{\rm im}(z)}}=d\left(\frac{iH/2}{{\rm im}(z)^{2}}{\rm Pf}(F){\rm Wit}(\nabla^{V})^{-1}e^{\frac{p_{1}}{{\rm im}(z)}}\right)

which supplies the data (v) in Proposition
9.10
,

Z_{v}=0,\qquad Z_{\bar{z}}=\frac{iH/2}{{\rm im}(\tau)^{2}}{\rm Pf}(F){\rm Wit}(\nabla^{V})^{-1}e^{\frac{p_{1}}{{\rm im}(\tau)}}

and these data satisfy property (i).

Definition 9.11
.

Define the object
{\rm Eu}(V)\in 2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
from the data (
436
) and (
438
).

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

To verify the construction of the object
{\rm Eu}(V)
, the discussion above provides the data (i)-(v) in Proposition
9.10
as well as property (i); it remains to verify property (ii). But this follows from the fact that the vector bundles
{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
are finite rank, and superconnection on finite rank bundles always admit index bundles by
[
BGV92
, Theorem 9.7]
. Using the translation between the modular and the holomorphic Witten class (
110
), we observe that
{\rm Eu}(V)\in 2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{{\rm dim}(V)}(M)
refines the
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
-Euler using the description of this class from §
3.7
and the construction of the cocycle map (
4
).
∎

Remark 9.12
.

To compare the object
{\rm Eu}(V)
above with Stolz and Teichner’s proposed
{\rm TMF}
-Euler class from
[
ST04
, Theorem 1.0.3]
, one must extend the
S^{1}\times{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{n}
-action on
{\mathcal{E}}
to a
S^{1}\rtimes{\rm Cl}(L{\mathbb{R}}^{n})
-action via (
375
), i.e., tensor
{\mathcal{E}}
with the invertible bimodule (
536
). The resulting bundle of
S^{1}\rtimes{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}(L{\mathbb{R}}^{n})
-modules on
M
recovers Stolz and Teichner’s stringor bundle restricted to the constant loops. The extension to nearly constant super loops encodes a version of the stringor connection.

9.6.
A partial construction of the supersymmetric
\sigma
-model

For a family of Riemannian string manifolds
\pi\colon X\to M
with
n
-dimensional fibers, we use Proposition
9.10
to provide the data of an object
\sigma(X)\in 2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{-n}(M)
. When
M={\rm pt}
we verify that this data satisfies the required properties to define such an object.

To begin, use (
430
) to define the infinitesimally generated degree
n
representation of
\mathscr{C}^{2|1}(M)
from 1-parameter family of superconnections determined by

(439)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}:=\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}q^{k}\pi_{*}({\mathbb{S}}\otimes W_{k}(T(X/M)))\to M,\qquad{\mathbb{A}}_{\ell,k}:={\mathbb{B}}(W_{k}(T(X/M)))(\ell)+2\pi\ell^{2}H

where
{\mathbb{B}}(W_{k}(T(X/M)))(\ell)
is the
\ell
-rescaled Bismut superconnection for the Dirac operator
\not{D}\otimes\nabla^{W_{k}(T(X/M))}
, and
{\mathbb{A}}_{\ell,k}
modifies this by the datum
H\in\Omega^{3}(X)
coming from a geometric string structure. This determines data (i)-(iv) in Proposition
9.10
.

We regard the normalized trace of the associated degree
n
representation of
\mathscr{C}^{2|1}(M)
as a formal power series,

(440)

\displaystyle Z=\phi(z)^{n}\sum_{k\geq 0}q^{k}{\sf sTr}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{-n}}(e^{-{\mathbb{A}}_{\ell,k}(2{\rm im}(\tau))^{2}})=\phi(z)^{n}\sum_{k\geq 0}q^{k}{\sf sTr}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{-n}}(e^{-{\mathbb{A}}_{k}(2{\rm vol})^{2}})

using that
2{\rm im}(z)\ell=2{\rm vol}
.

Lemma 9.13
.

The formal sum of traces (
440
) has the limit

\lim_{{\rm vol}\to 0}Z=\int_{X/M}{\rm Wit}^{*}(\nabla^{X/M})

and there are uniquely determined forms
\alpha_{k}(t)
satisfying

(441)

\displaystyle Z=\int_{X/M}{\rm Wit}^{*}(\nabla^{X/M})+d\left(\sum_{k\geq 0}q^{k}\int_{0}^{2{\rm vol}}\alpha_{k}(t)dt\right).

Proof.

This follows from Bismut’s local index theorem
[
BGV92
, Theorems 10.23 and 10.32]
, where the
\alpha_{k}(t)
are the Chern–Simons forms associated with rescaling superconnections. Specifically, we require the version of the local index theorem for vector bundles with superconnection
[
Kah11
, Theorems 5.33 and 5.41]
. Using this, the above formula follows from (
110
) and (
117
).
∎

Definition 9.14
.

Define the differential forms in
\Omega^{\bullet}(M;C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{>0})(\!(q)\!))
,

(442)

\displaystyle Z_{v}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}q^{k}\alpha_{k}(2{\rm vol})

(443)

\displaystyle Z_{\bar{z}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{X/M}\frac{-iH/2}{{\rm im}(z)^{2}}{\rm Wit}^{*}(\nabla^{X/M})+\partial_{\bar{z}}\left(\sum_{k\geq 0}q^{k}\int_{0}^{2{\rm vol}}\alpha_{k}(t)dt\right),

where
H
comes from the data of a geometric string structure.

Lemma 9.15
.

The forms (
442
) and (
443
) satisfy

\partial_{v}Z=dZ_{v},\quad\partial_{\bar{z}}Z=dZ_{\bar{z}}.

Proof.

This follows directly from (
441
).
∎

Proposition 9.16
.

Given a family
\pi\colon X\to M
of Riemannian string manifolds, the data (
439
), (
440
), (
442
) and (
443
) determine an object
\sigma(X)\in 2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{-n}(M)
if and only if
(Z,Z_{v},Z_{\bar{z}})
satisfy the
{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
-equivariance properties (
411
), (
413
), and (
414
).

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
9.10
: the required data are supplied as indicated, property (ii) follows from standard results for Bismut superconnections (see Proposition
D.16
), and so it remains to verify property (i).
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

The superconnections over
M={\rm pt}
are the twisted Dirac operators comprising the Dirac–Ramond operator (see Definition
2.6
) and we write
\not{D}_{k}={\mathbb{A}}_{k}
. Specializing the construction above to
M={\rm pt}
, we find that
Z
is independent of
{\rm vol}
by the McKean–Singer theorem
[
MS67
]
: the supertrace

\sum q^{k}{\sf sTr}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{-n}}(e^{-2{\rm im}(\tau)\ell\not{D}_{k}})=\sum q^{k}{\sf sTr}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{-n}}(e^{-2{\rm vol}\not{D}_{k}})

is independent of the parameter
{\rm vol}
and equal to Chern character of the formal sum of indicies. Hence,
Z_{v}=0
in this case and the second term in (
443
) also vanishes. The
{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
-equivariance properties for
Z
and
Z_{\bar{z}}
are clear, and so by Proposition
9.16
we obtain a class
\sigma(X)\in 2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{-n}({\rm pt})
. Agreement with the
{\rm KO}_{\rm MF}
-valued analytic index is clear from construction.
∎

Appendix A
Pfaffian lines and modular forms

Below we review the description of modular forms as sections of tensor powers of a Pfaffian line bundle. This fact is classical, e.g., see
[
Qui85
]
,
[
Fre87
, §4]
and
[
ST11
, §5]
. However, we require explicit formulas that do not seem to exist in the literature.

A.1.
Conventions for modular forms

Throughout,
\tau\in{\mathfrak{H}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
is the standard coordinate on the upper half plane.

Definition A.1
.

For
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, define the vector space of
weight
k
weak modular forms
as

{\rm MF}_{k}^{\omega}:=\left\{f\in\mathcal{O}({\mathfrak{H}})\mid f\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\right)=(c\tau+d)^{k}f(\tau),\ \left[\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right]\in{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\right\}.

The
ring of weak modular forms
is
{\rm MF}^{\omega}:=\bigoplus_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\rm MF}_{k}^{\omega}
with multiplication inherited from multiplication of functions on
{\mathfrak{H}}
.
A weak modular form is a
weakly holomorphic
if
f(\tau)
is meromorphic as
\tau\to i\infty
. Let
{\rm MF}=\bigoplus_{k}{\rm MF}_{k}
denote the ring of weakly holomorphic modular forms.

When regarding modular forms and weak modular forms as graded rings, the graded components are defined as

\displaystyle{\rm MF}^{k}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\rm MF}_{-k/2}&k\ {\rm even}\\
0&l\ {\rm odd}\end{array}\right.\qquad({\rm MF}^{\omega})^{k}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\rm MF}_{-k/2}^{\omega}&k\ {\rm even}\\
0&l\ {\rm odd}\end{array}\right.

which results in a graded-commutative product structure.

Remark A.2
.

An equivalent description of the graded ring
{\rm MF}^{\omega}
is the
{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
-invariants

(449)

\displaystyle{\rm MF}^{\omega}\simeq(\mathcal{O}({\mathfrak{H}})[u^{\pm 1}])^{{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})},\qquad|u|=-2

for the action by fractional linear transformations on
{\mathfrak{H}}
and
u\mapsto u/(c\tau+d)
.

A modular form can equivalently be expressed as a function of
q=e^{2\pi i\tau}
on the unit punctured disk in
{\mathbb{C}}
. Taylor expanding defines an injective ring homomorphism

(450)

\displaystyle q\hbox{-}{\rm expand}\colon{\rm MF}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!).

Let
{\rm MF}^{\mathbb{Z}}_{k}\subset{\rm MF}_{k}
denote the subgroup of
integral modular forms
of weight
k
, defined as having
q
-expansions in integral Laurent series,
{\mathbb{Z}}(\!(q)\!)\subset{\mathbb{C}}(\!(q)\!)
.

A.2.
The Pfaffian line over Euclidean spin tori

We recall the description of a Euclidean spin torus from Remark
4.13
in terms of a complex manifold
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
with a chosen square root of its canonical bundle. In this description, the chiral Dirac operator is the
\bar{\partial}
-operator twisted by the canonical bundle.

The chiral Dirac operator for the even spin structure is invertible (see §
4.2
for spin structures on the torus), and hence the Pfaffian line bundle is canonically trivialized by its
{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\times{\rm Spin}(2)
-invariant Pfaffian section.
3
3

3

This trivialization does not respect the Quillen metric: the norm of the Pfaffian section over tori with even spin structure is essentially given by the three Weber modular functions with appropriate normalizations; e.g., this can be seen by specializing
[
Fre87
, Proposition 4.10]
to real points in the Jacobian.

The chiral Dirac operator for the odd spin structure has a 1-dimensional kernel, and so the Pfaffian line bundle is the
{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\times{\rm Spin}(2)
-equivariant line bundle over
{\sf Lat}
whose fiber at
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\in{\sf Lat}
is the dual space of harmonic spinors. Since
{\rm Spin}(2)\simeq U(1)
acts on harmonic spinors through the weight 1 representation, the Pfaffian line
{\rm Pf}\to{\sf Lat}/\!\!/{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\times{\rm Spin}(2)
is classified by the map of stacks

(451)

\displaystyle{\sf Lat}/\!\!/{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\times{\rm Spin}(2)\to{\rm pt}/\!\!/U(1),\ \ {\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\times{\rm Spin}(2)\to{\rm Spin}(2)\simeq U(1)\xrightarrow{(-)^{-1}}U(1)

determined by the projection homomorphism, the identification
{\rm Spin}(2)\simeq U(1)
, and inversion on
U(1)
. The inversion comes from the fact that the Pfaffian line is
dual
to harmonic spinors. Tensor powers of the Pfaffian are then determined by the homomorphism

(452)

\displaystyle{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\times{\rm Spin}(2)\to{\rm Spin}(2)\simeq U(1)\xrightarrow{k}U(1)\iff{\rm Pf}^{\otimes-k}\to{\sf Lat}/\!\!/{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\times{\rm Spin}(2)

where the first arrow is the projection and the second arrow is the
k
-fold covering map,
z\mapsto z^{k}
for
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Global sections of
{\rm Pf}^{\otimes-k}
are therefore functions on
{\sf Lat}
that transform with weight
k
under the
U(1)\simeq{\rm Spin}(2)
-action and are invariant under the
{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
-action.

There is a line bundle
\overline{{\rm Pf}}^{\otimes-k}\to{\sf Lat}/\!\!/{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\times{\rm Spin}(2)
defined analogously to (
452
), but using the conjugate character
z\mapsto\overline{z}^{k}
of
U(1)
. The (positive) square root of the volume of a lattice determines a function

{\rm vol}^{-1/2}\in C^{\infty}({\sf Lat}),\qquad{\rm vol}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})=\frac{\ell_{1}\bar{\ell}_{2}-\bar{\ell}_{1}\ell_{2}}{2i}

that descends to a nonvanishing global section of
{\rm Pf}\otimes\overline{{\rm Pf}}
, i.e., a trivialization. Hence there is an isomorphism of line bundles

{\rm Pf}\simeq\overline{{\rm Pf}}^{\vee}=\overline{{\rm Pf}}^{\otimes-1}

over
{\sf Lat}/\!\!/{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\times{\rm Spin}(2)
determined by
{\rm vol}^{-1/2}
.

For future reference, we observe the functors

{\sf Lat}/\!\!/{\rm Spin}(2)\times{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\xrightarrow{\rm forget}{\sf Lat}/\!\!/{\rm SO}(2)\times{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\xrightarrow{{\rm vol}}{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}

where the first arrow forgets the odd spin structure (or equivalently, is determined by the double cover
{\rm Spin}(2)\to{\rm SO}(2)
), and the second takes the volume of a Euclidean torus. The fiber at
v\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
determines a
{\rm Spin}(2)\times{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
-invariant subspace of
{\sf Lat}
of lattices whose associated Euclidean spin tori have volume
v
.

A.3.
Sections of the Pfaffian line and modular forms

Next we explain how
{\rm Pf}^{\otimes k}
and its sections are related to modular forms.
Here it is useful to work with a different presentation of the stack of Euclidean tori with periodic-periodic spin structure. Let
{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
denote the metaplectic group, the nontrivial double cover of
{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
.
This group has the explicit description

{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\simeq\left\{\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right]\in{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}),\epsilon\in\mathcal{O}({\mathfrak{H}})^{\times}\mid\epsilon(z)^{2}=cz+d\right\}

where multiplication is determined by multiplication in
{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
, multiplication in
\mathcal{O}({\mathfrak{H}})^{\times}
, and the action of
{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
on
\mathcal{O}({\mathfrak{H}})^{\times}
by fractional linear transformations. Below we will use the notation
\sqrt{cz+d}
for the function
\epsilon(z)
when describing an element of
{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
. Define the quotient stack
{\mathfrak{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}/\!\!/{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
for the action

\left(\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right],\sqrt{cz+d}\right)\cdot(\tau,\ell)=\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d},\ell|c\tau+d|\right).

where we observe that this action factors through the homomorphism
{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\to{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
. The quotient stack
{\mathfrak{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}/\!\!/{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
supports line bundles indexed by
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
classified by functors
{\mathfrak{H}}/\!\!/{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\to{\rm pt}/\!\!/{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
determined by

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{H}}\times{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\times},\qquad\left(\tau,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right],\sqrt{cz+d}\right)\to(c\tau+d)^{k/2}.

Similarly, the maps

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{H}}\times{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\times},\qquad\left(\tau,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right],\sqrt{cz+d}\right)\to(c\overline{\tau}+d)^{l/2}.

determine conjugate line bundles indexed by
l\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Definition A.3
.

For
(k,l)\in{\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}
, let
{\rm mf}^{k,l}
denote the global sections of the tensor product of the line bundles (
A.3
) and (
A.3
). Hence, we have

(459)

{\rm mf}^{k,l}=\left\{f\in C^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{>0})\mid f\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d},\ell|c\tau+d|\right)=(c\tau+d)^{-k/2}(c\overline{\tau}+d)^{-l/2}f(\tau,\ell)\right\}

Lemma A.4
.

There is an equivalence of stacks

(460)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}/\!\!/{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\sf Lat}/\!\!/{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\times{\rm Spin}(2)

and the pullback of the lines
{\rm Pf}^{\otimes-k}
from (
452
) are isomorphic to the lines (
A.3
). Similarly, the pullback of
\overline{{\rm Pf}}^{\otimes-k}
are isomorphic to the lines (
A.3
). In particular, we obtain the isomorphism on global sections,

{\rm mf}^{k,l}\simeq\Gamma({\sf Lat}/\!\!/({\rm Spin}(2)\times{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}));{\rm Pf}^{\otimes k}\otimes\overline{{\rm Pf}}^{\otimes l}).

Proof.

Consider the functor between Lie groupoids defined by the maps

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\to{\sf Lat},

\displaystyle(\tau,\ell)\mapsto(\ell\tau,\ell)

and

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\times{\rm Spin}(2),

\displaystyle\left(\tau,\ell,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right],\sqrt{c\tau+d}\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\left(\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right],\frac{(c\tau+d)^{1/2}}{|c\tau+d|^{1/2}}\right).

A short calculation shows that these maps fit together to determine a fully faithful and essentially surjective functor (
460
), yielding the claimed equivalence of stacks.

To verify the isomorphism of line bundles, we view sections as smooth functions on
{\mathfrak{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
with transformation properties. Then multiplication by
\ell^{k/2}
provides the desired isomorphism between sections; indeed, for
f(\tau,\ell)\in C^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{>0})
, the transformation property for
\ell^{k/2}f(\tau,\ell)
to be a section of the pullback of
{\rm Pf}^{\otimes k}
along (
460
) is the equality (using (
452
))

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lll}\left(\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right],\sqrt{c\tau+d}\right)\cdot(\ell^{k/2}f(\tau,\ell))&=&(\ell|c\tau+d|)^{k/2}f\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d},\ell|c\tau+d|\right)\\
&=&\frac{|c\tau+d|^{k/2}}{(c\tau+d)^{k/2}}\ell^{k/2}f(\tau,\ell),\end{array}

which is equivalent to
f(\tau,\ell)
transforming with weight
-k/2
, via (
A.3
). The argument for
\overline{{\rm Pf}}{}^{\otimes k}
is analogous.
∎

The moduli stack of elliptic curves is presented by the complex analytic quotient stack
{\mathfrak{H}}/\!\!/{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
for the
{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
-action by fractional linear transformations. Define a line bundle
{\rm Mf}_{k}\to{\mathfrak{H}}/\!\!/{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
by the map

\displaystyle{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\to\mathcal{O}({\mathfrak{H}})^{\times},\qquad\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right]\to(c\tau+d)^{k}.

Holomorphic sections of
{\rm Mf}_{k}
are weight
k
weak modular forms, whence the notation.
The moduli stack of elliptic curves with periodic-periodic spin structure is presented by the complex analytic quotient stack
{\mathfrak{H}}/\!\!/{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
, where the action on
{\mathfrak{H}}
factors through the homomorphism
{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\to{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
. There is an evident functor
{\mathfrak{H}}/\!\!/{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\to{\mathfrak{H}}/\!\!/{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
; geometrically, this forgets the spin structure on the elliptic curve. The pullback of the line bundle (
A.3
) along this forgetful functor admits a square root denoted
{\rm Mf}_{k/2}
determined by the character (
A.3
). Taking underlying smooth stacks, there is a functor from Euclidean spin tori to elliptic curves given by the composition,

(474)

\displaystyle f\colon{\sf Lat}/\!\!/{\rm Spin}(2)\times{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\simeq{\mathfrak{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}/\!\!/{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\to{\mathfrak{H}}/\!\!/{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\to{\mathfrak{H}}/\!\!/{\rm SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})

where the first arrow is induced by projection. Then we obtain the following corollary to Lemma
A.4
.

Corollary A.5
.

The pullback of the line bundle determined by (
A.3
) along the composition (
474
) is isomorphic to the line determined by (
452
):

f^{*}{\rm Mf}_{k}\simeq{\rm Pf}^{\otimes 2k}.

In particular, weakly holomorphic modular forms of weight
k
pull back to sections of
{\rm Pf}^{\otimes 2k}
. More generally, smooth sections of
{\rm Mf}_{k/2}\otimes\overline{{\rm Mf}}_{l/2}
over
{\mathfrak{H}}/\!\!/{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
pull back to smooth sections of
{\rm Pf}^{\otimes k}\otimes\overline{{\rm Pf}}^{\otimes l}
.

A.4.
Geometry of the Dedekind
\eta
-function and 2nd Eisenstein series

Recall the Dedekind
\eta
-function,

(475)

\displaystyle\eta(q):=q^{1/24}\prod_{n>0}(1-q^{n}),\qquad q=e^{2\pi iz}.

The following lemma helps explain certain normalizations in the physics literature. Concretely, if a function on
{\mathfrak{H}}
transforms by a
U(1)
-phase under the
{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
-action, multiplying by a power of
\eta
gives a function that transforms as a modular form. Rephrasing in the geometry of stacks yields the following.

Lemma A.6
.

The Dedekind
\eta
-function determines an isomorphism between the line bundle over
{\mathfrak{H}}/\!\!/{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
determined by (
A.3
) and the line bundle determined by the character

(476)

\displaystyle\mu\colon{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\to{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})/[{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}),{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})]\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}/24\subset U(1)

given by embedding the abelianization of
{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
as 24th roots of unity.

Proof.

The transformation formula for the
\eta
-function under the action by
{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
is

\eta(\gamma z)=(\mu(\gamma)^{-1}\sqrt{(cz+d)})\eta(z),\qquad\gamma\in{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})

and hence
\eta
is a global section of the tensor product of the line bundle determined by (
A.3
) and the dual of the line bundle determined by (
476
). In view of (
475
),
\eta(z)
is a nonvanishing function and therefore determines a nonvanishing section of this tensor product of line bundles over
{\mathfrak{H}}/\!\!/{\rm MP}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
. This proves the lemma.
∎

There are two common forms of the 2nd Eisenstein series

\displaystyle E_{2}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n^{2}}+\sum_{m\neq 0}\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\frac{1}{(mz+n)^{2}},

(477)

\displaystyle E_{2}^{*}(z,\bar{z})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{2}(z)-\frac{\pi}{{\rm im}(z)}=E_{2}(z)-\frac{2\pi i}{z-\bar{z}}.

We recall
[
Zag08
, page 19]
that
E_{2}(z)
is holomorphic but not modular, whereas
E_{2}^{*}(z,\bar{z})
is not holomorphic but is weight 2,

E_{2}^{*}\left(\frac{az+b}{cz+d}\right)=(cz+d)^{2}E_{2}^{*}(z).

This can be rephrased as follows.

Lemma A.7
.

The nonholomorphic 2nd Eisenstein series
E_{2}^{*}
defines a smooth section of the
4
th tensor power of the line bundle determined by (
A.3
). This smooth section satisfies

(478)

\displaystyle\partial_{\bar{z}}E_{2}^{*}=-\frac{2\pi i}{(z-\bar{z})^{2}}.

Appendix B
Super background

B.1.
Real structures and super hermitian pairings

The category of
super vector spaces
is the category of
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded vector spaces over
{\mathbb{C}}
equipped with the graded tensor product. The adjective “super” refers to the sign in the braiding isomorphism for this tensor product

\sigma\colon V\otimes W\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}W\otimes V\qquad v\otimes w\mapsto(-1)^{|v||w|}w\otimes v\quad v\in V,\ w\in W

where
v
and
w
are homogeneous elements of degree
|v|,|w|\in{\mathbb{Z}}/2
.
Let
V^{\rm ev}\oplus V^{\rm odd}
denote the direct sum decomposition of
V
into its even and odd subspaces. The
grading involution
is the linear map
(-1)^{\sf F}\colon V\to V
that acts by
+1
on
V^{\rm ev}
and
-1
on
V^{\rm odd}
.

There is an involution on the category of super vector spaces that on objects reverses complex structures,
V\mapsto\overline{V}
, i.e.,
\overline{V}
is gotten from
V
by precomposing the action of
{\mathbb{C}}
with complex conjugation.

Definition B.1
.

A
real structure
on a super vector space
V
is an isomorphism
r\colon V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\overline{V}
of super vector spaces such that
\overline{r}\circ r={{\rm id}}_{V}
. The
real subspace
associated with a real structure is the fixed point set
V_{\mathbb{R}}\subset V
for
r
.

Definition B.2
.

A
hermitian form
on a super vector space
V
is the data of a map
\langle-,-\rangle\colon\overline{V}\otimes V\to{\mathbb{C}}
making the diagram commute,

(479)

\overline{V}\otimes V
{\mathbb{C}}
V\otimes\overline{V}
\overline{{\mathbb{C}}}
\langle-,-\rangle
\sigma
\overline{\langle-,-\rangle}
(\overline{\phantom{A}})

or equivalently, satisfying the formula
\langle x,y\rangle=(-1)^{|x||y|}\overline{\langle y,x\rangle}
for homogeneous elements. A hermitian form is
positive
if

(480)

\displaystyle\langle x,x\rangle>0,

\displaystyle x\neq 0\ {\rm even}\quad{\rm and}\quad i^{-1}\langle x,x\rangle>0,

\displaystyle x\neq 0\ {\rm odd.}

A
real
hermitian form on a super vector space with real structure is a hermitian form that when restricted to
(V_{\mathbb{R}})^{\rm ev}\subset V
takes values in
{\mathbb{R}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
and when restricted to
(V_{\mathbb{R}})^{\rm odd}\subset V
takes values in
i^{-1}{\mathbb{R}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
.

A positive hermitian form in the above sense determines a positive pairing
(-,-)
in the usual sense on the underlying (ungraded) vector space
V
by the formula

\displaystyle(x,y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&|x|\neq|y|\\
\langle x,y\rangle&x,y\ {\rm even}\\
i\langle x,y\rangle&x,y\ {\rm odd.}\end{array}\right.

When
V
is endowed with a real structure, this translation sends a real hermitian form to a real inner product on
V_{\mathbb{R}}
.

Definition B.3
.

Let
V
be a super vector space equipped with a positive hermitian form
\langle-,-\rangle
. The
super adjoint
of an operator
T\colon V\to V
is characterized by

(485)

\displaystyle\langle x,Ty\rangle=(-1)^{|x||A|}\langle T^{*}x,y\rangle.

In the translation to the ordinary pairing (
B.1
), we have

\displaystyle T^{*}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}T^{\dagger}&T\ {\rm even}\\
iT^{\dagger}&T\ {\rm odd}\end{array}\right.

where
(-)^{\dagger}
denotes the usual adjoint with respect to the positive pairing
(-,-)
.

B.2.
Super algebras and their modules

A
superalgebra
is an algebra object in the category of super (i.e.,
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded) vector spaces equipped with the
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded tensor product. Super algebras are the objects of a monoidal category, where the tensor product of superalgebras
A
and
B
is the super vector space
A\otimes B
with multiplication

(a\otimes b)\cdot(a^{\prime}\otimes b^{\prime})=(-1)^{|b||a^{\prime}|}aa^{\prime}\otimes bb^{\prime}.

The
opposite
of a superalgebra
A
is a superalgebra
A^{\sf{op}}
with the same underlying super vector space and multiplication

(489)

\displaystyle a\cdot_{{\sf{op}}}b:=(-1)^{|a||b|}b\cdot a

where
b\cdot a
is the multiplication in
A
.

Definition B.4
.

A
*
-superalgebra
is a superalgebra together with the data of a homomorphism
(-)^{*}\colon A\to\overline{A}{}^{\sf{op}}
that squares to the identity on
A
, where the opposite algebra is taken in the graded sense.

Given a superalgebra
A
, let
{}_{A}{\sf Mod}
and
{\sf Mod}_{A}
denote the categories of left and right
A
-modules, respectively, whose objects are super vector spaces with a (graded)
A
-action. These categories have symmetric monoidal structures from direct sum of
A
-modules. Similarly, let
{}_{A}{\sf Mod}_{B}
denote the category of
A-B
-bimodules. There are canonical equivalences of categories

(490)

\displaystyle{\sf Mod}_{A^{\sf{op}}\otimes B^{\sf{op}}}\simeq{}_{A}{\sf Mod}_{B^{{\sf{op}}}}\simeq{}_{A\otimes B}{\sf Mod}

where a left
A\otimes B
-bimodule
W
is sent to the
A-B^{\sf{op}}
-bimodule with the same underlying super vector space and action

a\cdot w\cdot b:=(-1)^{|w||b|}(a\otimes b)\cdot w

with similar formulas defining a right
A^{\sf{op}}\otimes B^{\sf{op}}
-module.
We often use notation like
{}_{A}W
for an object of
{}_{A}{\sf Mod}
, and
{}_{A}W_{B}
for an object of
{}_{A}{\sf Mod}_{B}
.

The
supertrace
of an endomorphism
T\colon V\to V
is

(491)

\displaystyle{\sf sTr}(T):={\sf Tr}((-1)^{\sf F}\circ T)

where
{\sf Tr}
is the ordinary trace.
The
super commutator
of elements in a superalgebra is

(492)

\displaystyle[a,b]=ab-(-1)^{|a||b|}ba\qquad a,b\in A.

A
supertrace
on a superalgebra is a map of super vector spaces

(493)

\displaystyle{\sf sTr}_{A}\colon A\to{\mathbb{C}},\qquad{\sf sTr}_{A}([a,b])=0

that vanishes on super commutators, and hence is determined by a linear map
A/[A,A]\to{\mathbb{C}}
.

A supertrace on a superalgebra
A
induces a supertrace on the category of (finite-dimensional)
A
-modules. For right
A
-modules, this supertrace comes from the composition

(494)

\displaystyle{\rm End}_{A}(W)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}W\otimes_{A}W^{\vee}\to A/[A,A]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\sf sTr}_{A}}}{{\to}}{\mathbb{C}}

where
W^{\vee}:={\rm Hom}_{A}(W,A)
is the dual (left)
A
-module, and the map to
A/[A,A]
is the Hattori–Stallings trace induced by the evaluation pairing,

{\rm eval}\colon W\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}W^{\vee}\to A.

One obtains traces for bimodules (and left modules) from traces on
A\otimes B^{\sf{op}}
using (
490
).

B.3.
(Real) supermanifolds

Stolz and Teichner’s supersymmetric field theories are couched in the language of supermanifolds whose structure sheaves are defined over
{\mathbb{C}}
; this is called the category of
cs
-supermanifolds in
[
DM99
]
. The majority of what we require is covered in the concise introduction
[
ST11
, §4.1]
, but we establish a little notation below.

By definition, any supermanifold is locally isomorphic to the supermanifold
{\mathbb{R}}^{n|m}
characterized by the global sections of its structure sheaf,
C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n|m})\simeq C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n};{\mathbb{C}})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\Lambda^{\bullet}{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
. We emphasize that this is a sheaf of super (i.e.,
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded) algebras over
{\mathbb{C}}
.

Let
\Pi
denote the functor

(495)

\displaystyle\Pi\colon{\sf Vect}_{/{\sf Mfld}}\to{\sf SMfld},\qquad(E\to N)\mapsto\Pi E

that sends a complex vector bundle
E\to N
over an ordinary manifold
N
to the supermanifold
\Pi E
whose structure sheaf is the sheaf of sections of
\Lambda^{\bullet}E^{\vee}
, viewed as a sheaf of
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded algebras. For a supermanifold
S
, let
S_{\rm red}
denote its reduced manifold; this is an ordinary manifold obtained (as locally ringed space) by the quotient of the structure sheaf of
S
by its nilpotent ideal. Every supermanifold can be written as
S\simeq\Pi E
, where
E\to S_{\rm red}
is a complex vector bundle
[
Bat79
]
. In particular, the supermanifolds
{\mathbb{R}}^{n|m}
arise as the trivial rank
m
-bundle over
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, i.e.,
E={\mathbb{C}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}=({\mathbb{R}}^{n|m})_{\rm red}
.

One frequently performs computations in supermanifolds using the
functor of points
. This formalism views a supermanifold as a representable presheaf.

Example B.5
.

The representable presheaf associated with
{\mathbb{R}}^{n|m}
assigns to a supermanifold
S
the set

(496)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}^{n|m}(S):=\{t_{1},t_{2},\dots,t_{n}\in C^{\infty}(S)^{{\rm ev}},\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\dots,\theta_{m}\in C^{\infty}(S)^{\rm odd}\mid(t_{i})_{\rm red}=\overline{(t_{i})}_{\rm red}\}

where
(t_{i})_{\rm red}
denotes the restriction of a function to the reduced manifold
S_{\rm red}\hookrightarrow S
, and
\overline{(t_{i})}_{\rm red}
is the conjugate of the complex-valued function
(t_{i})_{\rm red}
on the smooth manifold
S_{\rm red}
. We use this functor of points description throughout the paper, typically with roman letters denoting even functions and greek letters denoting odd functions.

Example B.6
.

We record two equivalent descriptions of the functor of points of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}

(497)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}(S)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\{x,y\in C^{\infty}(S)^{\rm ev},\ \theta\in C^{\infty}(S)^{\rm odd}\mid(x)_{\rm red}=\overline{(x)}_{\rm red},(y)_{\rm red}=\overline{(y)}_{\rm red}\}

(498)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\{z,w\in C^{\infty}(S)^{{\rm ev}},\theta\in C^{\infty}(S)^{\rm odd}\mid(z)_{\rm red}=\overline{(w)}_{\rm red}\}.

The isomorphism between (
497
) and (
498
) is
(x,y)\mapsto(x+iy,x-iy)=(z,w)
. Below we adopt the standard (though potentially misleading) notation
\overline{z}:=w
.

Definition B.7
.

A
super Lie group
is a group object in supermanifolds.

By definition, a super Lie group structure on a supermanifold
G
comes from endowing the sets
G(S)
with the structure of a group natural in
S
. Most of the super Lie groups in this paper will be defined in this manner. We also note that every ordinary Lie group determines a super Lie group.

Example B.8
.

Consider the super Lie group determined by the ordinary Lie group
U(1)\subset{\mathbb{C}}
, the unit complex numbers. We use the functor of points description

(499)

\displaystyle U(1)(S)=\{u,\bar{u}\in C^{\infty}(S)^{{\rm ev}}\mid(u)_{\rm red}=\overline{(\bar{u})}_{\rm red},\ u\bar{u}=1\},

analogous to the notation for the
S
-points of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2|1}
introduced after (
498
).

Definition B.9
.

An
open cover
of a supermanifold
S
is a collection
\{\varphi\colon U_{i}\to S\}
where
\varphi_{i}
is a local isomorphism for each
i
and
\{\varphi_{\rm red}\colon(U_{i})_{\rm red}\to S_{\rm red}\}
is an ordinary open cover of the manifold
S_{\rm red}
.

Definition B.10
.

There is an involution on the category of supermanifolds,

(500)

\displaystyle(\overline{\phantom{-}})\colon{\sf SMfld}\to{\sf SMfld},\qquad S\mapsto\overline{S}

sending a supermanifold to the same locally ringed space but whose sheaf has complex numbers acting through complex conjugation. A
real structure
on a supermanifold
S
is an isomorphism
r_{S}\colon S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\bar{S}
such that
\overline{r}\circ r={{\rm id}}_{S}
. A map
f\colon S^{\prime}\to S
between supermanifolds with real structure is
real
if
r_{S}\circ f=f\circ r_{S^{\prime}}
.

Since (
500
) is a functor, a super Lie group
G
has a conjugate super Lie group
\overline{G}
(which typically is not isomorphic to
G
). A
real structure
on a super Lie group is a real structure on its underlying supermanifold such that all the structure maps are real.

Example B.11
.

A real structure on a supermanifold presented as
S=\Pi E
amounts to a choice of real structure on the complex vector bundle
E\to S_{\rm red}
. In particular, real structures need not exist, and when they do exist they need not be unique. However, ordinary manifolds regarded as supermanifolds have a canonical real structure where the map
M\to\overline{M}
comes from complex conjugation of
{\mathbb{C}}
-valued functions on
M
. Similarly, ordinary Lie groups regarded as super Lie groups have canonical real structures.

Example B.12
.

For a complex vector bundle
E\to N
, a real structure on
E
(as a vector bundle) determines a real structure on
\Pi E
(as a supermanifold) via complex conjugation

C^{\infty}(\Pi E)\simeq\Gamma(N;\Lambda^{\bullet}E^{\vee})\simeq\Gamma(N;(\Lambda E_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee})\otimes{\mathbb{C}})\qquad(E_{\mathbb{R}})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\simeq E.

Indeed, complex conjugation is a
{\mathbb{C}}
-antilinear map
C^{\infty}(\Pi E)\to C^{\infty}(\Pi E)
, determining an isomorphism of supermanifolds
\Pi E\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\overline{\Pi E}
.

Finally, we require a technical lemma. Given a vector bundle
V\to M
, we obtain a vector bundle
p^{*}V\to{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)
by pulling back along the projection
p\colon{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)\simeq\Pi TM\to M
.

Lemma B.13
.

For any vector bundle
\mathcal{V}\to{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)
, there is an isomorphism
\mathcal{V}\simeq p^{*}V
where
V\to M
is a vector bundle on
M
.

Proof.

Let
i\colon M\hookrightarrow{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)
denote the inclusion of the reduced manifold. Given a vector bundle
\mathcal{V}\to{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)
, our candidate for
V\to M
in the statement of the lemma is
V:=i^{*}\mathcal{V}
. Indeed, the composition
i\circ p
is homotopic to the identity on
{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)
. For example, consider the homotopy
{\mathbb{R}}\times\Pi TM_{\mathbb{C}}\to\Pi TM_{\mathbb{C}}
that rescales the odd fibers of
\Pi TM_{\mathbb{C}}\to M
by a scalar
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
; in the functor of points this map is
(t,x,\psi)\mapsto(x,t\psi)
for
t\in{\mathbb{R}}(S)
and
(x,\psi)\in\Pi TM_{\mathbb{C}}(S)\simeq{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)(S)
. Now we use that pullbacks along homotopic maps yield isomorphic vector bundles,
concluding that
p^{*}V=p^{*}i^{*}\mathcal{V}\simeq(i\circ p)^{*}\mathcal{V}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{V}
.
∎

Appendix C
Stolz and Teichner’s twisted geometric field theories

We refer to the companion paper in this series
[
BE23b
, Appendix B]
for a more detailed overview of the geometric bordism categories constructed in
[
ST11
]
. Below we collect the main definitions and notation used in this paper, as well as the definition of a reflection structure for a twisted field theory (see §
C.3
). The presentation below emphasizes that twisted field theories fit into the larger context of twisted representations of internal categories; this larger context includes previously studied examples in Lie groupoid geometry and twisted equivariant K-theory.

C.1.
Categories internal to stacks

Definition C.1

(
[
ST11
, Definition 2.13]
)
.

A
category
{\sf C}
internal to stacks
is the data of stacks
{\rm Ob}({\sf C})
and
{\rm Mor}({\sf C})
with source, target, unit, and composition functors

(501)

\displaystyle{\sf s},{\sf t}\colon{\rm Mor}({\sf C})\to{\rm Ob}({\sf C})\quad{\sf u}\colon{\rm Ob}({\sf C})\to{\rm Mor}({\sf C}),\quad{\sf c}\colon{\rm Mor}({\sf C})\times_{{\rm Ob}({\sf C})}{\rm Mor}({\sf C})\to{\rm Mor}({\sf C})

along with additional coherence data (e.g., an associator and unitors) satisfying compatibility properties.
A category internal to symmetric monoidal stacks
and
a category internal to symmetric monoidal stacks with involution
are defined analogously.

Stacks form a
(2,1)
-category, and correspondingly we will refer to maps between stacks as 1-morphisms and isomorphisms between these 1-morphisms as 2-morphisms. The definitions below apply to categories internal to stacks, categories internal to symmetric monoidal stacks, or categories internal to symmetric monoidal stacks with involution. We will simply refer to an internal category when all three of these options are in play.

Remark C.2
.

For the categorical foundations in
[
ST11
]
(building on
[
MF06
]
), the composition functor uses the strict fibered product of stacks. This creates problems when attempting to define the correct notion of equivalence of internal categories, as the composition functor does not play well with equivalence of stacks. However, there is an easy fix: if the source and target functors
{\sf s}
and
{\sf t}
are fibrations of stacks, this strict fibered product coincides with the usual (homotopy) fibered product of stacks. Geometric bordism categories have this fibration property; see Remark
C.29
.

Definition C.3

(
[
ST11
, Definition 2.18]
)
.

For internal categories
{\sf C}
,
{\sf D}
, an
internal functor

F=(F_{0},F_{1},\mu,\varepsilon)\colon{\sf C}\to{\sf D}
consists of 1-morphisms
F_{0}\colon{\rm Ob}({\sf C})\to{\rm Ob}({\sf D})
,
F_{1}\colon{\rm Mor}({\sf C})\to{\rm Mor}({\sf D})
, and 2-morphisms
\mu
and
\epsilon
,

(502)

{\rm Mor}({\sf C})\times_{{\rm Ob}({\sf C})}{\rm Mor}({\sf C})
{\rm Mor}({\sf C})
{\rm Mor}({\sf D})\times_{{\rm Ob}({\sf D})}{\rm Mor}({\sf D})
{\rm Mor}({\sf D})
\mu\ \rotatebox[origin]{30.0}{$\Rightarrow$}
{\sf c}
F_{1}\times F_{1}
{\sf c}
F_{1}

{\rm Ob}({\sf C})
{\rm Ob}({\sf D})
{\rm Mor}({\sf C})
{\rm Mor}({\sf D}).
\varepsilon\ \rotatebox[origin]{30.0}{$\Rightarrow$}
F_{0}
{\sf u}
F_{1}
{\sf u}

These data are required to satisfy further properties involving the associator and unitors.

Definition C.4

(
[
ST11
, Definition 2.19]
)
.

An
internal natural transformation

(\eta,\rho)\colon F\Rightarrow G
between internal functors
F,G\colon{\sf C}\to{\sf D}
is a 1-morphism
\eta\colon{\rm Ob}({\sf C})\to{\rm Mor}({\sf D})
and 2-morphism
\rho

(503)

{\rm Mor}({\sf C})
{\rm Mor}({\sf D})\times_{{\rm Ob}({\sf D})}{\rm Mor}({\sf D})
{\rm Mor}({\sf D})\times_{{\rm Ob}({\sf D})}{\rm Mor}({\sf D})
{\rm Mor}({\sf D}).
\rho\ \rotatebox[origin]{30.0}{$\Rightarrow$}
\eta\circ{\sf t}\times F_{1}
G_{1}\times\eta\circ{\sf s}
{\sf c}
{\sf c}

These data are required to satisfy additional coherence properties. Define an
isomorphism between internal natural transformations

(\eta,\rho)\Rightarrow(\eta^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
as a 2-morphism
\eta\Rightarrow\eta^{\prime}
along which
\rho^{\prime}
pulls back to
\rho
.

Example C.5
.

A
super Lie category
is a category internal to supermanifolds whose source and target maps are submersions. This is a version of Definition
C.1
in which
{\rm Ob}({\sf C})
and
{\rm Mor}({\sf C})
are supermanifolds, i.e., representable stacks. There can be no higher coherence data in a super Lie category, so the associator and unitor are both necessarily trivial. A
smooth functor

F\colon{\sf C}\to{\sf D}
between super Lie categories is a pair of maps of supermanifolds
F_{0}\colon{\rm Ob}({\sf C})\to{\rm Ob}({\sf D})
,
F_{1}\colon{\rm Mor}({\sf C})\to{\rm Mor}({\sf D})
satisfying the axioms for a functor. A
smooth natural transformation

\eta\colon F\Rightarrow G
is a map of supermanifolds
\eta\colon{\rm Ob}({\sf C})\to{\rm Mor}({\sf D})
satisfying the axioms for a natural transformation between functors. By the Yoneda lemma for stacks, a smooth functor determines an internal functor, and a smooth natural transformation determines an internal natural transformation.

Example C.6
.

As a special case of the previous example, every (super) Lie groupoid determines an internal category. In particular, every super Lie group
G
determines the super Lie groupoid
*/\!\!/G
, which in turn provides a category internal to stacks. For the (discrete) Lie groupoid determined by an ordinary manifold
M
, a graded gerbe on
M
determines an invertible algebra bundle (possibly after replacing
M
with an equivalent Lie groupoid, see
[
Fre12
, Example 1.74]
). Hence, gerbes provide examples of twists for the internal category associated to
M
. We refer to
[
Sto19
]
for a related connection between
1|1
-dimensional field theories and twisted K-theory.

C.2.
Twisted representations of internal categories

Definition C.7

(Sketch of
[
ST11
, Definition 5.1]
)
.

Define the
internal Morita category

{\sf{TA}}
as the following category internal to symmetric monoidal stacks with involution. The stack
{\rm Ob}({\sf{TA}})
classifies locally trivial bundles of nuclear Fréchet superalgebras and isomorphisms of algebra bundles. The stack
{\rm Mor}({\sf{TA}})
classifies locally trivial bundles of nuclear Fréchet bimodules over topological algebras. Composition in
{\sf{TA}}
is the projective tensor product of bimodule bundles,

{\sf c}\colon{\rm Mor}({\sf{TA}})\times_{{\rm Ob}({\sf{TA}})}{\rm Mor}({\sf{TA}})\to{\rm Mor}({\sf{TA}}),\qquad{\sf c}(B,B^{\prime})=B\otimes_{A}B^{\prime}.

Viewing an algebra bundle as a bimodule over itself constructs the unit map
{\sf u}\colon{\rm Ob}({\sf{TA}})\to{\rm Mor}({\sf{TA}})
. Endow
{\rm Ob}({\sf{TA}})
and
{\rm Mor}({\sf{TA}})
with monoidal structures from the projective tensor product of algebra and bimodule bundles over the structure sheaf of the base supermanifold. Involutions on the stacks
{\rm Ob}({\sf{TA}})
and
{\rm Mor}({\sf{TA}})
come from the parity involution on bundles, and all structure maps are
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-equivariant for these involutions.

Remark C.8
.

Definition
C.7
deviates from the one in
[
ST11
]
in two ways. First, we require sheaves of topological vector spaces to be locally free, i.e., vector bundles. Stolz and Teichner’s motivation to work with more general sheaves is explained in Remark
1.4
. Second, we specialize from general locally convex topological vector spaces to nuclear Fréchet spaces. We make this choice in order to guarantee that certain formal categorical structures in the functorial definition of quantum field theory agree with standard structures in physics: Fréchet spaces have the
approximation property

[
Sch71
, page 109]
, leading to a well-behaved theory for traces of nuclear operators, see
[
ST12
, §4.5]
. It also happens that all the desired examples in this paper result in vector bundles of nuclear Fréchet spaces. We refer to
[
Cos11b
, Appendix 2]
for a succinct overview of nuclear Fréchet spaces.

Remark C.9
.

One can forget an involution or forget a monoidal structure on a stack. Hence Definition
C.7
produces three internal categories: a category internal to symmetric monoidal stacks with involution, a category internal to symmetric monoidal stacks, and a category internal to stacks. We will use the notation
{\sf{TA}}
to denote any one of these three options, where the particular choice should be clear from context.

Definition C.10
.

A
twist
for an internal category
{\sf C}
is an internal functor
\mathscr{T}\colon{\sf C}\to{\sf{TA}}
. The
trivial twist
is the functor

(504)

\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}\colon{\sf C}\to{\sf{TA}}

that is constant to the monoidal unit of
{\sf{TA}}
, e.g., all objects
Y\in{\rm Ob}({\sf C})(S)
are sent to the trivial algebra bundle on
S
, all objects
\Sigma\in{\rm Mor}({\sf C})(S)
are sent to the trivial bimodule bundle over
S
, and all the morphisms in
{\rm Ob}({\sf C})(S)
and
{\rm Mor}({\sf C})(S)
to the trivial automorphism of the trivial bundle.

Definition C.11
.

A
\mathscr{T}
-twisted representation
of an internal category
{\sf C}
is an internal natural transformation
E

(505)

{\sf C}
{\sf{TA}}.
E\Downarrow
\mathbbold{1}
\mathscr{T}

Isomorphisms between twists are internal natural isomorphisms
\mathscr{T}\Rightarrow\mathscr{T}^{\prime}
. Isomorphisms between twisted representations are isomorphisms between internal natural transformations
E\simeq E^{\prime}
.

Example C.12
.

The standard definition of a representation of a Lie groupoid
[
Mac87
, Definition 1.7.1]
determines a twisted representation of the associated internal category (see Example
C.6
) for the trivial twist
\mathscr{T}=\mathbbold{1}
.

Example C.13
.

When
{\sf C}
is a Lie groupoid, Freed’s invertible algebra bundles over
{\sf C}

[
Fre12
, Definition 1.59]
determine twists for the associated internal category. Invertible algebra bundles determine twists for equivariant K-theory
[
Fre12
, Definition 1.78]
, see also Karoubi
[
Kar68
]
and Donovan–Karoubi
[
DK70
]
.

Example C.14
.

For an ordinary manifold
M
regarded as an internal category (see Example
C.6
), a twist is a bundle of algebras
A
over
M
. A twisted representation is a bundle of
A
-modules
E\to M
.

Example C.15
.

For a super Lie group
G
regarded as an internal category (see Example
C.6
), a twist is an algebra
A
together with a bundle of
A-{\mathbb{C}}
bimodules over
G
with a map of bundles covering multiplication of
G
. When
A={\mathbb{C}}
this is the same data as a
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
-central extension of
G
. A twisted representation is an
A
-module
E
with (weak)
G
-action; when
A={\mathbb{C}}
this is the same data as a projective representation of
G
.

C.3.
Reflection structures for twisted representations

The following notions of internal involution and equivariance data expand on
[
HST10
, Definition 6.47]
. In the following definition we emphasize that
\mathscr{S}
is just a usual category, not an internal category.

Definition C.16

(
[
FH22
, Definition B.1]
)
.

For a category
{\mathscr{S}}
, an
involution
is a functor
(\overline{\phantom{A}})\colon{\mathscr{S}}\to{\mathscr{S}}
and a natural transformation
\varepsilon\colon{{\rm id}}_{\mathscr{S}}\Rightarrow(\overline{\phantom{A}})\circ(\overline{\phantom{A}})
satisfying a compatibility condition for the triple composition of
(\overline{\phantom{A}})
with itself.

Example C.17
.

Below we will consider two involutions on the category of supermanifolds. The first is the trivial involution, and the second is the
conjugation functor

(\overline{\phantom{A}})
from Definition
B.10
that reverses the complex structure on the structure sheaf of a supermanifold. In both of these cases, the involution squares to a functor that is equal to the identity, and so we take
\varepsilon
to be the identity.

Below we view (symmetric monoidal) stacks (with involution) as a category fibered over
{\sf SMfld}
, and so one can ask for a map of stacks covering an involution of
{\sf SMfld}
.

Definition C.18
.

Fix an involution
{\sf SMfld}\to{\sf SMfld}
on the category of supermanifolds. For an internal category
{\sf C}
, an
internal involution
is the data of an internal functor
\tau\colon{\sf C}\to{\sf C}
covering the fixed involution of supermanifolds, an internal natural isomorphism
\eta\colon{{\rm id}}_{\sf C}\Rightarrow\tau\circ\tau
covering the isomorphism data for the involution on
{\sf SMfld}
, and an isomorphism
\delta
between internal natural isomorphisms
\tau\simeq\tau\circ{{\rm id}}_{\sf C}\Rightarrow{{\rm id}}_{\sf C}\circ\tau\simeq\tau
in the diagram

{\sf C}
{\sf C}
{\sf C}
{\sf C}.
\eta\ \Downarrow
\eta\ \Uparrow
\tau
{{\rm id}}_{\sf C}
{{\rm id}}_{\sf C}
\tau
\tau

The isomorphism
\delta
satisfies a compatibility condition with
\eta
for the quadruple composition of
\tau
with itself.

Definition C.19
.

A
reflection
for an internal category
{\sf C}
is an internal involution relative to the conjugation functor (
500
) on
{\sf SMfld}
.

Lemma C.20
.

The internal category
{\sf{TA}}
admits a reflection from complex conjugation of algebras, bimodules and bimodule maps,

(506)

\displaystyle(\overline{\phantom{-}})\colon{\sf{TA}}\to{\sf{TA}},\ \ (A\to S)\mapsto(\overline{A}\to\overline{S}),

where
(\overline{\phantom{-}})\circ(\overline{\phantom{-}})={{\rm id}}
.

The following generalizes
[

to internal categories.

.

Let

Remark C.22
.

In the examples of interest in this paper, the coherence data

and

in Defintion
C.18
will be trivial. In such cases, the data in (

) becomes an isomorphism between
